Rustacean X Bitcoiner Journey

Introduction

This book is a reflection of my journey as both a Rustacean and a Bitcoiner. Rust, with its elegance and rigor, teaches me how to build systems that are safe, efficient, and resilient. Bitcoin, with its principles of decentralization and sovereignty, inspires me to think about freedom, trust, and human empowerment.

This journey is not only about learning the theories, but also about doing through practice, experimenting with ideas, and applying the lessons from both Rust and Bitcoin. By combining these passions, I aim to create tools, share insights, and build systems that reflect the values of resilience, sovereignty, and human dignity.

What This Book Covers

This book documents my hands-on experience building and learning, with a forward-looking focus on Rust and Bitcoin:

  • Bitcoin Node + Start9 (foundation): Full journey from hardware and networking to running a sovereign Bitcoin node on Start9 OS.
  • Rust-first lens on infrastructure: Ongoing deep-dives into Rust-driven systems (e.g., Proxmox VE, Start9 OS) to learn Rust through real products and codebases.
  • Living lab for future projects: A place to iterate on new Rust/Bitcoin experiments, integrations, and community work as they emerge (nodes, services, tooling, and beyond).
  • Technical documentation: Practical, battle-tested guides that double as personal notes and public references.

How to Use This Book

Each section is designed to be both a narrative of learning and a practical reference guide:

  • Read sequentially to follow the complete journey from decision-making to implementation
  • Jump to specific guides when you need detailed instructions for particular tasks
  • Learn from the process including the troubleshooting, adjustments, and real-world details often missing from official documentation

The Journey Ahead

My hope is that this exploration will serve as a foundation for building things that matter — ideas, communities, and technologies that contribute to a better world for humanity. Whether you’re setting up your first Bitcoin node, learning Rust, or exploring the intersection of technology and sovereignty, this book is an invitation to learn by doing.

📖 Experience Log Notice: This is a living document that grows as I learn. Each section reflects real experience, documented as it happens.

👉 Next: Bitcoin Node

Bitcoin Node

Running a Bitcoin node is a cornerstone of participating in the Bitcoin network. It enforces consensus rules, strengthens decentralization, and gives individuals sovereignty over their transactions. This section covers the complete journey: choosing your node software and hardware, setting up equipment securely, and installing and configuring Bitcoin services for sovereignty.

Planning and Selection

  • Choosing which bitcoin node software Exploring options like Bitcoin Core, Bitcoin Knots, Start9, or Umbrel, and how each reflects different values.

  • Choosing bitcoin node equipment Documenting the hardware and accessories selected to support reliable node operation.

Hardware Setup

Setting up Hardware with AT&T BGW320 (Fiber 1 GIG) Complete workflow for setting up the Minisforum Mini PC with Start9 OS. Estimated Total Time:

  • Direct Start9 install: ~1 hour 40 minutes
  • Proxmox VM route: ~3 hours

🗂️ Hardware Setup Steps: The following step sequence and time estimates are based on my own hardware setup experience. Your process may vary depending on hardware, network, and prior experience.

  1. Flashing Start9 OS to USB drive (~15 min)
  2. Inspecting Minisforum Hardware (~20 min)
  3. Connecting Mini PC to Router (~10 min)
  4. Booting and Installing Start9 OS (~30 min direct install)
    • Alternative: Virtualized Start9 OS Setup (Proxmox VE) (~90 min)
  5. Configuring Network Settings (Start9 OS) (~10 min)
  6. Securing Proxmox VE (if using virtualization) (~20 min)
  7. Securing AT&T BGW320 Router (~15 min)

Software Setup

Setting up Bitcoin Node and Self-Sovereignty Software Installing and configuring Bitcoin Core and other self-sovereignty applications via Start9 web interface. Estimated Total Time:

  • Essential stack: ~30 minutes setup + sync time (1-7 days IBD)
  • Payment stack: ~30 minutes setup
  • Privacy & communication stack: ~15-30 minutes

🗂️ Software Setup Steps: The following step sequence and time estimates are based on my own software setup experience. Your process may vary depending on hardware, network, and prior experience.

  1. Securing Start9 OS and VM Environment (~15-20 min)
  2. Installing Bitcoin Core (~5-10 min setup, 1-7 days IBD (sync))
  3. Installing Mempool Explorer (~5-10 min)
  4. Installing Electrum Server (~10-15 min setup, 4-8 hours indexing)
  5. Installing Nostr Relay (~5-10 min)
  6. Installing Privacy Tools (~5-15 min per service)
  7. Installing Lightning Network Node (~10-15 min)
  8. Installing BTCPay Server (~15-20 min)

👉 Next: Choosing which bitcoin node software

Choosing which bitcoin node software

Running a Bitcoin node is both a technical decision and a statement of sovereignty. The choice of software shapes how you interact with the network and what values you emphasize.

There is flexibility: you can run Bitcoin Core, the reference implementation trusted by the community, or Bitcoin Knots, a fork that includes additional features and policy tweaks. Beyond these, platforms like Start9 and Umbrel make it possible to experiment with different node software in user-friendly, self-sovereign environments.

Exploring these options is more than just installation — it is about learning, doing, and experimenting with the tools that secure Bitcoin. Each step deepens understanding of how consensus is enforced and how freedom is preserved.

Start9 itself is written in Rust, which creates a unique opportunity: to grow in both worlds at once — mastering Rust as a systems language while living the values of Bitcoin through node operation. By applying lessons from both, the goal is to build tools, ideas, and communities that contribute to a better world for humanity.

Why Start9 and Rust Together?

Start9 represents more than just another way to run a Bitcoin node. It embodies the principle of self‑sovereign computing, where individuals control their own infrastructure rather than relying on centralized services. Because it is built in Rust, it also reflects the values of safety, performance, and reliability that Rust brings to modern software development.

For someone passionate about both Bitcoin and Rust, Start9 becomes a bridge:

  • Bitcoin provides the philosophy of decentralization and freedom.
  • Rust provides the technical foundation for building secure, efficient systems.
  • Start9 unites them, offering a platform where experimenting with nodes also means sharpening Rust skills.

This combination is powerful because it allows me to not only run Bitcoin software but also understand and contribute to the underlying codebase. It’s a chance to practice sovereignty in the digital world while developing mastery in a language designed for resilience.

By embracing Start9 and Rust together, I can align my technical growth with my values — building systems that are both robust and human‑centered.

Comparing Node Software Options

To help clarify the differences between node software options, here’s a comparison:

Key Distinctions

Bitcoin Core is the reference implementation:

  • Most widely used and tested
  • Conservative approach to changes
  • Trusted by the majority of the network
  • Direct connection to Bitcoin’s development

Bitcoin Knots offers additional options:

  • Started as a fork of Bitcoin Core
  • Includes features not in Core (e.g., full RBF by default)
  • More aggressive policy defaults
  • For users who want more control over node policy

💸 RBF Policy Notice: RBF allows replacing an unconfirmed transaction with a higher-fee version to speed up confirmation. Bitcoin Knots enables Full RBF by default (replaces any transaction), while Bitcoin Core requires opt-in. This gives Knots users more control if their transaction gets stuck due to low fees.

Start9 OS is a platform, not just node software:

  • Provides an operating system for running Bitcoin (Core or Knots) and other services
  • Focus on ease-of-use and self-sovereignty
  • Written in Rust for security and performance
  • Includes Lightning Network, BTCPay Server, and other applications
  • Designed for users who want a complete sovereignty stack

Umbrel is a user-friendly personal server platform:

  • Popular choice for beginners due to beautiful interface
  • Large app ecosystem (Bitcoin, Lightning, media servers, etc.)
  • Written in TypeScript/Node.js
  • Can run on Raspberry Pi or dedicated hardware
  • Focus on great user experience and broad functionality
  • Optional cloud features for remote access

Decision Framework

Choose based on your goals and technical comfort level:

Choose Bitcoin Core if you want

  • ✅ The most tested and trusted implementation
  • ✅ To run exactly what the majority of the network runs
  • ✅ Direct participation in Bitcoin’s reference software
  • ✅ Maximum compatibility and support
  • ⚡️ Caution: Requires Linux/command-line knowledge

Choose Bitcoin Knots if you want

  • ✅ Additional features and policy options
  • ✅ More control over transaction relay policies
  • ✅ Enhanced privacy features by default
  • ✅ To support alternative implementation diversity
  • ⚡️ Caution: Requires advanced technical knowledge

Choose Start9 OS if you want

  • ✅ User-friendly GUI for all configurations
  • ✅ Complete sovereignty stack (node + Lightning + apps)
  • ✅ Automatic updates and easy maintenance
  • ✅ To learn Rust while running Bitcoin software
  • ✅ Built-in Tor and privacy features
  • ✅ Ability to run Bitcoin Core OR Knots
  • ✅ Fully local, no cloud dependencies
  • ⚡️ Caution: Requires dedicated hardware (Mini PC)

Choose Umbrel if you want

  • ✅ Beautiful, intuitive user interface
  • ✅ Large app ecosystem (50+ apps)
  • ✅ Flexibility to run on Raspberry Pi
  • ✅ Great for beginners
  • ✅ Active community and frequent updates
  • ✅ Optional remote access via cloud
  • ⚡️ Caution: Cloud features may compromise full sovereignty
  • ⚡️ Caution: Written in JavaScript (less system-level control)

My Choice: Start9 OS

For this journey, I chose Start9 OS over Umbrel and direct node installations for several reasons:

  • Alignment with Learning Goals: Start9 is written in Rust, allowing me to study and contribute to a codebase that aligns with my Rust learning journey. Umbrel’s TypeScript/Node.js stack, while accessible, doesn’t support my systems programming goals.

  • True Sovereignty: Start9 is fully local with no cloud dependencies. While Umbrel offers optional cloud features for convenience, this creates potential privacy and sovereignty tradeoffs that conflict with Bitcoin’s core principles.

  • Sovereignty Stack: Beyond just running a Bitcoin node, Start9 provides a complete platform for self-sovereign computing, including Lightning Network, BTCPay Server, and other services.

  • User Experience: The GUI-based approach allows me to focus on understanding Bitcoin concepts without getting bogged down in Linux system administration (though SSH access is available when needed). Umbrel also offers this, but Start9’s privacy-first design aligns better with my values.

  • Best of Both Worlds: Start9 can run either Bitcoin Core or Bitcoin Knots, so I’m not locked into one implementation. Umbrel only runs Bitcoin Core.

  • Privacy by Default: Built-in Tor support and privacy-focused design align with Bitcoin’s sovereignty principles. While Umbrel has Tor available, it’s not as deeply integrated.

  • System-Level Security: Rust’s memory safety guarantees provide better protection against certain classes of vulnerabilities compared to JavaScript/TypeScript.

  • Active Development: The Start9 team is actively developing and improving the platform, with a strong focus on user sovereignty.

Why Not Umbrel?

Umbrel is excellent for beginners and has a beautiful interface with a large app ecosystem. However:

  • Cloud dependencies in some features compromise full sovereignty
  • JavaScript/TypeScript doesn’t align with my systems programming learning goals
  • Less privacy-focused by default (Tor is optional, not built-in)
  • Single implementation (Bitcoin Core only, no Knots option)

This choice doesn’t diminish Umbrel’s value—it’s a great option for users prioritizing ease-of-use and a broad app ecosystem. For my goals of combining Bitcoin sovereignty with Rust development skills while maintaining strong privacy guarantees, Start9 OS is the better fit.

Choosing which bitcoin node equipment

Running a Bitcoin node is both a technical decision and a statement of sovereignty. The choice of hardware shapes your experience and what values you emphasize.

There is flexibility: you can use a mini PC (like the MINISFORUM AI X1-255 or Beelink SER9), a Raspberry Pi or similar DIY single-board computer, or a prebuilt Start9 Server. Each option offers different tradeoffs in cost, performance, expandability, and learning opportunity.

Exploring these options is about learning, doing, and experimenting with the tools that secure Bitcoin. Each step deepens understanding of how sovereignty is enforced and how freedom is preserved.

Why this equipment?

Choosing the right hardware for a self-sovereign Bitcoin node is about more than just specs:

  • Reliability: Stable hardware means less downtime and fewer headaches.
  • Performance: Sufficient CPU, RAM, and storage allow you to run multiple apps and future-proof your setup.
  • Learning: DIY hardware builds deepen your understanding and control.
  • Sovereignty: Owning and configuring your own device is a core value of the Bitcoin ethos.

Key distinctions

Raspberry Pi 5 (8GB):

  • ✅ Very affordable entry point
  • ✅ Low power, compact, huge community
  • ✅ Great for learning and experimentation
  • ❌ Limited RAM (8GB max), ARM compatibility issues
  • ❌ Not ideal for running many apps or heavy workloads
  • ❌ Requires external SSD for performance

Beelink SER9 Mini PC:

  • ✅ Extreme compute power, high RAM capacity
  • ✅ Premium build quality, future-proof
  • ✅ Many ports, Thunderbolt 4, eGPU support
  • ❌ Expensive, overkill for most node needs
  • ❌ High power consumption

Start9 Server One:

  • ✅ Plug-and-play, pre-installed Start9 OS
  • ✅ Official support, guaranteed compatibility
  • ✅ Good for those who want zero hassle
  • ❌ Higher cost for similar specs
  • ❌ Less DIY, less learning opportunity
  • ❌ Limited customization

MINISFORUM AI X1-255:

  • ✅ Excellent value, powerful and scalable
  • ✅ Up to 96GB RAM, 2x NVMe, OcuLink, eGPU
  • ✅ Great for running many apps, future-proof
  • ✅ DIY install, full control, learning opportunity
  • ❌ Slightly larger than Pi, more power draw

Decision framework

Choose based on your goals, budget, and technical comfort level:

Choose Raspberry Pi 5 (8GB) if you want

  • ✅ The lowest cost and lowest power usage
  • ✅ A compact, silent, and simple device
  • ✅ To learn and experiment with Bitcoin nodes on ARM
  • ✅ Huge community support and lots of guides
  • ⚡️ Caution: Limited RAM, not ideal for many apps or heavy workloads
  • ⚡️ Caution: Slower performance, needs external SSD for best results

Choose Beelink SER9 Mini PC if you want

  • ✅ Extreme compute power for many VMs or heavy multitasking
  • ✅ High RAM and storage expandability
  • ✅ Premium build and lots of ports (Thunderbolt, eGPU, etc.)
  • ✅ To use the device as a workstation/server beyond just a node
  • ⚡️ Caution: High price, overkill for most node-only setups
  • ⚡️ Caution: Higher power consumption

Choose Start9 Server One if you want

  • ✅ Zero hassle, plug-and-play experience
  • ✅ Official Start9 support and guaranteed compatibility
  • ✅ Pre-installed Start9 OS, ready out of the box
  • ✅ Minimal setup and maintenance
  • ⚡️ Caution: Higher cost for similar specs
  • ⚡️ Caution: Less DIY, less learning, limited customization

Choose MINISFORUM AI X1-255 if you want

  • ✅ The best value for high performance and expandability
  • ✅ To run many apps (Bitcoin Core, Lightning, BTCPay, Nostr, Nextcloud, Rust projects) at once
  • ✅ Full control over install, upgrades, and hardware
  • ✅ Room to grow (RAM, storage, OcuLink, eGPU)
  • ✅ A hands-on, learning-focused experience
  • ⚡️ Caution: Slightly larger than Pi, more power draw (but efficient for class)

My choice: MINISFORUM AI X1-255

After evaluating all options, I selected the MINISFORUM AI X1-255 for these reasons:

  • Exceptional value — $500 promo bundle (8-core Ryzen, 32GB RAM, 1TB SSD, all ports). Even at $550–700, it’s a great value for the specs.
  • Scalable for multiple Bitcoin applications — 32GB RAM and a powerful CPU enable running a full sovereignty stack (Bitcoin Core, Lightning, Mempool, BTCPay, Electrum, Nostr relay, Nextcloud, custom Rust projects) — all at once.
  • Expansion and connectivity — 2x M.2 NVMe slots (RAID-1 possible), OcuLink, USB 3.2, USB-C, external GPU support, and more. Room for backup, NAS, and family storage.
  • Professional build and learning — Reliable AMD platform, DDR5, active cooling, and a DIY install process for full control and learning.
  • Network and future-proofing — 2.5G Ethernet, WiFi 7, and enough power for years of growth and experimentation.

Summary: The MINISFORUM AI X1-255 is the best fit for a power user who wants to run a robust, scalable, and sovereign Bitcoin node with Start9 OS — and to keep learning and building for years to come.

Future Expansion Plan

The MINISFORUM AI X1-255 is chosen specifically for its expansion capabilities to support a complete redundant storage infrastructure. Here’s my planned future expansion:

Phase 1: Internal Storage Redundancy (RAID-1)

Goal: Dual internal SSDs with RAID-1 for operating system and application redundancy.

Configuration:

  • 2x 2TB NVMe SSDs in RAID-1 configuration
  • Primary use: Start9 OS installation and Bitcoin applications
  • Redundancy: Mirror configuration ensures no data loss if one drive fails

Hardware requirements:

  • X1-255 provides 2× M.2 2280 NVMe slots (use both for RAID-1)
  • Software RAID configuration via Proxmox VE (Btrfs, ZFS, or Linux md-raid at host level), or Start9 OS/Linux if running directly on hardware in future

Configuration method:

During Proxmox VE installation, select “Btrfs RAID-1” for the 2x 2TB NVMe drives to create mirrored storage at the host level. This protects all VMs including Start9 OS from single-drive failure. Proxmox will automatically manage the RAID-1, ensuring both Bitcoin Core and other applications in Start9 have redundant storage.

Suggested use — bundled 1TB NVMe SSD

The MINISFORUM X1-255 comes with a 1TB NVMe SSD that will not be used in Phase 1 (since Phase 1 requires dedicated RAID-1 drives). Once you replace the primary drive(s) with two larger NVMe SSDs for RAID-1, the bundled 1TB will be available. Consider using this spare 1TB drive for:

  • Start9 VM config backups: Store encrypted backups of Start9 master password, service configurations, and critical blockchain data outside the RAID-1 volume for disaster recovery
  • Offline signing / Cold storage: Use as an isolated signing device or air-gapped cold storage for private keys
  • Hot backup drive: Keep as a live backup that can be quickly swapped in if Phase 1 RAID drives fail
  • Blockchain data archive: Store Bitcoin Core snapshots or historical blockchain states for quick node rebuilds

To keep the spare 1TB SSD safe and accessible, consider a budget USB 3.1 M.2 NVMe enclosure (~$15-30 USD). Recommended options include ORICO or ASUS MB16ACV style USB-C M.2 enclosures (aluminum, passive cooling). Connect via USB-C to the MINISFORUM or an external USB hub; store the drive in a fireproof safe, safety deposit box, or other physically separate location. USB-powered enclosures eliminate the need for external adapters.

This lets you hot-swap the 1TB drive for testing/backup, store backups external to the main device for disaster recovery, and keep the drive secure yet accessible.

Estimated cost:

  • 2x 2TB NVMe SSDs: ~$125-175 each
  • Optional USB 3.1 M.2 NVMe enclosure for 1TB backup: ~$20-30

Total: ~$270-380

Phase 2: External Storage Redundancy (RAID-1)

Goal: Large-scale external storage with RAID-1 for family data and NAS functionality.

Configuration:

  • 2x 8TB+ SSDs in external enclosure with RAID-1
  • Primary use: Family storage, blockchain data, backup archives
  • Redundancy: Mirror configuration ensures no data loss if one drive fails
  • Connection: USB 3.2 Gen 2 or OcuLink for maximum speed

Hardware requirements:

  • Budget USB 3.2 Enclosure (no built-in RAID, 2+ drive bays for 3.5“/2.5“ SATA drives)
    • ~$30-60 USD (significantly cheaper than hardware RAID enclosures)
    • USB 3.2 Gen 2 (10 Gbps) connection
    • Tool-free drive installation preferred
    • Similar concept to Phase 1 M.2 NVMe enclosure, but for larger SATA drives
  • 2x 8TB SSDs (Samsung 870 QVO or similar)
    • SATA SSDs are more cost-effective for large capacities
    • Enterprise-grade options for better endurance

Configuration method:

Attach the budget USB enclosure with both 8TB drives to Proxmox VE host. Configure Proxmox to create a software RAID-1 using mdadm or LVM across the two drives. Mount this RAID volume to the Start9 OS VM, where Nextcloud will manage family file storage (photos, documents, encrypted backups) with full RAID-1 redundancy.

Estimated cost:

  • Budget USB 3.2 Enclosure: ~$30-60
  • 2x 8TB SSDs: ~$450-600 each

Total: ~$800-1,100

Planned Storage Architecture

MINISFORUM AI X1-255 with Proxmox VE
│
├── Phase 1: Internal Storage Redundancy (RAID-1)
│   └── 2x 2TB NVMe SSDs (Btrfs RAID-1 at Proxmox host level)
│       │
│       └── Start9 OS VM
│           ├── Bitcoin Core (full archival node)
│           ├── Lightning Network (instant payments)
│           ├── BTCPay Server (payment processor)
│           ├── Mempool Explorer (blockchain explorer)
│           ├── Electrs (Electrum server)
│           ├── Nostr Relay (censorship-resistant messaging)
│           ├── Mining Software (solo mining or pool)
│           ├── Wallet Services (multiple backends)
│           └── Custom Rust Bitcoin projects
│
└── Phase 2: External Storage Redundancy (Software RAID-1)
    └── 2x 8TB SSDs via Budget USB 3.2 Enclosure
        (Software RAID-1 configured in Proxmox using mdadm/LVM)
        │
        └── Mounted to Start9 OS VM
            └── Nextcloud NAS (family storage management)
                ├── Family Photos
                ├── Documents
                ├── Media Library
                ├── Encrypted Backups
                ├── Blockchain Data Archives
                └── Node Historical Data

Configuration Summary:

  • Phase 1: Btrfs RAID-1 protects Bitcoin applications and Start9 system
  • Phase 2: Software RAID-1 (mdadm/LVM) protects family data via Nextcloud NAS
  • Deployment: Both phases run on Proxmox VE with Start9 OS VM managing all services
  • Cost savings: Budget USB enclosure ($30–60) instead of expensive hardware RAID ($150–250)

Source: Minisforum AI X1 product page lists “2× M.2 2280 NVMe SSD Slot” under Product specification:

  • MINISFORUM Store Product Page
  • MINISFORUM Official Product Page

🗂️ Hardware Context Notice: The manufacturer page does not explicitly state PCIe generation/lanes per slot. PCIe 4.0 x4 per M.2 slot is typical for this platform, but you can confirm via the user manual or by inspecting with lspci once provisioned.

Additional Future Expansions

Beyond storage, the X1-255 can support:

  • External GPU - Via OcuLink for mining or AI experiments (~$300-800)
  • UPS Backup - Uninterruptible power supply for reliability (~$100-150)
  • Network Upgrade - 10 Gbps switch if needed for faster local transfers (~$200-300)

Timeline and Budget

Phase 1 (Internal RAID-1): Estimated 6-12 months

  • Cost: ~$270-380 (2x 2TB NVMe SSDs + optional USB enclosure for 1TB backup)
  • Implementation: After Start9 OS is stable and primary apps configured

Phase 2 (External RAID-1 + NAS): Estimated 12-24 months

  • Cost: ~$800-1,100 (budget USB enclosure + 2x 8TB SSDs)
  • Implementation: When family storage needs exceed local device capacity

Total future investment: ~$1,070-1,480 Combined with initial: ~$1,620-2,080 for complete sovereignty infrastructure

Why This Expansion Matters

  • Data Sovereignty: Complete control over family data without cloud dependencies
  • Redundancy: RAID-1 protects against drive failure (no data loss)
  • Bitcoin Growth: Blockchain continues growing (~500GB+ and increasing)
  • Application Diversity: Run multiple services without performance degradation
  • Future-Proof: Infrastructure scales with needs over 5-10 years

The initial investment provides a solid foundation that can scale with future needs without requiring a complete hardware replacement.

👉 Next: Setting up Hardware with AT&T BGW320 (Fiber 1 GIG)

Setting up Hardware with AT&T BGW320 (Fiber 1 GIG)

This section explains how to connect the chosen equipment to the AT&T BGW320 router, which provides a 1 GIG fiber internet package. The goal is to ensure stable, high‑speed connectivity for running a Bitcoin node with Start9 OS.

Pre-requisites

Before starting, ensure you are ready with all required hardware, software, and preparations as outlined below and in the previous step.

Preparations

  • Prepare your workspace with all components and tools

Hardware

  • MINISFORUM X1-255 Mini PC (with RAM and SSD installed; OcuLink chip will be installed in a later step)
  • SanDisk 64GB USB drive (for flashing Proxmox VE ISO image)
  • UGREEN Cat 8 Ethernet cable (3FT or longer)
  • AT&T BGW320 router (with available LAN ports)
  • Monitor and keyboard (for initial BIOS setup and configuration)
  • MacBook Air M1 (as a laptop client for remote access to Minisforum)
  • Power outlets for Mini PC and router

🔄 USB Drive Usage Notice: The SanDisk USB drive was originally planned for Start9 OS installation, but is now used to hold the Proxmox VE ISO due to incompatibility between Start9 and the MINISFORUM X1-255 hardware. Start9 runs as a VM inside Proxmox. See → Start9 Compatibility Notes for details.

Software

  • Proxmox VE ISO (latest stable release)
  • Start9 OS image (latest stable release)
  • Balena Etcher (for flashing USB drives)
  • Web browser (Safari, Chrome, Firefox, etc.)
  • SSH client (optional, for advanced troubleshooting)

References

  • Proxmox VE Official Downloads
  • Start9 Documentation
  • Balena Etcher
  • AT&T BGW320 Manual
  • Bitcoin Core Documentation
  • Start9 Community Forums

Step‑by‑Step Setup

🗂️ Hardware Setup Steps: The following step sequence and time estimates are based on my own hardware setup experience. Your process may vary depending on hardware, network, and prior experience.

Step 1: Prepare the Start9 Boot Drive

Create a bootable USB drive with the Start9 OS image.

📖 → Detailed Guide: Flashing Start9 OS to USB Drive

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Download Start9 OS (stable release)
  • Install Balena Etcher on MacBook Air M1
  • Flash the image to your SanDisk USB drive
  • Safely eject and prepare for installation

Time required: ~15 minutes

Step 2: Inspect Minisforum Hardware

Verify the internal components and install the OcuLink chip.

📖 → Detailed Guide: Inspecting Minisforum Hardware

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Open the Minisforum case safely
  • Verify 32GB DDR5 RAM installation
  • Confirm 1TB NVMe SSD presence
  • Install OcuLink chip/port
  • Close and secure the case

Time required: ~20 minutes

Step 3: Connect to Network

Connect the Mini PC to your AT&T BGW320 router via Ethernet.

📖 → Detailed Guide: Connecting Mini PC to Router

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Position hardware optimally
  • Identify correct Ethernet ports
  • Connect Cat 8 cable
  • Verify connection LEDs
  • Connect power supply

Time required: ~10 minutes

Step 4: Boot and Install Start9 OS

Boot from USB and install Start9 OS on the internal SSD.

📖 → Detailed Guide: Booting into Start9 OS

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Connect monitor and keyboard
  • Access BIOS/boot menu
  • Select USB boot device
  • Install Start9 OS to SSD
  • Complete first boot setup

Time required: ~30 minutes (direct install)

⚙️ Hardware Compatibility Notice: If Start9 OS fails to boot directly on the Minisforum X1-255 (kernel panics, black screens, GPU incompatibility), see → Start9 Compatibility Notes below for the recommended virtualized setup approach using Proxmox VE.

📖 → Alternative: Virtualized Start9 OS Setup (Proxmox VE)

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Install Proxmox VE on Minisforum X1-255 hardware
  • Configure Proxmox VE for secure virtualization
  • Create and configure a Start9 OS virtual machine (VM)
  • Install Start9 OS inside the VM
  • Set up network connectivity for the VM
  • Access Start9 web UI from your MacBook via https://xxx-yyy.local (replace with your device name; see below) or https://192.168.1.101 (example IP)
  • Troubleshoot hardware compatibility and virtualization issues

Time required: ~90 minutes (Proxmox install + VM setup + Start9 install)

Step 5: Configure Network Settings

Configure the network connection in Start9 OS to enable internet access and confirm your node has full gigabit connectivity for optimal blockchain synchronization.

📖 → Detailed Guide: Configuring Network Settings and Speed Verification

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Access Start9 OS setup wizard
  • Select wired Ethernet connection
  • Verify DHCP IP address assignment
  • Confirm gigabit connection speed (1 Gbps)
  • Run speed tests from multiple sources
  • Verify router connection details
  • Troubleshoot any speed issues
  • Complete initial network configuration

Time required: ~10 minutes

Step 6: Secure Proxmox VE (if using Proxmox virtualization)

If you chose the virtualized setup (Proxmox VE) rather than installing Start9 directly on the hardware, secure the Proxmox host before provisioning the Start9 VM.

📖 → Secure Proxmox VE

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Ensure the Proxmox host is patched and up to date
  • Configure SSH key access and disable password login for root
  • Set up the host firewall and management access rules

Time required: ~20 minutes

Step 7: Secure AT&T BGW320 Router

Before exposing services on your network, secure the AT&T BGW320 router (admin credentials, remote management, firewall/port-forwarding rules).

📖 → Secure AT&T BGW320 Router

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Change default admin password and create a local admin account if applicable
  • Disable remote/portal access from the WAN side
  • Configure any required port-forwards carefully and document them

Time required: ~15 minutes

Estimated Total Time:

  • Direct Start9 install: ~1 hour 30 minutes
  • Proxmox VM route: ~2 hours 50 minutes

Start9 Compatibility Notes

Why Proxmox VE instead of Direct Start9?

The MINISFORUM X1-255 is not fully compatible with Start9 OS as of early 2025. While the hardware is powerful enough to run Start9, installation and driver support are problematic. The solution is to:

  1. Install Proxmox VE on the MINISFORUM hardware as the hypervisor
  2. Run Start9 as a VM within Proxmox, providing excellent isolation and resource management
  3. Maintain full Bitcoin sovereignty while ensuring hardware stability

This approach actually provides advantages:

  • Better resource isolation between Proxmox host and Start9 VMs
  • Ability to run multiple VMs if needed (backups, testing, etc.)
  • More reliable hardware driver support via Linux kernel
  • Cleaner separation of concerns

For more information on the Phase 1 and Phase 2 storage architecture supporting this setup, see → Future Expansion Plan in Choosing Equipment.

Flashing Start9 OS to USB Drive

This guide provides clear, step-by-step instructions for flashing the Start9 OS image to a USB drive using a Mac. This process prepares your USB drive for installing Start9 OS on your Mini PC or compatible server hardware.

In this guide, you will:

  • Download the correct Start9 OS image
  • Verify the image’s integrity (optional but recommended)
  • Flash the image to a USB drive using Balena Etcher
  • Safely eject and prepare the USB for installation

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Use a Mac (Apple Silicon or Intel) with administrative access
  • Ensure a stable, preferably wired, internet connection for large downloads
  • Have at least 2–3 GB of free disk space in your Downloads folder
  • Review hardware compatibility notes for your Mini PC (see below if using Proxmox VE)

Hardware

  • MacBook Air M1 (or any Mac with Apple Silicon or Intel)
    • Tested on macOS Tahoe 26.1 (Apple Silicon, 16GB RAM)
  • USB drive, 16GB minimum (e.g., SanDisk 64GB Ultra Dual Drive USB Type-C)
  • Minisforum X1-255 Mini PC (or other compatible hardware for Start9 OS)
  • Wired Ethernet connection (recommended for image download)

Software

  • Start9 OS image (.iso file to be flashed)
  • Balena Etcher (for flashing OS images to USB)
  • Web browser (Safari, Chrome, or Firefox for downloading images)
  • Terminal (for optional verification and disk management commands)

References

  • Start9 OS Releases
  • Balena Etcher Download
  • Balena Etcher ARM64 GitHub Release

Step‑by‑Step Instructions

Step 1: Download Start9 OS Image

Download the Start9 OS image from the official GitHub releases page.

Understanding Version Types:

Start9 offers different release types on their GitHub page:

  • Stable Release: Thoroughly tested, production-ready versions

    • Filename format: startos-[version]-[commit]_x86_64.iso
    • Example: startos-0.3.5.1-39de098_x86_64.iso
    • No “Pre-release” tag on GitHub
    • Recommended for production use and maximum stability

  • Alpha/Beta Release: Latest features, actively developed versions

    • Filename format: startos-[version]-alpha.[number]-[commit].dev_x86_64.iso
    • Example: startos-0.4.0-alpha.15-24eb27f.dev_x86_64.iso
    • Marked with “Pre-release” tag (yellow label) on GitHub
    • Includes .dev in filename
    • May have bugs, recommended for testing and learning

For this guide, we’ll use the stable release (0.3.5.1) to maximize reliability while setting up a production-ready node.

Download Steps:

🌐 Download Integrity Notice: Use a wired connection when downloading the Start9 OS image to avoid corruption from unstable WiFi. On MacBook Air M1, ensure you have a stable internet connection.

  1. Open Safari or your preferred browser and navigate to the Start9 download page: Start9 Download Page

  2. Locate the latest stable release (no yellow “Pre-release” tag):

    • Look for the newest tag without a Pre-release label (e.g., “0.3.5.1”)
    • Stable builds are fully tested and recommended for production use
  1. Under the Assets section of the stable release, click to download:
    • File name: startos-0.3.5.1-39de098_x86_64.iso
    • Architecture: x86_64 (compatible with Minisforum X1-255)
    • File size: ~1.1 GB

💾 Storage Space Notice: Ensure your MacBook Air has at least 2-3 GB of free space in the Downloads folder for the Start9 OS image (actual size: ~1.1 GB).

  1. The download will begin automatically and save to your ~/Downloads folder

Wait for the download to complete before proceeding to the next step.

You can verify the download by running:

ls -lh ~/Downloads/startos-0.3.5.1-39de098_x86_64.iso

Expected output should show the file with a size of approximately 1.1 GB:

-rw-r--r--  1 yourusername  staff   1.1G Nov 27 10:30 startos-0.3.5.1-39de098_x86_64.iso

Verify file integrity (optional but recommended):

To ensure the downloaded ISO hasn’t been corrupted or modified, you can verify its SHA256 checksum:

  1. On the GitHub release page, look for the Checksums or Hashes section (usually in the release notes)
  2. Find the SHA256 hash for startos-0.3.5.1-39de098_x86_64.iso
  3. Run the verification command:
shasum -a 256 ~/Downloads/startos-0.3.5.1-39de098_x86_64.iso
  1. Compare the output with the hash provided on GitHub. If they match exactly, the file is genuine and uncorrupted.
# Expected output for version 0.3.5.1:
181fec43a3b7287fe6c6cdb70bcba79a5596fc2be1dbe5a54e09a4c142272155  startos-0.3.5.1-39de098_x86_64.iso

Step 2: Install Balena Etcher

Download and install Balena Etcher for Apple Silicon Macs:

  1. Open your browser and go to the Etcher download page: Etcher Download
  2. Click the Download Etcher button; you’ll be taken to the downloads page with several platform options. Choose macOS (Apple Silicon/ARM64).

🍏 Apple Silicon Notice: Balena Etcher for Apple Silicon (ARM64) runs natively on M1 Macs for optimal performance.

  1. The file will download as balenaEtcher-2.1.4-arm64.dmg to your Downloads folder
  2. Open the downloaded .dmg file from your Downloads folder:
open ~/Downloads/balenaEtcher-*.dmg

  1. Drag the balenaEtcher app icon to your Applications folder

  2. Eject the Balena Etcher disk image from Finder

    • In Finder’s sidebar under “Locations”, select the mounted Etcher disk (e.g., “balenaEtcher”)
    • Click the eject icon (⏏) next to the mounted disk, or right‑click and choose Eject
    • Alternatively, on the Desktop, drag the mounted Etcher disk to the Trash (which turns into an Eject icon)
    • Wait for the volume to disappear from the sidebar/Desktop before proceeding

  3. Open Balena Etcher from your Applications folder or Launchpad

  4. If macOS shows a security warning, click Open to confirm you want to run the app

🔒 Permissions Notice: You may need to grant Balena Etcher disk access permissions in System Settings > Privacy & Security > Full Disk Access if prompted.

Verify Balena Etcher integrity (optional but recommended):

You can verify the Balena Etcher DMG file using SHA256 checksum. Choose one of the following options:

Option 1: Using the official SHA256SUMS file

  1. Visit the Etcher ARM64 release page: Etcher v2.1.4 (ARM64) release
  2. Download the file SHA256SUMS.Darwin.arm64.txt from the Assets section
  3. Calculate the hash of your downloaded file:
shasum -a 256 ~/Downloads/balenaEtcher-2.1.4-arm64.dmg
  1. Compare the output with the hash in the SHA256SUMS file:
cat ~/Downloads/SHA256SUMS.Darwin.arm64.txt

If they match, the file is genuine and uncorrupted.

Option 2: Verify from GitHub release page

  1. Visit the Etcher ARM64 release page: Etcher v2.1.4 (ARM64) release
  2. Look for the SHA256 hash for balenaEtcher-2.1.4-arm64.dmg in the release notes or checksums section
  3. Calculate the hash of your downloaded file:
shasum -a 256 ~/Downloads/balenaEtcher-2.1.4-arm64.dmg
  1. Compare the output with the hash from Etcher ARM64 release page. If they match, the file is genuine and uncorrupted.

💽 File Format Notice:

  • A .dmg file is a macOS disk image. When you open it, macOS mounts it like a virtual drive containing the application.
  • After opening the .dmg, complete installation by dragging the balenaEtcher app into Applications. You can then eject the mounted disk image.

Step 3: Insert the USB Drive

Connect your USB drive to your MacBook Air:

  1. The SanDisk Ultra Dual Drive supports both USB‑C and USB‑A connectors. Connection speed considerations:

    • USB‑C (recommended): Provides speeds up to 5 Gbps — flashing typically completes in 30 seconds to 2 minutes
    • USB 3.0 or higher: Provides speeds up to 5 Gbps — expect 1-3 minutes for flashing
    • USB 2.0: Will work but is significantly slower (up to 480 Mbps). Expect longer flashing times (10-20 minutes)

  2. Plug the drive in and wait a few seconds for macOS to recognize it.

  3. A new volume should appear on your Desktop or in Finder’s sidebar

You can verify the USB drive is detected by running:

diskutil list

Look for your USB drive in the output (e.g., /dev/disk2, /dev/disk3, or /dev/disk4). Note the disk identifier for reference.

Example output:

/dev/disk4 (external, physical):
   #:                       TYPE NAME                    SIZE       IDENTIFIER
   0:     FDisk_partition_scheme                        *64.0 GB    disk4
   1:             Windows_FAT_32 SANDISK                 64.0 GB    disk4s1

Step 4: Flash the Image

🗑️ Data Loss Warning: Flashing will erase all data on the USB drive. Make sure it does not contain important files before proceeding.

Use Balena Etcher to write the Start9 OS image to your USB drive:

  1. Open Balena Etcher from your Applications folder
  2. Click Flash from file button
  3. Navigate to your Downloads folder and select: startos-0.3.5.1-39de098_x86_64.iso
  4. Click Select target button
  5. Choose your SanDisk USB drive from the list (be careful to select the correct drive!)
  1. Click Flash! to begin the flashing process
  2. macOS will show a popup asking for permission for Balena Etcher to access and write to the external USB drive. Click OK or Allow to grant permission.
  3. Enter your Mac password when prompted (required for disk write access)
  4. The flashing process will begin, showing progress and estimated time remaining
  5. Wait for the process to complete (typically 30 seconds to 3 minutes with USB-C, or 5-15 minutes with USB 2.0)
  6. Balena Etcher will automatically verify the written data to ensure integrity
  7. Once you see Flash Complete!, the USB drive is ready

💿 Boot Image Notice: After flashing completes, macOS may show a popup saying “The disk you inserted was not readable by this computer” with options to Initialize, Ignore, or Eject. This is normal and expected. The USB drive now contains a Linux-based boot image that macOS cannot read. Click Ignore or Eject. Do not click Initialize as it would erase the StartOS image.

Step 5: Safely Eject the USB Drive

If you clicked Eject in the macOS popup from previous step, the USB drive has already been ejected and you can skip to physically removing it. Otherwise, follow one of the options below to properly eject the USB drive to prevent data corruption:

Option 1: Using Finder

  1. Open Finder
  2. Locate your USB drive in the sidebar (under “Locations”)
  3. Click the eject icon (⏏) next to the drive name
  4. Wait for the drive to disappear from the sidebar
  5. Physically remove the USB drive from your MacBook Air

Option 2: Using Terminal

  1. First, identify your disk with:
diskutil list
  1. Eject the disk (using your disk identifier, in this case disk4):
diskutil eject /dev/disk4
  1. You should see:
Disk /dev/disk4 ejected
  1. Physically remove the USB drive from your MacBook Air

The USB drive is now ready to be used as a boot drive for installing Start9 OS on the Minisforum Mini PC.

🔄 USB Drive Usage Notice: This USB drive is only needed for the initial installation. For my setup, it is also used to install Proxmox VE due to hardware compatibility. After Start9 OS is installed on the Mini PC’s SSD (or as a VM in Proxmox VE), the USB drive can be reused for other purposes.

👈 Prev: Setting up Hardware with AT&T BGW320 (Fiber 1 GIG)

👉 Next: Inspecting Minisforum Hardware

Inspecting Minisforum Hardware

This guide provides clear, step-by-step instructions to inspect your Minisforum Mini PC hardware before installation or use. Careful inspection ensures all components are present, undamaged, and ready for your Bitcoin node or server setup.

In this guide, you will:

  • Prepare a static-free workspace
  • Open the Mini PC case and identify key components
  • Verify RAM, SSD, and OcuLink hardware
  • Document hardware details for future reference

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Set up a clean, static-free workspace
  • Have a small Phillips screwdriver available

Hardware

  • Minisforum AI X1-255 Mini PC (with pre-installed 1TB NVMe SSD)
  • OcuLink chip/port (included in the Minisforum package)
  • Camera or smartphone for documenting hardware details (optional)

Software

  • No software required

References

  • Minisforum X1-255 Product Page
  • Minisforum Support & Manuals

Step‑by‑Step Instructions

Step 1: Unbox and Check Package Contents

  1. Open the Minisforum AI X1-255 package.
  1. Carefully remove all items and lay them out on your static-free workspace.

🧤 Static-Free Workspace: Work in a static-free environment to avoid damaging sensitive components.

  1. Verify that the following items are included:

    • Minisforum AI X1-255 Mini PC (with pre-installed 1TB NVMe SSD)
    • Power supply unit (PSU) and power cable
    • OcuLink chip/port module
    • HDMI cable (included)
    • Accessories (e.g., screws, mounting brackets, documentation)

📸 Take Photos: Take photos of the internal components for your records if needed.

  1. Check that all items are undamaged and present.
  1. Locate the four screws on the back panel of Minisforum AI X1-255 Mini PC case.

⚡️ Warranty Notice: Opening the case may void the manufacturer’s warranty. Check warranty terms before proceeding if this is a concern.

  1. Gently unscrew and remove the back panel to access the internal components.

🔩 Screw Storage Tip: Keep the screws in a safe place while the case is open to avoid losing them.

Step 2: Check Pre-installed RAM

  1. Verify the amount and type of RAM currently installed in the SODIMM slots.
  2. The Mini PC may have one or two RAM modules pre-installed.
  3. Note the specifications (e.g., 16GB DDR5, DDR4, etc.) for your records.

🧠 RAM Upgrade Tip: The Mini PC has two SODIMM slots. If you need more RAM in the future, you can upgrade to 64GB or higher depending on motherboard support.

Step 3: Check Pre-installed NVMe Storage

  1. The MINISFORUM X1-255 comes with a 1TB NVMe SSD pre-installed in one of the two M.2 2280 NVMe slots.

💾 NVMe Storage Tip: Both M.2 2280 NVMe slots can be used for SSDs or for installing the OcuLink chip. If you need more storage, you can replace the existing drive with a larger capacity SSD (2TB, 4TB, etc.), or add a second SSD for expansion or RAID-1 mirroring.

  1. Note the capacity and model of the SSD for your records.
  2. Ensure the SSD is firmly seated in its slot.
  3. Locate the second M.2 2280 NVMe slot. As only one can be installed in the second slot at a time, decide whether you want to:
  • Add a second SSD (for more storage or RAID-1)
  • Or install the OcuLink chip (for high-speed external storage)

🛠️ Expansion Notice: If you plan for internal mirroring (RAID-1), plan to install a second, matching-capacity NVMe SSD in this slot later. If you want to use OcuLink, proceed to the next step.

Step 4: Install OcuLink Chip/Port (optional)

🔌 OcuLink Expansion: The OcuLink chip/port supports high-speed external storage expansion if needed in the future. Only install the OcuLink chip if you do not plan to use both M.2 slots for SSDs.

If you want to use OcuLink for external storage, follow these steps:

  1. Locate the OcuLink chip/port that comes with the Minisforum AI X1-255 package.
  2. Verify that the chip and connector are clean and free of dust or debris.
  3. Identify the available M.2 2280 slot on the motherboard (typically near the back I/O or expansion area).
  4. Unscrew the screw at the end of the available M.2 slot and set it aside.
  5. Carefully install the OcuLink chip/port into the slot, ensuring proper alignment.
  1. Gently press down until the chip is firmly seated, then use the screw to secure the OcuLink chip/port in place.

Step 5: Close the Case

  1. Carefully replace the back panel, ensuring it aligns properly with the case.
  2. Reinsert and tighten the four screws securely, but do not over-tighten.
  3. Verify the case is properly sealed before proceeding to network setup.

👈 Prev: Flashing Start9 OS to USB drive

👉 Next: Connecting Mini PC to Router

Connecting Mini PC to Router

This guide provides step-by-step instructions to physically connect your Minisforum X1-255 Mini PC to the AT&T BGW320 router using an Ethernet cable. This ensures a stable, high-speed network connection for your Bitcoin node and related applications.

In this guide, you will:

  • Position your Mini PC for optimal connectivity
  • Identify the correct Ethernet ports on both devices
  • Connect the Mini PC to the router using a Cat 8 Ethernet cable
  • Verify the physical network connection

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Clear space near the router for the Mini PC
  • Have the power cable for the Mini PC ready

Hardware

  • MINISFORUM X1-255 Mini PC (with hardware inspection completed)
  • AT&T BGW320 router (powered on and functioning)
  • UGREEN Cat 8 Ethernet cable (3FT or longer)

Software

  • No software required

References

  • AT&T BGW320 Manual

Step‑by‑Step Instructions

Step 1: Position the Mini PC

Find an appropriate location for your Mini PC near the router:

  • Place the MINISFORUM X1-255 Mini PC on a stable surface near the AT&T BGW320 router
  • Ensure there is adequate ventilation around the Mini PC (at least 2-3 inches of clearance on all sides)
  • Keep the Mini PC away from heat sources and direct sunlight
  • Position it within reach of the Ethernet cable length (typically 3FT)

Step 2: Identify the Ethernet Ports

🔌 Wired Ethernet Recommended: Wired Ethernet provides significantly better stability, consistent speeds, and reliability than WiFi—critical for running a Bitcoin node 24/7. Strongly recommended over WiFi for all node operations.

Before connecting, locate the correct ports on both devices:

On the Mini PC (back panel):

  • Locate the 2.5G RJ45 Ethernet port (usually labeled “2.5G LAN” or with a network icon)
  • This port is typically larger than USB ports and has a rectangular shape with a clip
  • The Mini PC may have multiple ports; look for the one marked for networking

On the AT&T BGW320 router:

  • Locate the LAN ports on the back of the router (typically 4 yellow ports labeled LAN 1-4)
  • Do NOT use the red WAN port (this is for the incoming fiber connection)
  • Any of the yellow LAN ports will work

Step 3: Connect the Ethernet Cable

🔗 Cat 8 Cable Tip: Cat 8 cables support up to 40 Gbps, providing excellent shielding and future-proofing for faster network standards.

Connect the UGREEN Cat 8 Ethernet cable between the two devices:

  1. Take one end of the UGREEN Cat 8 Ethernet cable

  2. Insert it into the 2.5G RJ45 port on the Mini PC’s back panel

    • Push gently but firmly until you hear a click
    • The cable should be securely locked in place

  3. Take the other end of the cable

  4. Insert it into any available LAN port on the BGW320 router (yellow ports)

    • Push gently until you hear a click
    • The cable should be securely locked in place

  5. Verify both ends are firmly seated and the cable is not bent at sharp angles

🧹 Cable Management Tip: Use velcro cable ties or clips to keep Ethernet cables organized and prevent accidental disconnection or tripping hazards.

Step 4: Verify Connection

After connecting the cable, verify the physical connection:

🚦 No Port LEDs Notice: The MINISFORUM X1-255 and AT&T BGW320 do not have visible LED indicators on the Ethernet ports. Network status will be confirmed through software after booting (Start9 OS or Proxmox VE).

On the Mini PC:

  • The MINISFORUM X1-255 typically does not have visible LED indicators on the Ethernet port. If your unit does have a visible LED, check that it is lit or blinking to confirm a physical link.
  • The cable connection itself is the primary indicator — ensure it is firmly seated with an audible click.
  • Network connectivity will be confirmed when you boot the system.

On the BGW320 router:

  • The BGW320 typically does not display LED lights on the LAN ports themselves. If your router does have visible LAN port LEDs, check that they are lit or blinking to confirm a physical link.
  • The cable connection is secure when fully inserted at both ends.
  • Network connectivity will be confirmed once the Mini PC powers on and establishes connection.

If the connection is not established later:

  • Verify the cable is fully inserted at both ends (listen for a click)
  • Try a different LAN port on the router
  • Check that the router is powered on
  • Check that the cable is not damaged or bent

Step 5: Connect Power to Mini PC

With the network cable in place, prepare to power on the Mini PC:

  1. Locate the power port on the Mini PC (typically a DC barrel jack or USB-C power port)
  2. Connect the included power adapter to the Mini PC
  3. Plug the power adapter into a wall outlet or surge protector
  4. Do not power on yet — you’ll do this in the next step when setting up the system

Notes

👈 Prev: Inspecting Minisforum Hardware

👉 Next: Booting into Start9 OS

Booting into Start9 OS

This guide provides step-by-step instructions to boot the Minisforum X1-255 Mini PC from a USB drive containing Start9 OS and install it onto the internal SSD. It covers BIOS/boot menu access, USB boot, installation, troubleshooting, and headless setup options. Follow these steps for a smooth, reliable installation and first boot.

In this guide, you will:

  • Boot the Mini PC from a prepared Start9 OS USB drive
  • Access and configure BIOS/boot menu settings (including UEFI/Legacy mode)
  • Select the correct boot device and install Start9 OS to the internal SSD
  • Remove the USB drive and boot from SSD
  • Troubleshoot common boot, display, and hardware compatibility issues
  • Set up headless access (SSH, web UI) after installation

⚡️ Hardware Compatibility Notice: Not all hardware is fully compatible with Start9 OS’s Linux kernel. If Start9 OS fails to boot directly on the Minisforum X1-255 (kernel panics, black screens, GPU incompatibility), or you experience persistent boot/display issues, check the Troubleshooting section below or see the Virtualized Start9 OS Setup (Advanced) for the recommended virtualized setup approach using Proxmox VE.

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Ensure your Mini PC is connected to the router and plugged into power
  • Have a monitor (HDMI or DisplayPort) and cable ready for initial installation (can be borrowed temporarily; see Headless Setup for post-install access options)
  • Prepare a keyboard (USB or wireless with USB receiver)
  • Mouse (optional, but recommended for easier navigation)

Hardware

  • MINISFORUM X1-255 Mini PC (connected to router and power)
  • AT&T BGW320 router (or other router, powered on and accessible)
  • USB drive (8GB or larger) with Start9 OS flashed (see previous step)
  • Monitor (HDMI or DisplayPort)
  • Keyboard (USB or wireless with receiver)
  • Mouse (optional)

Software

  • Start9 OS image (flashed to USB drive)
  • SSH client (optional, for headless setup)
  • Web browser (optional, for accessing Start9 web UI)

References

  • Start9 OS Documentation
  • Start9 OS GitHub Releases
  • Minisforum X1-255 Product Page
  • AT&T BGW320 Manual

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Connect Display and Input Devices

Before powering on, connect your peripherals:

  1. Connect your monitor to the Mini PC using an HDMI or DisplayPort cable

    • Locate the video output ports on the back of the Mini PC
    • Insert the cable firmly into both the Mini PC and monitor
    • Turn on the monitor and select the correct input source

  2. Connect a keyboard to one of the USB ports on the Mini PC

    • Use a USB-A or USB-C port depending on your keyboard
    • If using a wireless keyboard, insert the USB receiver

  3. (Optional) Connect a mouse for easier navigation

    • Plug into any available USB port

Step 2: Insert the Start9 OS USB Drive

Insert the prepared bootable USB drive:

  1. Locate an available USB port on the Mini PC (front or back panel)

🔌 USB Port Selection: The installation will work from either USB 2.0 or 3.0 ports, though 3.0 is faster.

  1. Insert the USB drive containing the flashed Start9 OS image

💾 USB Drive Reminder: Make sure you’re using the USB drive you prepared in the Flashing Start9 OS step, not an empty drive.

  1. Push gently until fully seated

Step 3: Power On the Mini PC

Turn on the Mini PC for the first time:

  1. Press the power button on the Mini PC (usually on the front panel)
  2. Watch the monitor for the Minisforum logo or POST screen to appear
  3. You’ll have a brief window (2-5 seconds) to enter the BIOS/boot menu

🖥️ Multiple Monitors: If you have multiple monitors, the BIOS may display on a different screen than the one showing the OS later.

Step 4: Enter the BIOS/Boot Menu

Access the boot menu to select the USB drive:

BIOS Setup Method

  1. As soon as the Minisforum logo appears, repeatedly press DEL or F2 (for me, DEL successfully enters BIOS setup)

🛠️ Keyboard Troubleshooting: If your keyboard doesn’t work in BIOS, try a different USB port or use a wired keyboard instead of wireless.

  1. This enters the BIOS Setup utility

💻 BIOS Prompt: If you miss the BIOS prompt on first boot, simply restart the Mini PC and press DEL or F2 repeatedly during startup.

  1. Use this method to change boot order and select the USB drive for installation

Common key combinations by manufacturer:

  • Minisforum: F11, F12, DEL, or F2
  • If unsure, try pressing all of these keys repeatedly during startup
  • If you miss the timing:
    • Wait for the system to finish booting (or show “No bootable device” error)
    • Press the power button to shut down
    • Wait 5 seconds and power on again
    • Be ready to press the key as soon as you see the logo

⌨️ Boot Key Tip: If F11/F12/DEL/F2 don’t work, check the Minisforum manual for your specific model’s boot key.

Step 5: Select the USB Drive as Boot Device

Choose the USB drive from the boot options:

🛡️ UEFI Requirement: Start9 OS typically requires UEFI boot mode. Ensure your BIOS is set to UEFI mode, not Legacy/CSM mode.

If using Boot Menu (F11/F12):

  1. Use arrow keys to navigate the list of boot devices
  2. Look for your USB drive, which may appear as:
    • “SanDisk” (or your USB brand name)
    • “USB HDD” or “USB Device”
    • “UEFI: SanDisk” or similar
  3. Highlight the USB drive and press Enter

If using BIOS Setup (DEL/F2):

  1. Navigate to the Boot tab using arrow keys
  2. Find Boot Option #1 or Boot Priority
  3. Change the first boot device to your USB drive

  1. Save and Exit:

    • Try pressing F10 to save and exit (confirm when prompted)
    • If F10 doesn’t work: Use the right arrow key to navigate to the Save & Exit tab at the top menu, then select Save Changes and Exit and press Enter

  2. The system will restart and boot from the USB drive

Step 6: Start9 OS Installation Begins

⚡️ Data Loss Warning: Installing Start9 OS will erase all existing data on the internal SSD. Make sure you’ve backed up any important data beforehand.

Once the USB drive boots, the Start9 OS installation will begin:

  1. You’ll see the Start9 logo and loading screen
  2. The installer will automatically detect the internal SSD
  3. Follow the on-screen prompts to install Start9 OS onto the SSD

Installation Process:

  • The installer will partition and format the internal SSD
  • Start9 OS files will be copied from the USB to the SSD
  • This typically takes 5-15 minutes depending on hardware
  • Do not power off or remove the USB drive during installation
  1. When installation completes, you’ll be prompted to remove the USB drive and restart

🗃️ USB Use: Once Start9 OS is fully installed on the Mini PC’s internal SSD, the USB drive is no longer needed and can be safely removed and reused for other purposes.

Step 7: First Boot from Internal SSD

After installation and restart:

  1. Remove the USB drive when prompted (if you haven’t already)
  2. The system will restart automatically
  3. Start9 OS will boot from the internal SSD (no USB needed)
  4. You’ll see the Start9 setup wizard on first boot

🛠️ Boot Priority Reminder: If the system tries to boot from USB again:

  • Enter BIOS/Boot Menu
  • Change boot priority to boot from the internal SSD first
  • Save and exit

If you encounter problems booting or do not see the expected display, see Troubleshooting: Boot and Display Issues.

Headless Setup (No Monitor)

If you don’t have a monitor available, you can still set up the Mini PC using headless access methods after Start9 OS is installed. The initial boot and installation still require a display (which can be borrowed temporarily). After installation, you have three options for accessing your node from your laptop:

Option 1: Access via Web UI (used in this guide)

  • Open a web browser on your laptop and go to https://xxx-yyy.local (replace with your device name; see below)
  • Alternatively, access Start9 directly via its IP address (example: https://192.168.1.101)

🔎 Device Name Lookup: In the Start9 web interface, go to System → About → Web Addresses → LAN. The .local address uses your device name (e.g., xxx-yyy.local). For Proxmox VE, use the VM’s hostname as shown in your router’s device list or network settings.

Option 2: Access via SSH (also used in this guide)

  1. Find the Mini PC’s IP address on your router’s admin page (AT&T BGW320):

    • Open browser to http://192.168.1.254
    • Log in with your admin credentials
    • Look for “Connected Devices” or “Device List”
    • Find “start” or “Minisforum” device and note its IP address

  2. Enable SSH on Start9 (if not already enabled):

    • Access Start9 web UI from your MacBook: https://xxx-yyy.local (replace with your device name)
    • Alternatively, access Start9 directly via its IP address: https://192.168.1.101
    • Navigate to System > SSH
    • Enable SSH access and add your SSH key

  3. Connect from your MacBook:

    ssh start9@192.168.1.101
# Or, using the .local address (replace xxx-yyy with your device name):
ssh start9@xxx-yyy.local

Option 3: Direct Ethernet Connection (advanced)

You can connect your MacBook directly to the Mini PC via Ethernet for initial configuration:

  1. Connect an Ethernet cable directly between MacBook and Mini PC
  2. On your MacBook:
    • Go to System Settings > Network
    • Select your Ethernet adapter
    • Configure IPv4: Using DHCP with manual address
    • Set IP: 192.168.2.100 (or any 192.168.2.x address)
  3. Access Mini PC at a static IP or use mDNS discovery

⚡️ Direct Ethernet Setup Note: This method is complex and requires networking knowledge. The temporary monitor option is much simpler for initial setup.

My Approach

Use both web UI and SSH access after installation, depending on your use case. The direct Ethernet method is not recommended due to its complexity. Borrow a monitor/TV for initial installation, then use web browser or SSH for all ongoing management and access.

Troubleshooting: Boot and Display Issues

Symptom: Kernel Panic or Black Screen After Installation

If Start9 OS installs successfully but fails to boot fully or shows a black screen with no web UI access, this is often caused by GPU driver incompatibility, particularly with AMD Radeon graphics cards in Minisforum systems.

Example issue: Minisforum AI X1-255 with integrated Radeon GPU may experience kernel incompatibility with Start9 OS’s Linux kernel, preventing proper graphics initialization and system boot.

Option 1: Try Booting with Minimal Graphics Mode

If you can access the GRUB boot menu during startup:

  1. At the GRUB menu (may appear briefly), press E to edit boot parameters
  2. Find the line starting with linux and add: nomodeset
    • Example: linux /boot/vmlinuz-... root=... nomodeset
  3. Press Ctrl+X to boot with this parameter
  4. This disables GPU acceleration and may allow the system to boot
  5. If successful, access the web UI at https://xxx-yyy.local (replace with your device name) or https://192.168.1.101 (example IP address)

Result: This may allow boot to complete, but graphics performance will be degraded.

Option 2: Check for Kernel Updates

Newer Start9 OS releases may include updated kernels with better hardware support:

  1. Boot from the USB drive again
  2. Try downloading the latest stable Start9 OS release from GitHub
  3. Flash it to the USB drive (overwriting the previous installation)
  4. Retry the full installation process

Result: Sometimes newer kernels resolve hardware compatibility issues.

Option 3: Virtualized Start9 OS (used in this guide for incompatible hardware)

If Options 1-2 don’t resolve the issue, you can run Start9 OS as a virtual machine on top of a compatible Linux host OS:

Approach:

  1. Install a Linux distribution or virtualization platform with proven Radeon driver support:
    • Proxmox VE
    • Ubuntu Server 24.04 LTS
    • Debian 12
  2. Use KVM/QEMU virtualization to run Start9 OS as a guest VM
  3. Allocate sufficient resources to the VM (CPU, RAM, storage)
  4. Access Start9 OS web UI normally from the guest VM

Advantages:

  • Works around kernel incompatibilities completely
  • Flexible resource allocation
  • Can run other services on the host
  • Provides a clean separation of concerns

Disadvantages:

  • Slight performance overhead from virtualization
  • More complex initial setup
  • Requires SSH access to the host for management

For detailed instructions on this approach, see: Virtualized Start9 OS Setup Guide (Advanced)

👈 Prev: Connecting Mini PC to Router

👉 Next: Virtualized Start9 OS Setup (Advanced)

Virtualized Start9 OS Setup Guide (Advanced)

This guide covers running Start9 OS as a virtual machine (VM) on a Linux host. Use this method if your hardware is incompatible with native Start9 OS installation, or if you want to run multiple services on one machine.

In this guide, you will:

  • Set up a Proxmox VE (Option A) or KVM/QEMU (Option B) host for Start9 OS
  • Configure VM resources and storage
  • Install and launch Start9 OS as a VM
  • Troubleshoot common virtualization issues

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Mini PC or server connected to router and power
  • Basic familiarity with Linux/web interfaces
  • SSH access capability

Hardware

  • Recommended: MINISFORUM X1-255 Mini PC (32GB RAM, 1TB SSD)
  • Wired Ethernet connection (e.g., UGREEN Cat 8 cable)
  • Monitor and keyboard (for initial setup)
  • Laptop/desktop for downloads and web access
  • USB drive (64GB+) for flashing ISOs

Software (Choose One)

  • Option A: Proxmox VE (Used in this guide)
    • Proxmox Virtual Environment 9.1 (web UI + CLI, free community edition)
  • Option B: Manual KVM/QEMU on Linux
    • Ubuntu Server 24.04 LTS or Debian 12
    • KVM/QEMU with libvirt (command-line management)
  • Web browser (Safari, Chrome, or Firefox)

References

  • Proxmox VE Documentation
  • Start9 OS Documentation

Step-by-Step Instructions -> Option A: Proxmox Virtual Environment

Proxmox VE is Start9’s recommended hypervisor for running Start9 OS. It provides an enterprise-grade web interface, built-in backup/restore, and is fully open-source.

Step 1: Download Proxmox ISO

  1. On your MacBook, download Proxmox VE 9.1:

    Option 1: Using your browser (Recommended for macOS)

    • Visit the Proxmox VE Downloads page
    • Click on “Proxmox VE 9.1 ISO Installer”
    • Click “Download” button
    • File will be saved to ~/Downloads/proxmox-ve_9.1-1.iso

    Option 2: Using curl (macOS command line)

# Download Proxmox VE (ISO)
cd ~/Downloads
curl -O https://enterprise.proxmox.com/iso/proxmox-ve_9.1-1.iso

# Verify download
ls -lh proxmox-ve_9.1-1.iso
# Expected size: ~1.83 GB
  1. (Optional) Verify the SHA256 checksum of the downloaded ISO:
shasum -a 256 proxmox-ve_9.1-1.iso
# Should match: 6d8f5afc78c0c66812d7272cde7c8b98be7eb54401ceb045400db05eb5ae6d22

Step 2: Flash Proxmox ISO to USB

  1. Flash to USB using Balena Etcher (installed from Flashing Start9 OS):

    • Open Balena Etcher application
    • Click “Flash from file” and select proxmox-ve_9.1-1.iso
    • Click “Select target” and choose your USB drive
      • Warning: This will erase the Start9 OS currently on the USB
      • Use the same USB drive from earlier, or use a different USB if you want to keep both ISOs
    • Click “Flash!” to begin writing Proxmox ISO to USB
    • Wait for flashing to complete (~5-10 minutes)
    • Balena Etcher will verify the flash automatically

Step 3: Install Proxmox on Minisforum

  1. Insert Proxmox USB into Minisforum

  2. Power on the Minisforum

  3. Enter BIOS setup:

    • Press DEL key immediately after power on (before Minisforum logo appears)
    • Look for “Boot” or “Boot Options” menu
    • Set USB as the first boot device (if not already set)
    • Save and exit BIOS

  4. Power off, then power on again - the system will boot from the Proxmox USB

  5. The Proxmox installer boot menu should appear with several options:

    • Install Proxmox VE (Graphical) - Recommended for first-time users (interactive graphical installer; selected and used in this guide)
    • Install Proxmox VE (Terminal UI) - Text-based installer with similar options
    • Advanced Options - For custom partitioning and other settings

  1. Select “Install Proxmox VE (Graphical)” (first option) - this opens the interactive installer

  2. Select the target disk and filesystem

    • Choose the internal SSD as the target harddisk (this will erase all data on it).
    • For Filesystem, select ext4 (recommended for single disk setups).
    • Leave storage and advanced parameters at their default values unless you have specific needs.
    • See below for Filesystem Options:
      • ext4 - Simple, low overhead, no RAID (best for single disk)
      • xfs - High performance, supports RAID0/1/10
      • zfs - Advanced, supports RAID0/1/10/Z1/Z2/Z3
      • btrfs - Modern, supports RAID0/1/10

🖲️ For Your Current Setup (1x 1TB SSD): Select ext4 filesystem for simplest option with lowest resource overhead. No RAID complexity needed for a single SSD.

🛤️ Future Upgrade Paths:

  • Adding 2nd SSD: Use btrfs with RAID1 (mirroring for redundancy)
  • 3+ SSDs at 2TB+: Use ZFS with Z2 (RAID6-like protection with superior data integrity)

Proxmox supports live storage migration (pvmove) if you need to change configurations later.

  1. Set location, time zone, and keyboard layout

    • Select your country, time zone, and keyboard layout as appropriate for your region.

  1. Set the administrator password and email address

    • Enter your email address (used for notifications and system alerts).
    • Create a strong password for the root account and confirm it.

🔑 Password Requirements:

  • Use at least 12 characters
  • Mix uppercase, lowercase, numbers, and symbols
  • Avoid common words or easy patterns
  • Store securely (you’ll need this to access Proxmox web console)

  1. Configure the management network

    • Select the management interface (nic0 is usually the Ethernet port with the cable connected from your router).
    • Enter a hostname (FQDN), e.g., pve.local.
    • Enter a static IPv4 address (e.g., 192.168.1.100/24) or choose DHCP.
    • Enter the gateway (usually your router’s IP, e.g., 192.168.1.254).
    • Enter DNS servers (use defaults or specify custom DNS if desired).

🌐 Network Configuration Tips:

  • The installer may show an IPv6 default; change to IPv4 to match your gateway.
  • For static IP, pick an unused address in your LAN’s range (e.g., 192.168.1.100/24).
  • Default gateway for AT&T BGW320 is 192.168.1.254.
  • Default DNS is pre-filled from AT&T; you can use these or specify your own.
  • See the guide for more details on network configuration and troubleshooting.

  1. Review the summary and start installation

    • Review all configuration values on the summary screen.
    • Confirm that all entries (disk, region, network, etc.) are correct.
    • When ready, click Install. Optionally, leave “Automatically reboot after successful installation” enabled.

  2. Installation takes ~10-15 minutes

  3. System reboots; remove USB drive

    Proxmox VE is now installed on the Minisforum’s internal SSD. The USB drive is no longer needed for booting and can be reused to flash the Start9 OS ISO in the next step.

  4. Reset BIOS boot sequence (optional but recommended)

    To prevent accidental USB boot in the future, set the boot order back to NVMe first:

    • Power off the Minisforum
    • Power on and press DEL immediately to enter BIOS
    • Go to Boot or Boot Options
    • Set NVMe (Proxmox disk) as the first boot device
    • Set USB as second (if you want USB fallback)
    • Save and exit BIOS
    • System will boot directly from Proxmox

  5. Access Proxmox web console:

    From your MacBook browser, navigate to Proxmox Web Console using https://192.168.1.100:8006 or use the hostname https://pve.local:8006

    Login credentials:

    • Username: root
    • Password: The password you created during installation as Proxmox administration password

🌐 Access Tip: You can use the hostname (e.g., https://pve.local:8006) or use IP address (e.g., https://192.168.1.100:8006) you configured for Proxmox earlier. If you used DHCP, check your router’s DHCP leases to find the assigned IP.

Step 4: Enable Proxmox VE No-Subscription Repository (Free Updates)

By default, Proxmox VE shows a subscription warning and only enables the enterprise repository. To receive updates without a paid subscription, enable the no-subscription repository using the GUI method:

  1. Log in to the Proxmox web interface

  2. In the left sidebar, select Datacenter, then your node, then Repositories

  3. Disable the enterprise repository (select the pve-enterprise line and click Disable)

  4. Click Add

  5. Select No-Subscription from the dropdown menu

    • All fields (URIs, Suites, Components) will populate automatically

  6. Click Add to confirm

  7. Go to Updates in the sidebar and click Refresh

  8. Click Upgrade to update from the free repository

    You now have access to free Proxmox VE updates without a paid subscription.

🛠️ Alternative Methods:

  • CLI Method: Use SSH or terminal to edit repository files directly (/etc/apt/sources.list.d/)
  • Manual Edit: Edit repository files via web shell under node → Shell
  • See Proxmox Package Repositories documentation for details

Step 5: Upload Start9 OS ISO to Proxmox

  1. Download Start9 OS stable release ISO:

    • Visit the Start9 OS GitHub Releases page
    • Download latest stable ISO to your MacBook (e.g., startos-0.3.5.1-39de098_x86_64.iso)

  2. In Proxmox web interface, navigate to Datacenter → pve (your node) → local storage

  3. Click ISO Images tab

  4. Click Upload button

  5. Select your Start9 stable ISO file and click Upload

    • Hash algorithm: Leave as None (optional integrity verification; not required for this upload)
    • Wait for upload to complete (~3-5 minutes depending on connection speed)

🔄 USB Transfer Option: Alternatively, flash ISO to USB drive (using your MacBook) and insert USB into Minisforum device running Proxmox to transfer the file

Step 6: Create the Virtual Machine

  1. In the Proxmox web interface, click Create VM (top right) to start the virtual machine creation wizard.
  2. Enter general information for your VM:
    • Set Node to your Proxmox server (e.g., pve).
    • Choose a VM ID (default is 100, or pick another if needed).
    • Enter a Name for your VM (e.g., start9-bitcoin-node).
    • Leave Resource Pool empty (not needed for a single VM).
    • Leave Add to HA unchecked (not needed unless you have a cluster).
  3. Select the Start9 OS ISO and operating system type:
    • In the OS tab, select Use CD/DVD disc image file (iso).
    • Set Storage to local.
    • Choose the ISO image you uploaded earlier (e.g., startos-0.3.5.1-39de098_x86_64.iso).
    • For Guest OS, set Type to Linux and Version to 6.x - 2.6 Kernel (or latest available).
  4. Configure the system hardware for the VM:
    • In the System tab, leave Graphic card, Machine, BIOS, and SCSI Controller at their defaults.
    • Leave Qemu Agent unchecked.
    • No changes needed for other options unless you have specific requirements.
  5. Allocate CPU resources for the VM:
    • In the CPU tab, set Sockets to 1 and Cores to 4 (recommended for a Bitcoin node).
    • Set Type to host to fully utilize your CPU features.
    • Leave Bandwidth at default (unlimited).
  6. Allocate memory for the VM:
    • In the Memory tab, set Memory (MiB) to 6144 (6 GB for Start9 OS).
    • Optionally set Minimum memory to 2048 for ballooning.
  7. Set up the VM’s virtual disk:
    • In the Disks tab, set Bus/Device to SCSI 0 (default).
    • Choose Storage as local-lvm (or your storage pool).
    • Set Disk size (GiB) to 1000 (1TB recommended; minimum 750GB if space is limited).
    • Set Cache to Default (or write through for safety).
    • Leave Discard unchecked.
    • Enable SSD emulation if using an SSD (recommended).
    • Keep I/O thread checked for better performance.
    • Leave Bandwidth at default.
  8. Configure the VM’s network connection:
    • In the Network tab, set Bridge to vmbr0 (default).
    • Set Model to VirtIO for best performance.
    • Leave MAC address as auto-generated.
    • Ensure No network device is unchecked (you need network access).
    • Leave VLAN tag empty.
    • Keep Firewall checked for basic security.
  9. Review and finalize the VM setup:
    • In the Confirm tab, review all your settings.
    • Check Start after created to boot the VM immediately after creation.
  10. Click Finish to create the VM.

Step 7: Install Start9 OS

  1. VM will automatically start and boot from the ISO

  2. Click Console tab to view the VM screen

    • You’ll see “Guest not running” message initially
    • Click Start now button to power on the VM

  3. Follow Start9 OS installation wizard (first stage):

    • Select the installation disk (the virtual disk matching your Disks tab allocation, e.g., 1TB)
    • Select Install StartOS as the installation type (not “Start Fresh”)
    • The system will display unmounting messages (normal during Live ISO eject phase)
    • Press ENTER to continue when prompted

  4. System will automatically reboot after installation (into Start9 Setup):

    • Wait for reboot to complete (~2-3 minutes)

  5. Complete Start9 OS Setup (second stage):

    • On the Start9 OS Setup screen, choose Start Fresh (Recover only if restoring from backup)
    • Select the installation disk again if prompted (same virtual disk as above)
    • Set your master password (enter and re-type to confirm)
    • Continue following the setup wizard prompts

  6. After Start9 OS setup completes:

    • Click Continue to Login when prompted.
    • Log in with the master password you just set.

  7. Remove the ISO:

    • In Proxmox, select your VM (start9-bitcoin-node).
    • Click Hardware tab.
    • Select the CD/DVD Drive.
    • Click Edit.
    • Select Do not use any media.
    • Click OK.
    • Why: Prevents the VM from booting the installer ISO again on future restarts and avoids keeping the live ISO mounted.

  8. Start9 OS is now running on the VM. You can restart the VM later if needed.

💽 VM Data Persistence: Start9 OS data persists in the VM disk; the VM can be stopped/restarted without data loss.

Step 8: Access Start9 OS Web Interface

Quick Access Summary for Proxmox VM:

The VM automatically obtains an IP from your router via DHCP. You can access Start9 OS in two ways:

  1. Via local hostname (easiest): Navigate to https://xxx-yyy.local from any device on your network (replace with your device’s hostname)
  2. Via direct IP: Find the VM’s IP in Proxmox Summary tab, then navigate to https://192.168.1.101 (for example, if your Start9 OS VM is assigned 192.168.1.101)

For ongoing maintenance and best practices, see the Ongoing Management → For Proxmox (Option A) section below.

High-Level Guidance -> Option B: KVM QEMU on Ubuntu Debian
