🖥️ Virtualization Approach: This guide focuses on Proxmox VE (Option A) as the chosen approach. KVM/QEMU on Ubuntu/Debian is an alternative for users who prefer traditional Linux distributions as the host OS.

Considerations for KVM/QEMU approach:

  • Host OS: Requires Ubuntu Server 24.04 LTS or Debian 12 installation
  • Virtualization Stack: Uses KVM/QEMU with libvirt for VM management
  • Management: Primarily CLI-based using virsh commands (less user-friendly than Proxmox web UI)
  • Networking: Requires manual bridge configuration for direct LAN access
  • Resource Overhead: Similar to Proxmox (~2GB for host OS, 6-8GB for Start9 VM)
  • Pros: More familiar for existing Ubuntu/Debian users; native Linux tools
  • Cons: No built-in web UI; requires more manual configuration; less optimized for virtualization

If you prefer this approach, refer to Ubuntu/Debian KVM documentation:

  • Ubuntu KVM Installation Guide
  • Debian KVM Guide

Ongoing Management -> For Proxmox (Option A)

Proxmox Ongoing Management (Future)

  • Snapshots: Use Proxmox snapshots to safely test configuration changes and revert if needed. Take one before upgrades; keep only a few; prune old ones.
  • Backups: Use Proxmox web UI or vzdump command for automated backups. Schedule periodic VM backups to external storage (USB/NAS).
  • Updates: Keep Proxmox and Start9 OS up to date via their UIs.
  • Monitoring: Watch CPU, RAM, and disk; adjust VM resources as needed.
  • Security: Review firewall rules and user permissions on the node.

🌐 Host Services: The Proxmox host can still run other services (firewall, DNS, backup scripts, etc.).

Storage Upgrade Path (Future)

When you’re ready to add redundancy or expand storage:

  • Start with two SSDs in btrfs RAID1 for redundancy and low overhead.
  • Use live storage migration (pvmove) when replacing/expanding disks; consult Proxmox docs.
  • At 3+ SSDs, consider ZFS (Z2) for stronger integrity; budget extra RAM.

👈 Prev: Booting Start9 OS

👉 Next: Configuring Network Settings

Configuring Network Settings (Start9 OS)

This guide provides step-by-step instructions to configure the network connection in Start9 OS to enable internet access and confirm your node has full gigabit connectivity for optimal blockchain synchronization. You’ll learn how to set up your network, verify DHCP IP assignment, confirm gigabit connection speed, run speed tests, and troubleshoot any issues to ensure your node is ready for reliable Bitcoin operation.

In this guide, you will:

  • Set up network access for Start9 OS (native or VM)
  • Access the web interface and find your device
  • Configure static IP, DNS, and advanced options
  • Verify your node is using a wired gigabit connection and check network speed
  • Troubleshoot common network issues

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Complete one of the following installation methods:
    • Booting Start9 OS directly on Minisforum device
    • Virtualized Start9 OS Setup Guide (recommended for hardware compatibility and used in this guide)
    • Alternative installation on Ubuntu/Debian
  • Basic familiarity with web interfaces or Proxmox console
  • Ensure you have access to your AT&T BGW320 router and its admin interface.
  • Gather your Start9 node credentials and device information (IP, device ID).
  • Confirm you have a laptop/desktop on the same network as your Start9 node.
  • Use a wired Ethernet connection (Cat 5e or higher) for your node and verify gigabit connectivity for optimal performance.

Hardware

  • MINISFORUM X1-255 Mini PC (or similar x86_64 hardware)
  • Ethernet connection to AT&T BGW320 router (Cat 5e or higher cable required for gigabit speeds)
  • Monitor and keyboard (native boot) or Proxmox web console (VM)
  • AT&T BGW320 router with DHCP enabled (default)
  • Laptop/desktop on the same network (for speed test comparison)

Software

  • Start9 OS installed (native or VM)
  • Web browser (Safari, Chrome, Firefox, etc.)
  • Access to Proxmox web interface (if using VM)
  • SSH client (for command-line speed checks)
  • speedtest-cli (for bandwidth testing, optional but recommended)

References

  • Start9 OS Documentation – Networking
  • AT&T BGW320 Router Manual

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Access Start9 OS Web Interface

You can access the Start9 OS web interface after installation, whether running as a Proxmox VM or natively on Minisforum hardware.

For Proxmox VM Installation

If you installed Start9 OS as a Proxmox VM, you have two ways to access the web interface:

Option 1: Access via Start9 Hostname (.local)

  1. From your MacBook (or any device on the same network), open a web browser.
  2. Type https://xxx-yyy.local (replace with your device name) directly into the address bar and press Enter.
  3. The browser will connect to your Start9 OS instance on the local network using mDNS/Bonjour.
  4. If the .local address does not work, try your device’s local IP address (e.g., https://192.168.1.101).
  5. Log in with the master password you set during installation.

🔎 Device Name Lookup: In the Start9 web interface, go to System → About → Web Addresses → LAN. The .local address uses your device name (e.g., xxx-yyy.local). For Proxmox VE, use the VM’s hostname as shown in your router’s device list or network settings.

Option 2: Access via Private IP Address

  1. In Proxmox web interface, select your Start9 VM (start9-bitcoin-node)
  2. Click Summary tab to view VM details
  3. Note the IP Address (e.g., 192.168.1.101 if you configured static IP)
    • Or check the Proxmox console for the IP address displayed on the Start9 login screen
  4. From your MacBook (or any device on the same network), open a web browser
  5. Navigate to https://192.168.1.101 (replace with your actual IP address)
  6. Accept the self-signed certificate warning:
    • Safari: Click “Show Details” → “visit this website”
    • Chrome: Type thisisunsafe (no spaces) on the warning page
    • Firefox: Click “Advanced” → “Accept the Risk and Continue”
  7. Log in with the master password you set during installation

Network Configuration in Proxmox VM:

  • The VM automatically obtains an IP via DHCP from your router
  • The Proxmox bridge (vmbr0) connects the VM to your LAN
  • No additional network configuration needed - Start9 OS handles this automatically
  • The VM appears as a regular device on your network (192.168.1.x range)

For Native Boot Installation

If you’re running Start9 OS directly on the Minisforum hardware (native boot), simply follow the on-screen setup wizard after first boot. Select a wired Ethernet connection for best reliability and performance, and complete the guided steps to finish network setup and access the web interface.

Step 2: Verify Automatic Configuration

📡 DHCP: The BGW320 router handles IP address assignment automatically via DHCP, so no manual network configuration is typically needed.

The BGW320 router automatically assigns network settings via DHCP:

  1. Start9 OS will attempt to obtain an IP address automatically
  2. Wait a few seconds for DHCP negotiation to complete
  3. The system will display the assigned IP address (typically in the 192.168.1.x range)
  4. Confirm the connection status shows as “Connected” or “Active”

Where to find your Start9 OS IP address:

  • In the Start9 web interface, go to System → About and look for the ens18 (IPv4) entry. This shows your device’s current IP address on the network.

Example IP assignment:

  • IP Address: 192.168.1.101
  • Subnet Mask: 255.255.255.0
  • Gateway: 192.168.1.254 (BGW320 router)
  • DNS Servers: Automatically provided by router

🧩 IP Assignment Help: If no IP address is assigned:

  • Verify the Ethernet cable is securely connected at both ends
  • Check that the connection LEDs are lit on both Mini PC and router
  • Ensure the BGW320 router has available DHCP addresses
  • Try unplugging and replugging the Ethernet cable

Step 3: Confirm Wired Gigabit Connection

  1. Ensure your Start9 node is connected via wired Ethernet (not Wi-Fi)
  2. Use a Cat 5e or higher Ethernet cable
  3. Plug into a gigabit port on the BGW320 router
  4. Check the router’s device list for your node (should show 1000 Mbps or 1 Gbps)
  5. If using a VM, check the Proxmox summary for the VM’s network interface

📊 Network Speed Insights:

  • Initial blockchain sync for Bitcoin Core can take days. Gigabit speed reduces this from weeks (at 100 Mbps) to days (at 1 Gbps).
  • 900–950 Mbps is excellent; protocol overhead and network conditions mean you’ll rarely see exactly 1000 Mbps.
  • Upload speed is important for serving blocks; fiber connections (like AT&T Fiber 1 GIG) provide symmetric speeds.
  • Consistent connection is more important than absolute max speed. A stable 500 Mbps is better than a flaky 1000 Mbps.
  • Streaming or large downloads on your network may impact speed test results. Run tests when the network is idle for best results.

Step 4: Check Speed via Router Admin Interface

  1. Open your AT&T BGW320 router admin page (192.168.1.254)
  2. Go to Device → Status
  3. Find your Start9 device in the list (labeled “start” or “start9”)
  4. Click More Info next to “Home Network Devices”
  5. Select Device → Device List to view connection details
  6. Check the connection details (type, speed, IP address, etc.)

🌐 Network Verification & Bandwidth:

  • The AT&T router device list is the most reliable way to verify your Start9 node’s network connection.
  • Check if your ISP has data caps. Running a Bitcoin node uses significant bandwidth (20-50 GB/month for pruned, 200+ GB/month for full archival).

Expected results:

  • Type: Ethernet (wired connection)
  • Speed: 1000 Mbps or 1 Gbps (gigabit connection)
  • IP Address: Should match your Start9 node (e.g., 192.168.1.101)
  • Status: Connected

🔄 Ongoing Monitoring: After setup, your node will maintain connections to other nodes. Gigabit speed ensures you can serve blocks and relay transactions efficiently.

Alternative Methods for Speed Verification

If you want to check your network speed using other methods, here are some high-level options:

  • Start9 Web Interface: View network status and interface details in the dashboard.
  • Command Line (SSH): Use tools like ethtool or speedtest-cli to check link speed and run bandwidth tests.
  • External Speed Test (Laptop/Desktop): Run a speed test from another device on the same network for comparison.

For most users, the router device list and Start9 dashboard provide sufficient confirmation of gigabit connectivity. Use these alternatives only if you need additional verification or troubleshooting.

🆘 Need Help? See Troubleshooting Common Speed Issues below for common issues and solutions.

Troubleshooting Common Speed Issues

✅ Optimal (900–1000 Mbps):

  • Connection is optimal for Bitcoin node operation
  • Proceed with confidence to Bitcoin Core installation
  • Your node will sync and relay efficiently

⚠️ 100 Mbps Detected:

  • Connection negotiated at Fast Ethernet instead of Gigabit
  • Check cable quality (use Cat 5e or higher)
  • Try a different LAN port on the BGW320
  • Verify Mini PC Ethernet port isn’t damaged

⚠️ Below 100 Mbps:

  • Potential cable damage or poor connection
  • Unplug and replug both ends of Ethernet cable
  • Try a different cable
  • Check for physical damage to ports

⚠️ Speeds Vary Significantly:

  • Network congestion or other devices consuming bandwidth
  • Test during off-peak hours
  • Check router for bandwidth-heavy devices
  • Consider QoS (Quality of Service) settings on router

👈 Prev: Virtualized Start9 OS Setup Guide

👉 Next: Securing Proxmox VE

Securing Proxmox Virtual Environment (VE)

This guide provides step-by-step instructions to secure your Proxmox VE host before running Start9 OS or Bitcoin node VMs. Securing the hypervisor is essential to protect your Bitcoin infrastructure and all hosted services from unauthorized access and network threats.

In this guide, you will:

  • Harden Proxmox VE authentication and access controls
  • Configure firewall and network security
  • Set up user management and SSH best practices
  • Review maintenance and advanced security options

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Access to your Proxmox VE host (IP address, credentials)
  • Ability to SSH into the Proxmox server from your MacBook or workstation
  • Familiarity with basic Linux commands

Hardware

  • Proxmox VE host machine (physical or virtualized)
  • Reliable network connection between your MacBook and Proxmox host

Software

  • Proxmox VE installed and accessible via web interface (e.g., https://192.168.1.100:8006)
  • SSH client (Terminal app on macOS)
  • Password manager for storing credentials securely

References

  • Proxmox Official Documentation
  • Proxmox Firewall Documentation
  • Proxmox User Management
  • Linux SSH Security Best Practices

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Update Proxmox VE

Before making configuration changes, update the Proxmox host to the latest security patches.

  1. SSH into the Proxmox host:

    ssh root@192.168.1.100

  2. Update package lists and upgrade:

    apt update && apt dist-upgrade -y

  3. Reboot if the kernel or critical packages were upgraded:

    reboot

📦 Repository Guidance: If you use the Proxmox enterprise repository, ensure your subscription is valid. Non‑enterprise users can enable the no‑subscription repo following Proxmox guidance.

Step 2: Change Proxmox Root Password

The default root password is set during installation. Change it immediately to a strong, unique password.

  1. Access Proxmox web interface: https://192.168.1.100:8006
  2. Log in as root with your initial password
  3. Click on Datacenter → Permissions → Users (or top-right profile menu)
  4. Find root@pam in the user list
  5. Click the Password button for the root user
  6. In the pop-up, enter and confirm a strong, unique password (20+ characters, mix of case/numbers/symbols)
  7. Click OK to save

🔑 Password Recommendations:

  • Minimum 20 characters
  • Mix uppercase, lowercase, numbers, special characters
  • Use passphrase format (e.g., CorrectHorseBatteryStaple!2025)
  • Store securely in password manager

Step 3: Configure SSH Key-Based Authentication

Set up SSH keys for secure, password-less access to Proxmox:

  1. Check for Existing SSH Key

    First, check if you already have an SSH key pair on your MacBook:

    ls -la ~/.ssh/

    If you see an Ed25519 key pair, you can reuse it and proceed directly to next step to copy your existing public key to Proxmox:

    • id_ed25519 (private key)
    • id_ed25519.pub (public key)

    If you don’t see an Ed25519 key pair, generate a new one:

    ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "proxmox@192.168.1.100" -f ~/.ssh/id_proxmox
    • When prompted for passphrase, enter a strong password (or leave blank for no passphrase)
    • File will be saved to ~/.ssh/id_proxmox
    • Verify the key was created:
    ls -la ~/.ssh/id_proxmox*
# Should show: id_proxmox (private key) and id_proxmox.pub (public key)

  2. Copy SSH Public Key to Proxmox Root

    • Determine which public key to use:

      • If using existing Ed25519 key: ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub
      • If using new key: ~/.ssh/id_proxmox.pub

    • SSH to Proxmox with password (one last time):

    ssh root@192.168.1.100
    • Create SSH directory if it doesn’t exist:
    mkdir -p /root/.ssh
chmod 700 /root/.ssh
    • On your MacBook (in a new terminal), copy your public key:
    # If using existing id_ed25519.pub:
cat ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub | ssh root@192.168.1.100 "cat >> /root/.ssh/authorized_keys"

# OR if using new id_proxmox.pub:
cat ~/.ssh/id_proxmox.pub | ssh root@192.168.1.100 "cat >> /root/.ssh/authorized_keys"
    • Set correct permissions on Proxmox:
    ssh root@192.168.1.100
chmod 600 /root/.ssh/authorized_keys
    • Verify the key was added:
    ssh root@192.168.1.100
cat /root/.ssh/authorized_keys
# Should show your public key starting with "ssh-ed25519"

  3. Test SSH Key Access

    From your MacBook, test SSH with the key:

    # If using existing id_ed25519:
ssh root@192.168.1.100
# SSH should automatically use ~/.ssh/id_ed25519

# OR if using id_proxmox:
ssh -i ~/.ssh/id_proxmox root@192.168.1.100

    Should log in without asking for password (unless you set a passphrase on the key).

    If prompted for a passphrase (not password), enter the passphrase you set when creating the key.

🔐 SSH Agent Tip: Add the key to SSH agent to avoid entering passphrase repeatedly.

# If using id_ed25519:
ssh-add ~/.ssh/id_ed25519

# OR if using id_proxmox:
ssh-add ~/.ssh/id_proxmox

# Now SSH will use the cached key without asking for passphrase

Step 4: Disable SSH Root Login

After confirming SSH key access works, disable password authentication:

  1. SSH into Proxmox host:

    # If using existing id_ed25519 (it auto-connects):
ssh root@192.168.1.100

# OR if using id_proxmox:
ssh -i ~/.ssh/id_proxmox root@192.168.1.100

  2. Edit SSH configuration:

    nano /etc/ssh/sshd_config

  3. Find and modify these lines:

    PermitRootLogin prohibit-password
PasswordAuthentication no
PubkeyAuthentication yes

    Explanation:

    • PermitRootLogin prohibit-password: Allow root login only with SSH keys, not passwords
    • PasswordAuthentication no: Disable password-based authentication entirely
    • PubkeyAuthentication yes: Enable SSH key authentication

  4. Save the file (Ctrl+O, Enter, Ctrl+X)

  5. Restart SSH service:

    systemctl restart ssh

  6. Important: Keep your current SSH session open and test from a new terminal:

    # Test with explicit key path (works even if path is wrong - SSH tries default keys):
ssh -i ~/.ssh/id_proxmox root@192.168.1.100
# Should work

# Test with SSH agent (auto-uses available keys):
ssh root@192.168.1.100
# Should work (uses default key or SSH agent)

# Test that password auth is disabled:
ssh -o PubkeyAuthentication=no root@192.168.1.100
# Should fail (no password prompt, connection refused)

🔑 SSH Key Path Tip: SSH is forgiving with key paths - if you specify -i ~/.ssh/ed25519 but the key doesn’t exist there, SSH will automatically try default key locations like ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 or keys in your SSH agent. This is why both -i ~/.ssh/ed25519 and -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 work!

Security Benefits:

  • No brute-force password attacks possible
  • SSH key is stored locally on your MacBook
  • Only devices with the private key can access Proxmox

Step 5: Configure Proxmox Firewall

Enable the host firewall to restrict traffic:

  1. In Proxmox web interface, go to Datacenter → Firewall
  2. Enable IN policy: Set to DROP (deny all inbound by default)
  3. Enable OUT policy: Set to ACCEPT (allow all outbound)
  4. Click Save

Step 6: Add Firewall Rules for Management Access

Allow only necessary ports:

Management Access:

  • Port 8006 (HTTPS web interface) - from your home network

Important: Proxmox firewall rules are organized in Security Groups. You must create a Security Group first, then add rules to it.

  1. Create a Security Group

    • Go to Datacenter → Firewall → Security Group
    • Click Create button
    • Enter the following:
      • Name: Management-Access (or similar name)
      • Comment: Allow management traffic for Proxmox host (optional but recommended)
    • Click Create

  1. Add Firewall Rules to Security Group

    • With your new Security Group selected, click Add Rule
    • Create the following ACCEPT rules (in order):

Important: Add rules in this order and test each before proceeding!

Rule 1 - Proxmox Web Interface:
- Enable: ☑ (checked)
- Direction: In
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Dest Port: 8006
- Source: 192.168.1.0/24 (your home network)
- Macro: (leave blank)
- Comment: "Proxmox web interface (Datacenter)"

Rule 2 - SSH Access:
- Enable: ☑ (checked)
- Direction: In
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Dest Port: 22
- Source: 192.168.1.0/24 (your home network)
- Macro: (leave blank)
- Comment: "SSH key-based access"

  1. Insert Security Group into Datacenter Firewall

    • Go to Datacenter → Firewall
    • Click the Insert: Security Group button
    • Select your created Security Group (e.g., Management-Access)
    • Click Add to add the group to the Datacenter firewall rules
  1. Test ACCEPT rules
    • After adding Rules 1 and 2 and inserting the Security Group, verify you can still access:
      • Proxmox web interface: https://192.168.1.100:8006
      • SSH: ssh root@192.168.1.100
    • If access works, you’re done! The DROP Input Policy handles the rest.
    • If access fails, troubleshoot ACCEPT rules (check Enable is ☑, verify port numbers, confirm Security Group is inserted)

About the default DENY behavior:

You don’t need to add a specific DENY rule because you already configured Input Policy = DROP in Step 4 (Datacenter → Firewall → Options). Here’s how this works:

  • DROP policy: By default, ALL inbound traffic is silently discarded (no response sent)
  • ACCEPT rules: Create exceptions to the DROP policy for ports 8006 and 22
  • Result: Only ports you explicitly ACCEPT are allowed; everything else is dropped

DROP vs REJECT:

  • DROP: Silently discards packets (no response to sender). Recommended for security - attackers don’t know if port exists.
  • REJECT: Sends ICMP error back to sender. Lets attackers know port exists but is blocked. Not recommended for external interfaces.

About Enable checkbox and Macro:

  • Enable: Must be checked (☑) for rule to be active
  • Macro: Provides predefined rule templates (e.g., “HTTP”, “HTTPS”, “SSH”). Leave blank for custom rules.

Order matters: Rules are evaluated top-to-bottom. ACCEPT rules match first, then DROP policy catches everything else.

Step 7: Enable the Firewall

🛡️ Firewall Activation Required: The firewall configuration doesn’t take effect until you enable it!

  1. Go to Datacenter → Firewall → Options
  2. Find Firewall setting (at the top of the options list)
  3. Double-click on the Firewall row to edit it
  4. Check the box to enable the firewall
  5. Click OK

What happens when you enable:

  • Your ACCEPT rules for ports 8006 and 22 will allow access
  • All other inbound traffic will be DROPped (silently discarded)
  • You should still be able to access web UI and SSH (if rules are correct)

Safety check after enabling:

  • Verify web interface still works: https://192.168.1.100:8006
  • Verify SSH still works: ssh root@192.168.1.100
  • If you lose access, use Proxmox console to disable firewall: pve-firewall stop

Step 8: Review User Accounts

Proxmox supports multiple user accounts. You can manage them for team collaboration:

🗝️ Solo Operator Advice: For solo Bitcoin node operators, the root account with SSH keys is all you need.

  1. Go to Datacenter → Permissions → Users (or Datacenter → User Management in older versions)
  2. Review all users in the list
  3. For Bitcoin node setups, the root account is sufficient
  4. Remove any default or test accounts if you want to minimize attack surface

👤 User Account Setup Tip: Recommended setup:

  • One admin account (root) with strong SSH key authentication
  • Use SSH key-based auth only (no password login)
  • Skip additional users unless you need team collaboration

👈 Prev: Configuring Network Settings

👉 Next: Securing the Start9 VM with Proxmox Firewall Rules

Securing the Start9 VM with Proxmox Firewall Rules

The Proxmox datacenter firewall you configured in the previous guide protects the hypervisor host itself. The Start9 VM needs its own separate VM-level firewall — one that controls exactly which traffic can reach the Bitcoin node and which outbound connections the VM is allowed to make.

Without these rules the Start9 VM sits on the same LAN bridge as the AI agents VM and any other future VM, fully reachable from all of them. Locking it down at the VM level means that even if another VM were compromised, it cannot reach your Bitcoin node, your Lightning wallet, or your Electrs index.

In this guide, you will:

  • Enable the Proxmox VM-level firewall for the Start9 VM
  • Apply an inbound DROP-default policy allowing SSH and web access from your MacBook, wallet connections, and Bitcoin P2P ports
  • Apply an outbound DROP-default policy restricting egress to Bitcoin peers, Tor, updates, and the Proxmox backup share
  • Verify isolation from the AI agents VM before proceeding to software setup

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Proxmox VE secured following Securing Proxmox Virtual Environment
  • Start9 OS VM running and accessible from your MacBook browser
  • Your MacBook’s static LAN IP (check with ipconfig getifaddr en0 in Terminal)
  • The AI agents VM LAN IP, if already provisioned (assigned during Provisioning the AI VM)

Hardware

  • Minisforum X1-255 running Proxmox VE
  • Start9 VM running normally (you will verify isolation after applying rules)

Software

  • Proxmox VE web interface (Safari, Chrome, or Firefox)
  • SSH client (Terminal on macOS)

References

  • Proxmox VE Firewall Documentation

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Enable the VM-Level Firewall

  1. In the Proxmox web interface, select your Start9 VM from the left sidebar

  2. Click the Firewall tab → Options

  3. Double-click Firewall → set to Yes

  4. Set default policies:

    • Double-click Input policy → set to DROP
    • Double-click Output policy → set to ACCEPT (temporarily — you will restrict this in Step 3)

🛡️ Two-layer firewall: The Proxmox datacenter firewall protects the Proxmox host (ports 8006 and 22). These VM-level rules are separate — they protect the Start9 VM’s network boundary. Both layers must be active and each operates independently.

Step 2: Add Inbound Rules

Click the Firewall tab → Add to create each rule below in order.

🔑 Enable SSH inside Start9 first: The firewall rule opens the network path, but Start9 OS does not run SSH by default. Before testing SSH, go to the Start9 web interface → System → Manage → SSH → + Add New Key and paste your MacBook’s public key (pbcopy < ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub). Both steps are required.

Rule 1 — SSH from MacBook:
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: in
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Source: 192.168.1.XXX   ← your MacBook's static LAN IP
- Dest. port: 22
- Comment: SSH access from MacBook

Rule 2 — Start9 web UI (HTTPS) from MacBook:
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: in
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Source: 192.168.1.XXX   ← your MacBook's static LAN IP
- Dest. port: 443
- Comment: Start9 web UI + all service UIs (Mempool, BTCPay, Nostr, Vaultwarden, etc.)

Rule 3 — Start9 web UI (HTTP redirect) from MacBook:
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: in
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Source: 192.168.1.XXX
- Dest. port: 80
- Comment: HTTP → HTTPS redirect for Start9 UI

Rule 4 — Electrs Electrum protocol from MacBook:
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: in
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Source: 192.168.1.XXX
- Dest. port: 50001
- Comment: Sparrow / Electrum wallet connections (unencrypted Electrum protocol)

Rule 5 — Electrs TLS from MacBook:
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: in
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Source: 192.168.1.XXX
- Dest. port: 50002
- Comment: Sparrow / Electrum wallet connections (TLS)

Rule 6 — Bitcoin Core P2P (inbound peers):
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: in
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Source: (leave blank — any)
- Dest. port: 8333
- Comment: Bitcoin Core inbound peer connections — only reachable after router port forwarding is configured

Rule 7 — Lightning Network P2P (inbound channels):
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: in
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Source: (leave blank — any)
- Dest. port: 9735
- Comment: Lightning P2P inbound channels — only reachable after router port forwarding is configured

Rule 8 — mDNS (local hostname resolution):
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: in
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: udp
- Source: (leave blank)
- Dest. port: 5353
- Comment: mDNS — MacBook resolves xxx-yyy.local hostnames for service UIs

  1. Verify the Start9 web interface is still reachable from your MacBook after enabling the firewall:

    • Open your browser and load https://192.168.1.XXX (Start9’s LAN IP)
    • The Start9 login page must load — if it does not, temporarily disable the VM firewall in Options → Firewall → No, fix the Source IP in Rule 2, and re-enable

🌐 Port 443 covers the Start9 admin panel. The Start9 management interface — and services that tunnel entirely through Start9’s HTTPS reverse proxy — are served at port 443, covered by Rule 2. However, some marketplace services (including Mempool Explorer) also expose their own web UI on a separate LAN port. If clicking “Launch UI” for a service opens a URL on a port other than 443, you need an additional inbound rule for that port from your MacBook’s IP. See the Troubleshooting section for details.

Tor .onion addresses for each service are handled entirely inside Start9’s Tor process, which connects to the Tor network through standard HTTPS ports. No inbound firewall rules are needed for Tor hidden services.

🛑 Bitcoin Core RPC port 8332 is intentionally absent from inbound rules. It is consumed only by other Start9 services internally (Electrs, Mempool, BTCPay). Exposing it on the LAN would allow any device to issue arbitrary RPC commands to your node.

📡 Rules 6 and 7 (ports 8333, 9735) have no effect yet. They only become reachable from the internet after you configure port forwarding on the AT&T BGW320 router pointing those ports at the Start9 VM’s LAN IP. Adding the rules now means the VM firewall is ready when you reach that step — you will not need to return here.

Step 3: Switch to Outbound DROP and Add Egress Rules

Now restrict outbound traffic so the Start9 VM can only reach what it legitimately needs.

  1. In Firewall → Options, double-click Output policy → set to DROP

  2. Click Add and create each outbound rule below:

Rule 9 — Block all traffic to AI VM (explicit isolation):
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: out
- Action: DROP
- Protocol: tcp
- Destination: 192.168.1.YYY   ← AI agents VM LAN IP
- Dest. port: (leave blank — all ports)
- Comment: Prevent Start9 from reaching AI VM

Rule 10 — DNS:
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: out
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: udp
- Dest. port: 53
- Comment: DNS resolution

Rule 11 — mDNS (local hostname advertisement):
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: out
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: udp
- Dest. port: 5353
- Comment: mDNS — Start9 advertises its xxx-yyy.local hostname on the LAN

Rule 12 — NTP (clock sync):
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: out
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: udp
- Dest. port: 123
- Comment: System clock sync

Rule 13 — Internet HTTP/HTTPS (updates, Tor, marketplace):
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: out
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Dest. port: 80,443
- Comment: Start9 marketplace, system updates, Tor bootstrap (Tor uses standard HTTPS ports)

Rule 14 — Bitcoin Core P2P outbound:
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: out
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Dest. port: 8333
- Comment: Bitcoin Core connecting to peers

Rule 15 — Lightning Network P2P outbound:
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: out
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Dest. port: 9735
- Comment: Lightning outbound channel and payment routing

Rule 16 — SMB backup to Proxmox host:
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: out
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Destination: 192.168.1.100   ← Proxmox host IP
- Dest. port: 445
- Comment: Start9 → Proxmox Samba share for encrypted backups

Rule 17 — Tor OR port (add only if Tor relay/bridge service is installed):
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: out
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Dest. port: 9001
- Comment: Tor relay OR port — skip this rule unless you install the Start9 Tor Bridge or Relay service

🛡️ Rule ordering matters: Rule 9 (AI VM DROP) must appear above Rule 13 (broad ACCEPT for 80/443) in the rule list. Proxmox evaluates rules top-to-bottom and the first match wins, so the explicit DROP for the AI VM destination must fire before the broad port ACCEPT could match it. All other LAN devices are blocked by the output DROP default — no explicit DROP rules are needed for them.

🔒 Proxmox host access is locked down too: Rule 16 allows only port 445 (SMB) to the Proxmox host (192.168.1.100). All other ports to the Proxmox management interface — including port 8006 (web UI) — are blocked by the output DROP default. The Start9 VM has no legitimate reason to initiate connections to the Proxmox management interface.

Step 4: Verify Isolation

SSH into the Start9 VM (or open Start9’s web console) and run these checks:

# Confirm Start9 cannot reach the AI VM — should time out
curl --connect-timeout 5 http://192.168.1.YYY:11434
# Expected: curl: (28) Connection timed out

# Confirm internet access still works (required for blockchain sync and marketplace)
curl -s https://bitcoin.org | head -5
# Expected: HTML content returned

# Confirm backup path to Proxmox host is open
timeout 5 bash -c 'echo >/dev/tcp/192.168.1.100/445' 2>/dev/null && echo "Port open" || echo "Port blocked"
# Expected: Port open

From your MacBook, confirm .local hostname resolution works (required for service UIs):

ping -c 3 xxx-yyy.local
# Expected: replies from 192.168.1.101

Future Services — Additional Ports

As you install additional Start9 marketplace apps, add these inbound rules from your MacBook’s IP when needed:

ServiceDirectionProtocolPortWhen to add
Syncthingouttcp/udp22000When installing Syncthing for encrypted file sync
Syncthing discoveryoutudp21027Together with the Syncthing rule above

All other planned services (Nextcloud, Vaultwarden, Matrix, XMPP) are served through Start9’s port 443 HTTPS proxy and require no additional firewall rules.

Troubleshooting

Cannot SSH to the Start9 VM (connection timed out):

  • The firewall is dropping port 22. Verify Rule 1 is enabled (☑), Source IP matches your MacBook, and Dest. port is 22. Also confirm SSH is enabled inside Start9 OS: System → Manage → SSH — if no key is listed, add your public key there before retrying.

Start9 web interface unreachable after enabling VM firewall:

  • Verify Rule 2 is enabled (☑), the Source IP exactly matches your MacBook’s LAN IP, and port is 443. Temporarily disable the firewall in Options → Firewall → No, fix the Source IP, re-enable.

Bitcoin Core shows 0 inbound peers:

  • This is expected without router port forwarding. Start9 connects to peers outbound via Rule 14. Inbound peers via Rule 6 only apply after you configure the AT&T BGW320 to forward port 8333 to the Start9 VM’s LAN IP.

Start9 backup to Proxmox Samba share fails after adding outbound DROP:

  • Verify Rule 16 is enabled and Destination is exactly 192.168.1.100. Test from the Start9 VM: timeout 5 bash -c 'echo >/dev/tcp/192.168.1.100/445' 2>/dev/null && echo "Port open" || echo "Port blocked" — should print Port open. If it prints Port blocked, check Rule 16’s destination and port.

Sparrow or Electrum wallet cannot connect to Electrs:

  • Verify Rules 4 and 5 are enabled with the correct Source IP (your MacBook’s LAN IP). Confirm Electrs is running and fully indexed in the Start9 web UI before testing wallet connections.

Service “Launch UI” opens https://xxx-yyy.local but the page does not load (ping xxx-yyy.local fails):

  • Start9 may not be running avahi-daemon, so it never broadcasts its .local hostname via mDNS. The reliable fix is a static hosts entry on your MacBook — add it once and it persists across reboots:

    echo "192.168.1.101 xxx-yyy.local" | sudo tee -a /etc/hosts

    Replace xxx-yyy.local with your actual Start9 hostname. macOS checks /etc/hosts before mDNS for .local names. After adding the entry, ping xxx-yyy.local should resolve to 192.168.1.101 and all service UIs will load.

  • If ping resolves but the browser still shows a certificate error, install the Start9 Root CA in your MacBook’s Keychain — see Securing Start9 OS — Step 3.

A service’s “Launch UI” button opens a blank or timed-out page:

  • The service web UI is served on a port that is not in the inbound rules. Look at the URL in the failed browser tab — it will show the port number (e.g., http://192.168.1.101:4080). Add an inbound rule for that port with your MacBook’s LAN IP as the source. See Installing Mempool Explorer — Troubleshooting for a worked example.
  • The Start9 admin panel (port 443) and individual service web UIs can run on different ports. Rule 2 covers the admin panel and proxied services, but not services that expose a dedicated LAN port.

👈 Prev: Securing Proxmox Virtual Environment

👉 Next: Securing the AT&T BGW320 Router

Securing AT&T BGW320 Router

This guide provides step-by-step instructions to secure your AT&T BGW320 router for use with Proxmox VE, Start9 OS, and Bitcoin node applications. Following these steps will help protect your infrastructure from unauthorized access and network threats.

In this guide, you will:

  • Change default admin credentials
  • Enable firewall and secure access controls
  • Configure static IP reservations (for Proxmox and Start9)
  • Disable unnecessary services (UPnP, remote management)
  • Set up port forwarding (only when needed for Bitcoin node)
  • Configure guest network isolation (optional)

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Ensure you have physical access to your AT&T BGW320 router and its admin label.
  • Gather admin credentials (Access Code, Wi-Fi name, Wi-Fi password).
  • Confirm you have access to a device (laptop/desktop) connected to the router’s network.

Hardware

  • AT&T BGW320 router (with admin access)
  • Minisforum X1-255 (or similar x86_64 hardware) for Proxmox VE

Software

  • Proxmox VE installed on your hardware
  • Start9 OS running (either natively or as a VM)

References

  • AT&T BGW320 Manual
  • Bitcoin Core Port Forwarding
  • Network Security Best Practices (NIST)

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Review Router Label Credentials

Your AT&T BGW320 router label contains three important credentials:

CredentialPurposeSecurity Recommendation
Access CodeRouter admin interface login✅ Keep default (random, unique per router) if physically secure
Wi-Fi NameWireless network SSID⚠️ Change (default name reveals router model and AT&T customer)
Wi-Fi PasswordWireless network password⚠️ Change (default may be shorter and less secure)

🔒 Physical Security: If your router is in a locked cabinet/room with no visitor access, the default credentials are reasonably secure. If service technicians, guests, or roommates have access, change all credentials.

Step 2: Access Router Admin Interface

  1. Open your web browser
  2. Navigate to http://192.168.1.254 (default gateway)

🌐 Admin Page Access Tip: Make sure you are connected to the local network (not cellular/mobile data). If you cannot access the admin page, try the alternate gateway address http://192.168.1.1, reset your browser cache or use incognito mode, and check your Ethernet connection to the router.

  1. The router home page loads without requiring authentication

🔑 Access Control: The AT&T BGW320 allows browsing basic status pages without authentication, but certain settings require the Access Code (from the router label) to modify. This provides reasonable security while allowing easy monitoring.

Step 3: Change Access Code

Should you change the default Access Code?

The printed Access Code on your AT&T BGW320 is randomly generated and unique to your router, making it reasonably secure if:

  • Your router is physically secured in your home
  • You trust household members and visitors won’t access the label
  • You haven’t shared the code publicly

Consider changing it if:

  • You’ve shared photos of your router online (showing the label)
  • You have concerns about physical access by service technicians or guests
  • You prefer rotating credentials as a security best practice

To change the Access Code (if already set):

  1. Go to Device → Access Code
  2. Enter current Access Code
  3. Enter a new Access Code:
    • At least 10-16 characters
    • Mix of uppercase, lowercase, numbers, and symbols
    • Use a password manager to generate and store it
  4. Click Save

🔑 Access Code Security & Recovery: Store your Access Code securely. If you lose or forget it, you must factory reset the router (hold the reset button for 10+ seconds) and reconfigure all settings from this guide. This will disconnect all devices and require you to set up your network again.

Step 4: Configure Static IP Reservations

📡 Static IPs: Ensures your Proxmox host and Start9 node always get the same IP addresses.

On the AT&T BGW320, you can only reserve IP addresses for devices within the DHCP range (192.168.1.64 to 192.168.1.253). Static IPs outside this range cannot be assigned via the web interface.

  1. Go to Device → Status
  2. Find your Proxmox host (Minisforum) and Start9 VM in the device list
  3. Note its MAC address and current IP
  4. Go to Home Network → IP Allocation
  5. Click Add or Allocate
  6. Configure Proxmox reserved IP:
    • Device Name: proxmox-minisforum
    • MAC Address: (copied from device list)
    • IP Address: 192.168.1.100 (choose from 192.168.1.64 to 192.168.1.253)
    • Click Save
  7. Repeat for Start9 VM:
    • Device Name: start9-bitcoin-node
    • MAC Address: (find VM in device list)
    • IP Address: 192.168.1.101 (choose from 192.168.1.64 to 192.168.1.253)
    • Click Save

📡 BGW320 DHCP Reservation Tips:

  • The BGW320 only allows IP reservations within the DHCP range (192.168.1.64 to 192.168.1.253). Pick an address in this range for each device you want to reserve.
  • Reserved addresses: 192.168.1.1 (router), 192.168.1.254 (gateway), 192.168.1.255 (broadcast)
  • Double-check that the device MAC address matches the reservation.
  • After reserving, reboot the device to obtain a new DHCP lease.
  • Do not assign duplicate IPs or use reserved addresses for other devices.
  • If you need a static IP outside the DHCP range, set it manually on the device and ensure it does not conflict with any reserved or DHCP-assigned addresses.

Step 5: Enable Firewall and Access Controls

🛡️ Firewall Protection: Protects your network from unauthorized access and attacks.

Based on your AT&T BGW320, verify these firewall settings:

  1. Go to Firewall tab at the top navigation
  2. Click Status to view current firewall configuration
  3. Verify the following are enabled:
    • Packet Filter: On (should already be on)
    • Firewall Advanced: On (recommended)
    • NAT Default Server: Off (unless you need DMZ - not recommended)
    • IP Passthrough: Off (unless specifically needed)

🛡️ Default Protection: For standard home and node use, also leave the following sub-menus at their default settings (usually “Off” or “Disabled”) unless you have a specific need: Packet Filter, NAT/Gaming, Public Subnet Hosts, IP Passthrough, Firewall Advanced, and Security Options. Changing these options is not recommended for most users and can reduce security.

Configure Advanced Firewall Rules (Optional)

If you need custom firewall rules:

  1. Go to Firewall → Firewall Advanced
  2. Suggested secure defaults:
    • Drop incoming ICMP Echo requests to LAN: Off (keep local ping working; turn On if you want to drop WAN-originated pings to LAN)
    • Drop incoming ICMP Echo requests to Device LAN Address: On (blocks ping to the router LAN IP from WAN/IPv6)
    • Drop incoming ICMP Echo requests to Device WAN Address: On (blocks ping to the router WAN IP)
    • Reflexive ACL: On (stateful inspection; keep enabled)
    • ESP ALG: Off (enable only if you run IPsec passthrough)
    • SIP ALG: On (default; generally safe, but if VoIP issues arise, try turning this Off)

Step 6: Change Wi-Fi Network Name and Password

📶 Wi-Fi Security: Default Wi-Fi credentials reveal your router model and may be less secure.

  1. Go to Home Network → Wi-Fi (or 2.4 GHz Settings / 5 GHz Settings)
  2. Change the Network Name (SSID):
    • Choose a unique name that doesn’t reveal personal information
    • Avoid using your name, address, or “ATT” in the SSID
    • Example: HomeNetwork2025 instead of ATT-Wi-Fi-ABCD
  3. Change the Wi-Fi Password:
    • Create a strong passphrase (20+ characters recommended)
    • Mix of uppercase, lowercase, numbers, and symbols
    • Use a password manager to generate and store it
    • Example: Correct-Horse-Battery-Staple-2025!
  4. Verify Security settings:
    • Security: WPA (default)
    • WPA Version: WPA-2 (default; sufficient security for most users)

🔐 Wi-Fi Encryption: WPA-3 may not be available on BGW320. WPA-2 is secure when using a strong password (20+ characters)

  1. SSID Broadcast: Keep enabled (disabling doesn’t significantly improve security)
  2. Click Save
  3. Repeat for both 2.4 GHz and 5 GHz networks if configured separately

🔄 Reconnect Devices: After changing Wi-Fi credentials, you’ll need to reconnect all devices (phones, laptops, smart home devices) with the new password.

Step 7: Set Up Guest Network

🧑‍💻 Guest Network: Isolates untrusted devices from your Bitcoin infrastructure.

  1. Go to Home Network → Wi-Fi → Guest Network
  2. Enable guest network
  3. Configure:
    • SSID: ATT-Guest (or custom name)
    • Network Access: Internet Only (isolates guests from your home LAN)
    • Security: WPA with WPA Version: WPA-2
    • Password: Strong unique password
  4. Click Save

📱 Guest Use Case: Connect phones, IoT devices, or visitor devices to guest network.

Step 8: Review Connected Devices Regularly

🕵️‍♂️ Device Monitoring: Detects unauthorized devices on your network.

  1. Go to Device → Status
  2. Review all connected devices
  3. Verify each device is recognized:
    • Proxmox host (Minisforum)
    • Start9 VM (e.g., xxx-yyy)
    • Your laptop/MacBook
    • Any other authorized devices
  4. If unknown device found:
    • Note its MAC address
    • Go to Firewall → MAC Filtering
    • Add to block list

Step 9: Firmware Updates

🔄 Auto Firmware Updates: The AT&T BGW320 firmware updates are managed automatically by AT&T. There is no user-accessible menu in the web interface to manually configure or trigger firmware updates. AT&T pushes updates to the gateway automatically when new firmware is available.

Keeps router secure with latest security patches. You can verify your current firmware version:

  1. Go to Device → System Information
  2. Look for Software Version in the device information

The router will automatically download and install firmware updates from AT&T. No manual configuration is required.

For best practice, document and securely store your device and network details—see the IP Address Reference section for a template.

For ongoing protection, see Maintenance Schedule.

IP Address Reference

Document your network layout for future reference:

DeviceIP AddressMAC AddressNotes
AT&T Router192.168.1.254-Gateway
Proxmox Host192.168.1.100XX:XX:XX:XX:XX:XXMinisforum X1-255
Start9 VM192.168.1.101XX:XX:XX:XX:XX:XXBitcoin node
MacBookDHCPXX:XX:XX:XX:XX:XXAdmin device

🗄️ Document Storage: Store this document securely (password manager or encrypted file).

Maintenance Schedule

Regular maintenance ensures ongoing security:

TaskFrequencyNotes
Review connected devicesWeeklyCheck for unauthorized devices
Check router logsMonthlyLook for suspicious activity
Update admin passwordQuarterlyUse password manager rotation
Review firewall rulesQuarterlyRemove unused port forwards
Backup router configMonthlyExport settings if available
Check for firmware updatesMonthlyOr enable auto-updates

🔒 Security is an ongoing process—review these settings periodically and stay informed about new threats and best practices.

Setting up Bitcoin Node and Self-Sovereignty Software

This section explains how to install and configure Bitcoin Core and other self-sovereignty applications on your Start9 OS via the web interface. The goal is to build a complete sovereign stack for financial independence and privacy.

Pre-requisites

Before starting, ensure you have completed all hardware setup and network configuration steps as outlined in the previous section.

Preparations

  • Hardware setup and Start9 OS installation completed
  • Network configuration and internet connectivity verified
  • Access to Start9 web interface (https://xxx-yyy.local or via IP address)
  • Start9 OS initial setup wizard completed
  • Proxmox VE host secured if using virtualization (optional)

Hardware

  • MINISFORUM X1-255 Mini PC (or similar x86_64 hardware, with RAM and SSD installed)
  • AT&T BGW320 router (with available LAN ports)
  • Monitor and keyboard (for initial setup and troubleshooting)
  • MacBook Air M1 or other laptop/desktop (for remote access and management)

Software

  • Start9 OS installed and running (latest stable release)
  • Web browser (Safari, Chrome, Firefox, etc.)
  • SSH client (optional, for advanced troubleshooting)

References

  • Start9 Documentation
  • Bitcoin Core Documentation
  • Lightning Network Resources
  • Start9 OS Downloads
  • Start9 Community Forums

Step‑by‑Step Setup

🗂️ Software Setup Steps: The following step sequence and time estimates are based on my own hardware setup experience. Your process may vary depending on hardware, network, and prior experience.

🧭 Install Sequence & Tips:

For the reasoning behind this installation order and other key setup tips, see:

  • 🗂️ Installation Order Recommendations: Why each service is installed in this sequence for best results
  • ⚙️ Important Considerations: Storage, resource, and backup advice for a reliable node

Step 1: Prepare Your Environment

Before installing any software, secure your Start9 OS and infrastructure.

📖 → Detailed Guide: Securing Start9 OS and VM Environment

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Change Start9 master password to strong, unique password
  • Configure automatic backups
  • Enable Tor for anonymous access
  • Verify system health and resources
  • Document your configuration

Time required: ~15-20 minutes

Step 2: Install Bitcoin Core

Set up the Bitcoin Core full node to validate transactions and maintain blockchain sovereignty.

📖 → Detailed Guide: Installing Bitcoin Core

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Access Start9 Marketplace
  • Install Bitcoin Core service
  • Configure Bitcoin node settings
  • Start initial blockchain download (IBD)
  • Monitor synchronization progress

Time required: ~5-10 minutes setup, 1-7 days IBD (sync)

Step 3: Install Mempool Explorer

Visualize the Bitcoin mempool and explore blockchain data with your own Mempool instance.

📖 → Detailed Guide: Installing Mempool Explorer

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Install Mempool service
  • Configure Mempool to use your Bitcoin Core node
  • Access Mempool web interface
  • Explore transaction fees and mempool statistics
  • Monitor your own transactions

Time required: ~5-10 minutes

Prerequisites: Bitcoin Core must be fully synchronized

Step 4: Install Electrum Server (Electrs)

Enable lightweight Bitcoin wallet connectivity with your own Electrum server.

📖 → Detailed Guide: Installing Electrum Server

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Install Electrs service
  • Connect Electrs to Bitcoin Core
  • Configure Electrum wallet on desktop/mobile
  • Verify wallet connects to your server
  • Test transaction queries

Time required: ~10-15 minutes setup, 4-8 hours indexing

Prerequisites: Bitcoin Core must be fully synchronized

Step 5: Install Nostr Relay

Run your own Nostr relay for decentralized social communication.

📖 → Detailed Guide: Installing Nostr Relay

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Install Nostr relay service
  • Configure relay settings and policies
  • Connect Nostr clients to your relay
  • Backup and manage relay data
  • Enable Lightning zaps (optional)

Time required: ~5-10 minutes

Step 6: Install Additional Privacy Tools (Optional)

Enhance privacy and sovereignty with additional services.

📖 → Detailed Guide: Installing Privacy Tools

Services covered:

  • Tor Relay/Bridge: Support the Tor network
  • Syncthing: Encrypted file synchronization
  • Vaultwarden: Self-hosted password manager
  • Nextcloud: Personal cloud storage
  • Bitwarden: Password management

Time required: ~5-15 minutes per service

Step 7: Install Lightning Network Node

Run a Lightning Network node for instant Bitcoin payments.

📖 → Detailed Guide: Installing Lightning Network Node

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Choose Lightning implementation (CLN, LND, or Eclair)
  • Install Lightning service from marketplace
  • Connect to your Bitcoin Core node
  • Configure Lightning node settings
  • Open your first Lightning channel

Time required: ~10-15 minutes

Prerequisites: Bitcoin Core must be fully synchronized

Step 8: Install BTCPay Server (Optional)

Accept Bitcoin payments for your business or projects.

📖 → Detailed Guide: Installing BTCPay Server

What you’ll accomplish:

  • Install BTCPay Server
  • Connect to Bitcoin Core and Lightning
  • Create your first store
  • Generate payment invoices
  • Configure webhooks and integrations

Time required: ~15-20 minutes

Prerequisites: Bitcoin Core and Lightning node synchronized

Estimated Total Time:

  • Essential stack: ~30 minutes setup + sync time (1-7 days IBD)
  • Privacy & communication stack: ~15-30 minutes
  • Payment stack: ~30 minutes setup

After Setup

  • Monitor synchronization: Check Bitcoin Core IBD progress daily
  • Open Lightning channels: Once Bitcoin Core synced, fund Lightning node
  • Connect wallets: Configure Electrum, Sparrow, or BlueWallet to your Electrum server
  • Explore marketplace: Browse additional Start9 services
  • Join community: Connect with other Start9 users on Nostr or forums

Installation Order Recommendations

Essential Stack (Recommended First)

  1. Bitcoin Core - Foundation of everything
  2. Mempool Explorer - Visualize blockchain data
  3. Electrum Server - Connect your wallets

Total time: ~30 minutes setup + sync time

Privacy & Communication Stack (Anytime)

  1. Nostr Relay - Decentralized communication
  2. Privacy Tools - Enhanced sovereignty

Total time: ~15-30 minutes

Payment Stack (After Bitcoin Core sync)

  1. Lightning Network - Instant payments
  2. BTCPay Server - Accept payments

Total time: ~30 minutes setup

Important Considerations

Storage Requirements

  • Bitcoin Core: ~600GB (and growing ~50GB/year)
  • Mempool Explorer: ~5-10GB
  • Electrum Server: ~80-100GB index
  • Nostr Relay: ~1-5GB (depends on usage)
  • Privacy Tools (Tor Relay/Bridge, Syncthing, Vaultwarden, Nextcloud, Bitwarden): ~1-10GB each
  • Lightning Network: ~1-5GB (depends on channels)
  • BTCPay Server: ~2-5GB

Recommended minimum: 1TB SSD for Bitcoin infrastructure

Initial Blockchain Download (IBD)

Bitcoin Core must download and verify the entire blockchain history:

  • Time required: 1-7 days (depending on hardware and internet speed)
  • During IBD: Your node validates every transaction since 2009
  • Be patient: This is a one-time process that ensures full sovereignty
  • Network usage: ~600GB download (one-time)

Tip: Most services (including Mempool Explorer, Electrum Server, Lightning Network, BTCPay Server, and privacy apps) require Bitcoin Core to be fully synchronized before they can function.

Resource Usage

  • CPU: Bitcoin Core uses moderate CPU during IBD, minimal after sync. Mempool Explorer, Electrum Server, Nostr Relay, and privacy tools use minimal CPU after initial setup.
  • RAM: Start9 OS manages resources efficiently; 32GB recommended for full stack including privacy and communication apps.
  • Network: Bitcoin Core uses ~200GB/month upload bandwidth after sync. Nostr Relay and privacy tools may use additional bandwidth depending on usage.
  • Storage: Plan for blockchain growth (~50GB/year) and additional privacy/communication app data.

Port Forwarding (Optional but Recommended)

For optimal Bitcoin network participation:

  • Bitcoin Core: Port 8333 (TCP)
  • Lightning Network: Port 9735 (TCP)
  • Nostr Relay: Port 4848 (TCP, optional, for public relay access)

Forwarding these ports allows your node and apps to accept inbound connections, strengthening the Bitcoin and Nostr networks.

Backup Recommendations

After installing services:

  • Backup seed phrases: Write down and store securely offline
  • Backup Lightning channels: Use Start9’s backup feature
  • Backup Nostr relay data and privacy tool configurations: Use built-in backup/export features
  • Backup Start9 configuration: Create system snapshots
  • Store backups offsite: Protect against hardware failure
