👉 Next: Securing Start9 OS and VM Environment

Securing Start9 OS and VM Environment

This guide provides step-by-step instructions to secure your Start9 OS instance—whether running natively or as a Proxmox VM—before installing Bitcoin Core and other self-sovereignty software. Securing your operating system is essential for protecting your node, wallets, and all self-hosted services from unauthorized access, data loss, and privacy threats.

Objectives:

  • Change the Start9 master password to a strong, unique value
  • Configure and verify automatic encrypted backups
  • Enable Tor for anonymous, private access to your node
  • Verify system health, resources, and connectivity
  • Document your configuration and backup details securely

Pre-requisites

Before starting, ensure you have completed the initial Start9 OS setup and have access to the web interface from a trusted device.

Preparation

  • Start9 OS installed and booted (native or as a Proxmox VM)
  • Initial Start9 setup wizard completed
  • Master password set during initial setup
  • Access to Start9 web interface (https://xxx-yyy.local or via IP address)

Hardware

  • Start9 OS device (native hardware or Proxmox VM)
  • MacBook, desktop, or laptop for secure access and documentation
  • USB drive, NAS, or external storage for backups (optional)

Software

  • Start9 OS running (latest stable release)
  • Web browser (Safari, Chrome, Firefox, etc.)
  • SSH client for advanced access (optional)
  • or Browser for private access (optional)

References

  • Start9 Official Documentation
  • Tor Browser Download
  • Start9 Community Forums
  • Proxmox: How to add a Samba (CIFS) share
  • Samba: Official Documentation
  • Ubuntu: Samba server guide

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Change Start9 Master Password

The master password is your primary access control to Start9. Set a strong, unique password:

  1. Access Start9 web interface: https://xxx-yyy.local or https://192.168.1.101
  2. Log in with your existing master password
  3. Click on System in the sidebar
  4. Go to Manage
  5. Select Change Master Password
  6. Enter your current password
  7. Enter new strong password (20+ characters)
  8. Confirm new password
  9. Click Change Password

🔒 Password Recommendations:

  • Minimum 20 characters
  • Mix uppercase, lowercase, numbers, special characters
  • Use passphrase format (e.g., Bitcoin*Sovereignty2025!)
  • Store securely in password manager
  • Keep backup written down in secure location

Step 2: Configure Start9 Backup & Recovery

Back up your Start9 OS before proceeding. First, decide where you want to store your backup. Start9 supports several backup destinations:

Backup destination options (for this setup):

  • Internal SSD (chosen): A dedicated folder on the 1TB SSD used by Proxmox VE and other VMs, selected because it has ample available space for regular backups. This SSD is separate from the 2TB SSD fully allocated to Start9 OS and its data, ensuring true redundancy and protecting against data loss if the main Start9 disk fails.
  • Local storage: USB drive connected to Start9 (optional, for portability and offline safety)
  • External storage: NAS or external hard drive on your local network (optional)
  • Cloud backup: Encrypted backup to a supported cloud provider (optional)

🔐 Backup Safety: Backups contain all your service data, private keys, and configurations. Store them securely and encrypt if using external storage.

If you choose to use an internal SSD (allocated to Proxmox VE and other services/VMs) as your backup destination, follow these steps:

How to prepare and use an internal SSD for Start9 backups:

  1. On your Proxmox VE host, identify the 1TB SSD (used for Proxmox VE and other VMs) that has available space for backups. Do not use the 2TB SSD fully allocated to Start9 OS and its data—this is for redundancy.

    To create a dedicated backup folder on the 1TB SSD:

    • Log in to your Proxmox VE host via SSH or the web shell.

    • Create a folder for Start9 backups (e.g., /mnt/start9-backups):

      sudo mkdir -p /mnt/start9-backups
# Check the UID and GID for the 'start9' user and group:
id start9
# Example output: uid=1000(start9) gid=1001(start9) ...
# Use these values to set ownership:
sudo chown 1000:1001 /mnt/start9-backups
# (Replace 1000:1001 with the actual UID:GID if different)

🧑‍💻 Create start9 User and Group (if needed):

sudo groupadd -g 1001 start9
sudo useradd -m -u 1000 -g 1001 start9
sudo passwd start9

This creates a start9 user with UID 1000 and GID 1001, and sets a password for use as backup credentials.

  • (Optional) If you want to use a dedicated partition, use lsblk to identify free space, then partition and format it (e.g., with fdisk and mkfs.ext4), then mount it at /mnt/start9-backups and add to /etc/fstab for persistence.

  • Next, map the directory in Proxmox VE:

  • In the Proxmox web UI, go to Datacenter → Directory Mapping.

  • Click Add.

  • In the pop-up, fill in the following attributes:

    • Name: start9-backups
    • Path: /mnt/start9-backups
    • Node: (select your Proxmox node)
    • Comment: (optional, e.g., “Start9 backup storage”)

  • Click Create to add the mapping.

  1. Make this folder accessible to your Start9 VM as a network share (SMB/CIFS or NFS):

    Set up an SMB (Samba) or NFS share for /mnt/start9-backups on your Proxmox host, and connect to it from the Start9 OS using the backup destination setup. Ensure network access and permissions are configured appropriately.

    → For detailed instructions, see How to Set Up an SMB (Samba) or NFS Share on Proxmox VE

  2. In the Start9 web interface, click on System in the sidebar.

  3. Go to Backups.

  4. Click Create Backup.

  5. In the backup dialog, click Open New to select or add a new backup destination.

  1. Fill in values for backup destination attributes to create new network folder:

    • Hostname/IP: The IP address or hostname of your Proxmox host (e.g., 192.168.1.100)
    • Path: The shared folder path (e.g., /mnt/start9-backups)
    • Username: The user account with access to the share (e.g., start9 or your Proxmox user)
    • Password: The password for the above user account

  2. After filling in the backup destination values, click the Connect button to continue.

  3. Select the Start9 services you want to back up (e.g., Bitcoin Core, Electrs, Mempool) by checking their boxes.

  1. Click Back Up Selected button to continue.
  2. When prompted, enter your Start9 master password (this will be used to encrypt the backup).
  3. Click Create Backup button to start the process.
  4. Wait for the backup to complete and verify that it finishes successfully.
  • You can verify backup completion in two ways:
    • Via notification:
      • Under Notifications, a notification will appear when the backup completes successfully.
    • Via last backup info:
      • The last backup information is shown under System → Backups → Create Backup menu.

💡 Backup Tip:

Start9 backups cannot be scheduled automatically. Make it a habit to perform manual backups regularly—especially after major changes, updates, or installing new services. This ensures your data and configurations are always protected.

Step 3: Enable Tor Service for Anonymity

Start9 OS includes built-in Tor support for accessing your node privately.

  1. In the Start9 web interface, click on System in the sidebar.
  2. Go to About.
  3. Under Web Addresses, locate the Tor address (looks like: abcd1234efgh5678.onion).
  4. Document your Tor address securely.

Benefits:

  • Access Start9 from anywhere without exposing your IP
  • Enhanced privacy when connecting to your node
  • Protection from ISP monitoring

Usage:

  • Install Tor Browser on your MacBook
  • Navigate to your .onion address to access Start9 securely
  • Connect wallets and services via Tor for maximum privacy

How to install Tor Browser on macOS:

📱 iOS Access Guide: See Accessing Start9 Web Interface via Tor on iOS for step-by-step instructions.

  1. Go to Tor Browser Download Page — Official site to download the Tor Browser for macOS, Windows, and Linux.

  2. Click Download for macOS

  3. Verify the download (critical security step):

    • On the Tor Project page, find the “Signature” link next to your macOS download
    • Download both the .dmg file and its .asc signature file
    • Open Terminal and run:
    # Install GnuPG if needed
brew install gnupg

# Navigate to Downloads folder
cd ~/Downloads

# Verify the signature
# Optional: Fetch the key (may fail if keyserver is down)
gpg --recv-keys 0xEBE9E286975A05796955E2084E3EDDDD37601E14 2>/dev/null || true

# Verify - this works even if recv-keys fails (uses cached key or key in signature)
gpg --verify tor-browser-macos-15.0.3.dmg.asc tor-browser-macos-15.0.3.dmg
    • You should see: “Good signature from Tor Project” ✓
    • If you see “no public key” error, try the recv-keys command again or download the key manually from https://keys.openpgp.org/
    • If signature verification fails, do not use the file - download again

  4. Open the verified .dmg file

  5. Drag Tor Browser to your Applications folder

  6. Open Applications → Tor Browser

  7. Click Connect to connect to the Tor network (takes 30-60 seconds)

  8. Once connected, paste your Start9 .onion address in the address bar

  9. You may see a certificate warning - This is normal and expected

About the HTTPS certificate warning:

When you access your Start9 .onion address via Tor Browser, you’ll likely see:

Warning: Potential Security Risk Ahead
Tor Browser detected a potential security threat...

This is NOT a security problem. Here’s why:

  • Start9 uses a self-signed certificate (not from a Certificate Authority)
  • Self-signed certs are normal for local/private services
  • Tor Browser warns about self-signed certs as a precaution
  • Your connection is still encrypted via Tor

How to proceed:

  1. Click Advanced → Accept the Risk and Continue
  2. You’ll now see Start9 login page
  3. Log in with your Start9 master password
  4. The connection is secure (traffic goes through Tor encryption + HTTPS)

Why self-signed certs are fine here:

  • You’re connecting to your own hardware via Tor (private network)
  • Tor provides encryption and anonymity
  • Certificate validation is less critical for personal services
  • This is standard practice for self-hosted services

How to permanently fix the certificate warning:

There are two approaches:

Option 1: Install a valid certificate (Advanced)

🛡️ macOS Security Prompt: During this process, macOS will prompt you to enter your Mac password to confirm changes to the System keychain and trust settings.

  1. In the Start9 web interface, click on System in the sidebar.
  2. Go to Manage.
  3. Click the Root CA section.
  4. Click the Download Root CA button to download the certificate authority file.
  5. Install the Root CA on your Mac to trust your Start9’s self-signed certificates:
    • Double-click the downloaded Root CA file (named after your Start9 device, e.g., xxx-yyy.crt).
    • The Keychain Access app will open. Choose System as the keychain and click Add.
    • In Keychain Access, find the imported certificate under the System keychain, named after your Start9 device (e.g., xxx-yyy Local Root CA).
    • Double-click the certificate, expand Trust, and set When using this certificate to Always Trust.
    • Close the window.
    • Restart your browser (including Tor Browser) for the changes to take effect.
    • Your browser will now trust your Start9’s self-signed certificates, and you should no longer see HTTPS warnings when accessing your node.
  6. Once installed, your browser (including Tor Browser) will recognize the certificate as valid for your Start9 services.

Option 2: Trust the self-signed certificate (Simple)

  1. On first visit, click Advanced → Accept the Risk and Continue
  2. Tor Browser will remember the certificate for this .onion address
  3. On future visits, the warning may not appear (or appear less prominently)
  4. This is the practical approach for personal self-hosted services

Option 3: Use HTTP (Least Secure)

  1. Access Start9 over unencrypted HTTP (not recommended)
  2. Less security but no certificate warnings
  3. Not recommended - defeats the purpose of HTTPS

Recommendation:

  • For personal use: Option 2 (accept and continue) is recommended and widely used
  • For multiple users or shared environments: Option 1 (install Root CA) is more secure and preferred
  • Most Bitcoin node operators use Option 2 without issues; Option 1 is for advanced or multi-user setups

Why verify the signature?

  • Protects against man-in-the-middle attacks
  • Ensures the file hasn’t been tampered with
  • Verifies it’s authentic Tor Project software

🕸️ Tor Connection Tip: The first connection may take a few seconds as Tor establishes a circuit. If you see a security warning, ensure you typed the .onion address correctly.

Alternative: Using curl with Tor (command line):

# If you prefer CLI access:
brew install tor

# If you get an error on MacBook Air M1 (Apple Silicon):
arch -arm64 brew install tor

# Start Tor daemon
tor &

# Access your Start9 .onion address via Tor proxy
curl -x socks5h://127.0.0.1:9050 https://abcd1234efgh5678.onion

💡 Note for Apple Silicon (M1/M2/M3) Macs: If you see errors with brew install tor, use arch -arm64 brew install tor to ensure Tor is installed for your Mac’s ARM64 architecture. This avoids compatibility issues and ensures Tor runs natively and efficiently.

Step 4: SSH Access (Optional)

🛡️ SSH Security Note: SSH access to Start9 is not critical for running a Bitcoin node. The web interface provides everything you need. Skip this unless you specifically need command-line access.

If you want command-line access to Start9:

  1. In the Start9 web interface, click on System in the sidebar.

  2. Go to Manage.

  3. Click on SSH.

  4. Click the + Add New Key button to add your SSH public key.

  5. Paste your SSH public key and save:

    • If you do not have an SSH key, generate one on your MacBook by running:

      ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "your_email@example.com"
# or use rsa if needed:
# ssh-keygen -t rsa -b 4096 -C "your_email@example.com"

    • By default, your public key will be in ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub (or id_rsa.pub).

    • To copy your public key to the clipboard, run:

      pbcopy < ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub
# or for RSA:
# pbcopy < ~/.ssh/id_rsa.pub

    • In the Start9 web interface, paste the copied key into the field provided and click Submit.

  6. From your MacBook, connect using:

    ssh start9@192.168.1.101

🛑 Troubleshooting: SSH Host Key Has Changed

If you see a warning like:

@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@
@    WARNING: REMOTE HOST IDENTIFICATION HAS CHANGED!     @
@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@
IT IS POSSIBLE THAT SOMEONE IS DOING SOMETHING NASTY!
Someone could be eavesdropping on you right now (man-in-the-middle attack)!
It is also possible that a host key has just been changed.
Host key verification failed.

This usually happens after reinstalling or resetting your Start9 device. To fix:

  1. If you trust the new key, remove the old key from your known_hosts file:

    ssh-keygen -R 192.168.1.101

  2. Try connecting again:

    ssh start9@192.168.1.101

    Accept the new host key when prompted.

Step 5: Network Configuration

🗄️ Proxmox VM Only: This step is only for Proxmox VM users. Skip if running native Start9.

If running Start9 as a Proxmox VM, verify the VM network:

  1. In Proxmox web interface, select your Start9 VM

  2. Go to Hardware → Network Device

  3. Verify:

    • Bridge: vmbr0 (or your bridge name)
    • Model: VirtIO (fastest)
    • MAC Address: Should be assigned by DHC

Step 6: Verify Internet Connectivity (Final Check)

🌐 Network Requirement: This step is informational only. Start9 needs internet access to sync the Bitcoin blockchain and reach peers.

Before installing Bitcoin Core, verify:

  1. Test from MacBook:

    # Ping Start9 IP
ping 192.168.1.101

# Or by hostname if using mDNS
ping xxx-yyy.local

  2. Check Start9 web interface: Load https://192.168.1.101 - if it loads, internet works

If connectivity fails:

  • Check Proxmox VM network settings
  • Verify DHCP lease on router
  • Restart the VM
  • Check cable connections (if native hardware)

Step 7: Document Your Configuration

Create a secure document with:

## Start9 Configuration Reference

**Access Information:**

-  URL: https://xxx-yyy.local
-  IP Address: 192.168.1.101
-  Master Password: [STORED SECURELY]
-  Tor Address: [your-onion-address].onion

**Backup Information:**

-  Backup Location: [where backups are stored]
-  Backup Frequency: Daily
-  Last Successful Backup: [date]

**Network Information:**

-  Router IP: 192.168.1.254
-  Start9 IP: 192.168.1.101
-  Proxmox Host IP: 192.168.1.100 (if using virtualization)

**Important Dates:**

-  Start9 Installation Date: [date]
-  Last System Update: [date]
-  Last Password Change: [date]

Store this document in an encrypted password manager or safe.

Accessing Start9 Web Interface via Tor on iOS

You can securely access your Start9 web interface from your iPhone or iPad using the Tor Browser app. This is useful for remote administration or when you want to use your .onion address for privacy.

  1. Install Tor Browser

    • Download the recommended Tor browser app for iOS: Onion Browser by Mike Tigas, available for free on the iOS App Store. This is the most widely used and trusted Tor browser for iPhone and iPad, endorsed by the Tor Project.
    • In the App Store, search for “Onion Browser” (developer: Mike Tigas) and verify it is the official app. Avoid unofficial clones or apps with few reviews.
    • Open Onion Browser and complete any initial setup prompts.

  2. Get Your Start9 .onion Address

    • In the Start9 web interface (from a trusted device), go to System → About.
    • Under Web Addresses, copy your Tor’s .onion address (e.g., abcdefg123456789.onion).

  3. Open the .onion Address in Tor Browser

    • Open the Tor Browser app on your iOS device.
    • Paste your Start9 .onion address into the address bar and press Go.
    • Log in with your Start9 credentials as usual.

📱 Tor Mobile Tips:

  • For best security, always use the Tor Browser app for .onion addresses.
  • You can bookmark your Start9 .onion address in the app for quick access.
  • If you have trouble connecting, check that Tor is enabled on your Start9 device and your mobile device has internet access.

How to Set Up an SMB (Samba) or NFS Share on Proxmox VE

SMB (Samba) vs NFS: Pros and Cons

FeatureSMB (Samba)NFS
CompatibilityWorks on Windows, macOS, LinuxBest for Linux/Unix, limited Windows support
PerformanceSlightly slower on Linux, good on WindowsGenerally faster on Linux
AuthenticationUser/password, supports granular permissionsHost-based, simpler but less granular
Ease of SetupMore config steps, more optionsSimpler for Linux hosts and clients
SecuritySupports encryption, user authNo built-in encryption, relies on network
Use CaseMixed-OS networks, Windows/macOS clientsLinux-to-Linux sharing, VM environments
Firewall Ports445, 139 (SMB/CIFS)2049 (NFS)
StabilityVery stable, mature protocolVery stable, mature protocol

Summary:

  • Use SMB (Samba) if you need compatibility with Windows or macOS, or want user-based access control.
  • Use NFS for Linux-only environments or when you want simpler, faster file sharing between Linux systems (like Proxmox and Linux VMs).

To set up an SMB (Samba) share (used in this guide)

  1. Check that the start9 user and group exist and have the correct UID and GID:

    id start9
getent group 1001

  2. Check the ownership of the backup directory:

    ls -ld /mnt/start9-backups

  3. Install Samba if not already installed:

    sudo apt update
sudo apt install samba

  4. Edit the Samba configuration file:

    sudo nano /etc/samba/smb.conf

  5. Add a section for your backup share at the end of the file:

    [start9-backups]
path = /mnt/start9-backups
browseable = yes
read only = no
guest ok = no
valid users = start9
force user = start9
force group = start9
create mask = 0660
directory mask = 0770

  6. Set a Samba password for the start9 user:

    sudo smbpasswd -a start9

  7. Restart Samba:

    sudo systemctl restart smbd

  8. Ensure your firewall allows SMB/CIFS (port 445; port 139 is only needed for legacy systems).

🛡️ Firewall Troubleshooting:

If you have trouble connecting to your Samba share, the issue is usually with the firewall on your Proxmox Datacenter or the Start9 VM itself—not your AT&T or home router. By default, most home routers allow local LAN traffic, so no changes are needed there.

Where to check:

  • Proxmox Datacenter: Check the firewall settings in the Proxmox web UI under Datacenter → Firewall and on the specific node or VM. Enable TCP port 445 (both IN and OUT) for SMB/CIFS.
  • Proxmox PVE Node: Also check the firewall settings for the Proxmox PVE node itself (Node → Firewall) to ensure TCP port 445 is allowed for SMB/CIFS traffic between the node and VMs.
  • Start9 VM: If the firewall is enabled for the Start9 VM via the Proxmox web UI (VM → Firewall), ensure it allows inbound SMB/CIFS traffic on TCP port 445.
  • Client device: If your Mac, Linux, or Windows client has a firewall, ensure it allows outbound connections to these ports.

Testing connectivity:

  • On Linux/macOS: nc -zv <proxmox-ip> 445
  • On Windows: telnet <proxmox-ip> 445
  • Successful connection means port 445 is open and reachable.

For most home networks, no router changes are required. Only adjust router settings if you have custom LAN firewall rules. Never open SMB/CIFS ports to the internet for security reasons.

To set up an NFS share

NFS shares are not used or covered in this mdbook. If you require an NFS share for your environment, please refer to the official Proxmox or Linux documentation for up-to-date instructions:

  • Proxmox: How to add an NFS share
  • Ubuntu: NFS server setup

NFS is generally recommended only for advanced Linux-to-Linux sharing scenarios. For most users, SMB (Samba) is preferred for compatibility and ease of use.

👈 Prev: Setting up Bitcoin Node and Self-Sovereignty Software

👉 Next: Installing Bitcoin Core

Installing Bitcoin Core

This guide provides step-by-step instructions to install and configure Bitcoin Core on your Start9 OS instance. Running your own Bitcoin Core node gives you complete sovereignty over your Bitcoin transactions and eliminates trust in third parties.

In this guide, you will:

  • Access the Start9 Marketplace
  • Install Bitcoin Core
  • Configure node settings
  • Monitor blockchain sync
  • Enable port forwarding (optional)
  • Connect other services

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Start9 OS installed and accessible via web interface
  • Initial Start9 setup wizard completed
  • Master password set during initial setup

Hardware

  • Start9 OS device (native or Proxmox VM)
  • At least 600GB free storage space
  • MacBook or desktop for access

Software

  • Start9 OS running
  • Web browser (Safari, Chrome, Firefox, etc.)

References

  • Bitcoin Core Documentation
  • Start9 Bitcoin Core Guide

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Access Start9 Marketplace

Bitcoin Core is the reference implementation of a Bitcoin full node. It validates all transactions, enforces consensus rules, provides privacy, supports the network, and enables Lightning.

  1. Open your web browser
  2. Navigate to your Start9 web interface:
    • Local access: https://xxx-yyy.local (replace with your device name)
    • IP access: https://192.168.1.101 (use your Start9 IP address)
  3. Log in with your Start9 master password

Step 2: Browse and Install Bitcoin Core

  1. Click on Marketplace in the left sidebar
  2. Search for “Bitcoin Core” in the search bar
  3. Locate Bitcoin Core in the results
  4. Click on Bitcoin Core to open the service details
  5. Review the service description and requirements
  6. Click Install button
  7. Wait for the download and installation to complete (~2-5 minutes)

🌐 Marketplace Help: If you don’t see Bitcoin Core, ensure you’re connected to the internet and refresh the marketplace.

Step 3: Configure Bitcoin Core

After installation completes:

  1. Select the Bitcoin Core service in the Services list.

  2. For first-time setup, click Status → Configure. For future changes, use Menu → Config to access Bitcoin Core settings.

  3. Review and adjust the configuration options as shown in the screenshot below. Available config settings include (suggested values in parentheses):

    • RPC Settings (RPC configuration options)
      • Enable (Allow remote RPC requests) (On — required for Start9 web portal to connect; do not expose to the public internet)
      • Username (custom, not default) — Set a strong, unique username for security.
      • RPC Password (custom, not default) — Set a strong, unique password for security.
      • Advanced:
        • Authorization (default; nothing is added)
        • RPC Server Timeout (30s)
        • Threads (16) — Increase for high-performance or multi-user environments.
        • Work Queue (128 requests) — Default is fine unless you have many concurrent RPC calls.
    • ZeroMQ Enabled (On for service integrations)
    • Transaction Index (On — default) — Recommended for full nodes with sufficient storage (pruning disabled). Required for Mempool, Electrum, and other advanced services. Leave enabled if you plan to install these or want full transaction search capabilities.
    • Cointstat Index (Off unless required by another service)
    • Wallet
      • Enable Wallet (On if you want to use the built-in wallet)
      • Avoid Partial Spends (On)
      • Discard Change Tolerance (0.0001 BTC/kB)
    • Advanced
      • Mempool
        • Persist Mempool (On)
        • Max Mempool Size (300 MiB)
        • Mempool Expiration (336 hours)
        • Enable Full RBF (On) — Recommended for modern wallets and fee flexibility.
        • Permit Bare Multisig (On) — Only disable for strict policy or privacy reasons.
        • Relay OP_RETURN Transactions (On)
        • Max OP_RETURN Size (83 bytes)
      • Peers
        • Make public (On if you want to help the network)
        • Disable Peer Discovery (Off)
        • Disable Clearnet (Off)
        • Use V2 P2P Transport Protocol (On)
        • Add Nodes (leave blank unless connecting to specific peers; nothing is added)
      • Pruning Mode (Automatic) — Use Automatic for most users; set a custom value if you need to limit disk usage.
        • Max Chain Size (550 MiB) — Minimum for pruned node; set higher for more history.
      • Database Cache (5000 MiB) — Higher cache improves performance if you have RAM available.
      • Database Batch (33554432 bytes) — Default is fine for most users.
      • Block Filters
        • Compute Compact Block Filters (BIP158) (On for Electrum)
        • Serve Compact Block Filters to Peers (BIP157) (Off) — Enable only if you want to serve filters to other clients.
      • Bloom Filters (BIP37)
        • Serve Bloom Filter to Peers (Off unless needed by a wallet)
  1. Click Save to apply configuration

Step 4: Start Bitcoin Core

  1. After configuration is saved, click Start button
  2. Confirm you want to start the service
  3. Wait for the service to initialize (~30 seconds)

Step 5: Monitor Initial Blockchain Download (IBD)

Bitcoin Core must download and verify the entire blockchain history:

  1. Click on Bitcoin Core in the Services list
  2. Navigate to Logs or Status tab
  3. Monitor synchronization progress

IBD Progress Indicators:

Progress: 15.23%
Current Height: 150,000
Best Block: 850,000
Verification Progress: 15.23%

Expected Timeline:

  • Day 1-2: Downloads blocks rapidly (10-30% per day)
  • Day 3-5: Verification slows (older blocks have more transactions)
  • Day 5-7: Final verification and indexing
  • Total: 1-7 days depending on hardware and connection

Network & Memory Activity:

  • Download: ~600GB total
  • Upload: Minimal during IBD
  • CPU: Moderate usage during verification
  • Memory: Typically 1–4 GB RAM used during sync (can be higher if database cache is increased)

Step 6: Verify Bitcoin Core is Running

Once IBD completes:

  1. Check service status shows Running
  2. Verify Synced indicator shows 100%
  3. Check peer connections (should be 8-125 peers)

Indicators of successful sync:

Status: Running
Synced: 100%
Peers: 45
Best Block: 850,000 (current tip)

Step 7: Enable Port Forwarding (Recommended)

After Bitcoin Core is installed and running, you should enable port forwarding on your router to allow inbound connections from the Bitcoin network:

  1. Access your router’s configuration page (see your router’s manual for details)
  2. Forward Port 8333 (TCP) to your Start9 node’s IP address (e.g., 192.168.1.101)
  3. Save and apply the changes
  4. Verify port is open by checking Bitcoin Core peer connections (should increase to 40-125)

Why port forwarding?

  • Supports the Bitcoin network
  • Helps new nodes sync faster
  • Increases network decentralization

🔒 Network Ports Explained:

  • RPC port 8332 (🔑): For remote commands (web portal, integrations). Keep this accessible only on your local network—do not forward to the internet.
  • P2P port 8333 (🌐): For Bitcoin network peer connections. This is the only port you will forward in Step 7 to help the network.
  • Enabling remote RPC does not expose your node to the public internet unless you manually forward port 8332 (not recommended).

Step 8: Verify Your Published IP Address

To check what IP address Bitcoin Core is advertising to the network:

Method 1: Check Bitcoin Core Logs (Optional But Recommended)

  1. Go to Bitcoin Core → Logs in Start9 interface

  2. Search for lines containing addr or Trying to listen on port

  3. Look for output like:

    
2025-01-15 10:23:45 Listening on port 8333
2025-01-15 10:23:46 Received addr from peer: 203.0.113.42:8333

Method 2: Use Bitcoin Core RPC (Advanced)

If you want to query Bitcoin Core directly via RPC (requires RPC credentials):

# Get RPC credentials from Start9 Bitcoin Core properties
# Then query from your MacBook:
curl -u rpc_user:rpc_password http://192.168.1.101:8332 \
  -d '{"jsonrpc":"1.0","id":"curltest","method":"getnetworkinfo","params":[]}' \
  -H 'content-type: application/json'

# Look for "localaddresses" section in the output
# It will show:
# "address": "192.168.1.101"      # (without port forwarding)
# "address": "203.0.113.42"       # (with port forwarding - your public IP)

Note: bitcoin-cli command won’t work via SSH to Start9 because Bitcoin Core runs in a container. Use the RPC method instead or check logs.

Method 3: Check External Services (Quick)

  1. Visit https://bitnodes.io/ (Bitcoin node explorer)
  2. Search for your public IP address (check at https://whatismyipaddress.com/)
  3. Status will show one of:
    • Unreachable ❌ (no port forwarding)
    • Reachable ✅ (port forwarding configured)

Understanding “Unreachable” Status (Normal Before Port Forwarding):

When your node shows as Unreachable on Bitnodes.io, it means:

  • Your Bitcoin Core node IS running
  • Your Bitcoin Core IS syncing the blockchain
  • Your Bitcoin Core IS connecting to other peers (outbound connections)
  • External nodes CANNOT initiate connections TO your node (inbound)

Implications:

AspectUnreachable (No Port Forwarding)Reachable (Port Forwarding)
Blockchain Sync✅ Works normally✅ Works normally
Broadcasting Transactions✅ Full nodes relay✅ Full nodes relay
Supporting Lightning✅ Works normally✅ Works normally
Network Support⚠️ Limited (outbound only)✅ Helps new nodes sync
Your Privacy✅ Better (hidden)⚠️ Public IP visible

Key Point: Your node still functions 100% for personal use (running Lightning, Electrum, etc.). The only thing you’re missing is helping OTHER nodes find and sync from you.

What to do next:

  1. If you have not already, see Step 7: Enable Port Forwarding to allow inbound connections (forward port 8333 to your node as needed).
  2. Wait 5–10 minutes for Bitcoin to re-advertise your node.
  3. Check Bitnodes.io again—should show Reachable ✅ if port forwarding is working.
  4. Peer count in Start9 should increase significantly.

What you should see:

  • Without port forwarding: Your local IP (192.168.1.101) appears in Start9 logs, Bitnodes shows “Unreachable”
  • With port forwarding: Your ISP’s public IP appears in Bitnodes shows “Reachable”, peer connections increase
  • Peer connections: Typically 8-30 outbound peers always, 40-125+ total with inbound connections

Troubleshooting:

  • If peers don’t increase after port forwarding, wait 5-10 minutes (Bitcoin needs time to advertise)
  • Check that port 8333 TCP is actually forwarded (test at canyouseeme.org)
  • Verify Bitcoin Core status shows “Public: Enabled” in configuration

Step 9: Configure Blockchain Pruning (Optional)

If storage is limited and you don’t need full blockchain:

  1. Stop Bitcoin Core service
  2. Go to Configure
  3. Enable Pruning Mode
  4. Set pruning size (minimum 550MB, recommended 10-20GB)
  5. Save and Restart

Warning: Pruning requires re-downloading and re-verifying the blockchain. Do this during initial setup, not after full sync.

Step 10: Test RPC Connection

Verify Bitcoin Core RPC is accessible:

  1. Go to Bitcoin Core → Menu → Properties
  2. Find RPC Credentials:

ℹ️ Default Credentials: If you did not change them, the default is RPC User: bitcoin, RPC Password: (auto-generated by Start9; see Properties page)


RPC URL: http://192.168.1.101:8332
RPC User: bitcoin
RPC Password: [auto-generated]
  1. Copy credentials for use with other services

Step 11: Check Blockchain Data

Verify blockchain storage:

  1. Go to Bitcoin Core → Menu → Properties
  2. Check Disk Usage: Should show ~600GB when fully synced
  3. Verify Synced Block Height: Should match current Bitcoin Block Height

You have now completed all the essential steps to install and configure your Bitcoin Core node on Start9 OS. If, in the future, you need to upgrade your VM’s resources (for example, to enable advanced features like transaction indexing, improve sync speed, or resolve performance issues), refer to the section below for detailed instructions.

🛠️ Resource Upgrade Tip: For tips on upgrading your VM’s resources, see Ongoing Maintenance and Improvement.

Ongoing Maintenance and Improvement

If your Bitcoin node sync is slow, memory usage is high, or you need to enable features like transaction indexing (which requires pruning to be disabled), you may need to increase the memory and storage allocated to your Start9 VM in Proxmox VE.

Increasing Memory (RAM)

  1. In Proxmox VE, select your Start9 VM in the left sidebar.
  2. Click the Hardware tab.
  3. Select Memory and click Edit.
  4. For the MINISFORUM AI X1-255 (with 32GB DDR5 RAM), allocate 24,576 MiB to 28,672 MiB RAM to your Start9 VM. This ensures optimal performance for Bitcoin Core and all other apps, while leaving 4,096–8,192 MiB for Proxmox VE host overhead and system stability.
  5. Click OK.
  6. Restart the VM for the new memory allocation to take effect.

💡 Recommended: With 32GB RAM and only one main VM (Start9 OS), allocating 24,576–28,672 MiB to Start9 is ideal for running multiple Bitcoin apps and future-proofing your node.

Increasing Storage

  1. The MINISFORUM AI X1-255 comes with a 1TB NVMe SSD, which is suitable for most users running Proxmox VE and a single Start9 VM with all apps installed via Start9 OS.
  2. In Proxmox VE, select your Start9 VM.
  3. Click the Hardware tab.
  4. Select the disk (e.g., scsi0 or virtio0), click the Disk Action button, then select Resize. In the ‘Resize Disk’ popup, enter the Size Increment (GiB) to expand the disk if you add a larger SSD in the future.
  5. For most setups, allocate at least 800GB to the Start9 VM to ensure enough space for the Bitcoin blockchain, apps, and future growth. Leave the remainder for Proxmox VE and system overhead.
  6. Click Resize disk if expanding.
  7. In Start9 OS, expand the filesystem to use the new space:
    • Log in to the Start9 web interface.
    • Go to System → Monitor (under the Insights section), then check the Disk section for storage info.
    • Use the built-in disk expansion tool if available, and follow prompts to expand the root filesystem and use the new space.

💡 Recommended: With a 1TB SSD, allocate 800GB+ to your Start9 VM for blockchain and app data. This covers current needs and allows for future expansion. If you plan to run additional services or store large files, consider upgrading to a larger SSD (2TB+) in the future.

Restart and Verify

  • Restart the Start9 VM.
  • Check available memory and storage in the Start9 web interface (System → About or System → Storage).

ℹ️ Tip: Upgrading your VM’s resources can help with sync speed, reduce memory pressure, and enable features like transaction indexing (txindex) that require pruning to be disabled.

Option: Reclaim Used Space from Existing 1TB SSD (No New Physical Storage)

If you want to reclaim space on your existing 1TB internal SSD (for example, if old or unused VM disks are taking up space), you can safely delete those disks and reallocate the freed space to your current Start9 VM. This is useful if you want to maximize available storage without adding new hardware.

Steps:

  1. Shut down your Start9 VM in Proxmox VE.
  2. Detach and remove old/unused virtual disks:
    • In the Proxmox web UI, select your Start9 VM.
    • Go to the Hardware tab, select the old or unused disk (e.g., scsi1, virtio1, or similar), and click Detach.
    • After detaching, click Remove to delete the disk entry from the VM.
  3. Delete the unused disk image to reclaim space:
    • Go to Datacenter → Storage and select the storage pool (e.g., local-lvm).
    • Find the unused disk image (it may be listed as ‘Unused Disk’ or by VM ID) and delete it. This will free up space on your 1TB SSD.

⚠️ Disk Reclaim Safety: Always back up important data before deleting disks. Double-check you are removing the correct disk to avoid accidental data loss.

  1. (If needed) Re-add the ‘local-lvm’ storage to Proxmox:

    • In the Proxmox web UI, go to Datacenter → Storage → Add → LVM-Thin (or LVM if appropriate).
    • Select the correct volume group (usually local-lvm), give it a name, and confirm to re-add it to your storage list.

  2. Add a new virtual disk to your Start9 VM:

    • In the Proxmox web UI, select your Start9 VM.
    • Go to the Hardware tab, click Add → Hard Disk.
    • Choose the storage pool (e.g., local-lvm) where you reclaimed space, set the desired size, and select the disk type (scsi or virtio recommended).
    • Add the new disk to the VM.

  3. Change boot option to CD/DVD ISO and mount Start9 ISO image:

    • In the Proxmox web UI, select your Start9 VM.
    • Go to the Hardware tab, click Add → CD/DVD Drive (if not already present).
    • Select the Start9 OS ISO image from your Proxmox storage.
    • Go to the Options tab, set Boot Order to prioritize the CD/DVD drive.
    • Confirm changes.
    • Restart the Start9 VM to boot from the ISO image and begin installation or recovery as needed.

  4. Start the Start9 VM after reinstalling from ISO and expand the filesystem in Start9 OS:

    • After completing the Start9 OS installation or recovery from the ISO (see previous step), start the Start9 VM.
    • Log in to the Start9 web interface.
    • Go to System → Monitor (under the Insights section), then check the Disk section for storage info.
    • Use the built-in disk expansion tool if available, and follow prompts to expand the root filesystem and use the new space.

🛑 Storage Full Warning: If you experience issues with Bitcoin Core not starting or syncing, see Troubleshooting: “No space left on device” after syncing Bitcoin blockchain for solutions.

Troubleshooting: “No space left on device” after syncing Bitcoin blockchain

Initial Symptom:

  • The Bitcoin Core app in Start9 OS gets stuck in Starting… and never progresses to syncing or completing the blockchain download. This is often the first sign that your storage is full, even if no error is shown.

Explicit Error:

  • After you restart the Start9 OS VM in Proxmox VE, you may see a no space left on device error. This confirms that your VM or device storage is full.

Both symptoms require the same solutions below.

What causes this?

  • The Bitcoin blockchain (as of 2025) is 700–800GB and growing.
  • Start9 OS, apps, logs, and overhead require additional space (20–40GB+).
  • If your VM disk is 1TB or less, you may run out of space after a full sync, especially if running other apps or keeping old backups/logs.

Options to resolve

1. Free up space (temporary fix)

  • Delete old backups, logs, or unused apps from the Start9 web interface (look under System, Storage, or the app’s own settings page).
  • Remove unnecessary files via SSH if enabled.
  • This may only provide a short-term solution.

2. Expand with SSD storage (faster, more expensive, path to internal upgrade)

  • Add a new, larger SSD to your Proxmox host (internal NVMe or external USB 3.0/3.1 SSD).

🔎 SSD Comparison Table (2TB, Dec 2025):

⚙️ Hardware Compatibility: The MINISFORUM AI X1-255 supports M.2 2280 NVMe SSDs (PCIe Gen 3, Gen 4, and Gen 5 only—not SATA). All SSDs in the table above are physically compatible with the internal slot, but Gen 5 drives will operate at Gen 4 speeds due to platform limits.

Recommended picks for the MINISFORUM AI X1-255 (Dec 2025):

  • Best Value & Performance: Samsung 990 PRO 2TB — Outstanding Gen 4 speed, high endurance, and proven reliability. Ideal for most users.
  • Best Budget: Crucial P310 2TB — QLC-based, but fast for the price; suitable for light to moderate workloads.
  • Best for Future-Proofing: Crucial T710 2TB or Samsung 9100 PRO 2TB — Gen 5 drives, fully compatible but limited to Gen 4 speeds in this system; choose if you plan to reuse the SSD in a future Gen 5 device.

All other models in the table are also compatible and offer excellent performance. Always choose 2TB or larger for future-proofing your node, and verify the model and capacity before purchase.

💡 Alternative Option: Quick but Temporary External SSD Storage

You can use a 2.5“ SATA SSD (such as Crucial MX500, WD Blue 3D NAND, or Samsung 870 EVO) in a USB enclosure (e.g., UGREEN 2.5“ SATA to USB 3.0 Enclosure) for fast data transfer or as a temporary external drive. This is useful for migration or backup, but these drives will not fit the internal M.2 slots of the MINISFORUM AI X1-255 and are not recommended for permanent use as your main node storage.

3. Expand with HDD storage (slower, cheaper, path to NAS storage upgrade)

🗄️ HDD Storage Options: Best Value for Large, Reusable Storage

If you are comfortable with slower storage (for archival, blockchain, or NAS use), HDDs offer the best cost per TB and can be easily repurposed for Nextcloud, RAID-1, or other NAS storage in the future. There are two main approaches:

Option 1: Buy a NAS HDD and Enclosure (Modular, Flexible, RAID-Ready)

  • Buy a bare NAS-grade HDD (e.g., Seagate IronWolf, WD Red Plus) and a compatible USB 3.0/3.1 or SATA enclosure.
  • Example drives:
    • Seagate IronWolf 4TB NAS HDD
    • WD Red Plus 4TB NAS HDD
  • Choose larger capacities (6TB, 8TB, or more) as needed for future NAS/RAID-1 upgrades.
  • Pair with a quality enclosure (single or dual-bay for RAID-1, e.g., TerraMaster, ORICO, or similar brands).
  • This setup is ideal if you want to migrate the drive(s) into a dedicated NAS or RAID-1 box for Nextcloud or other self-hosted storage later.

Option 2: Buy an All-in-One External HDD (Plug-and-Play, Shuckable)

  • Purchase a pre-assembled external HDD with enclosure, such as the Seagate Expansion 24TB/26TB External USB 3.0 Desktop Hard Drive.
  • These are plug-and-play with Proxmox and can be used immediately for blockchain or archival storage.
  • When ready to upgrade, you can “shuck” (remove) the internal HDD and install it in a NAS or RAID-1 enclosure for Nextcloud or other uses.

🛠️ Shucking & Warranty: Shucking voids the Seagate warranty, but is a common, cost-effective upgrade path for large, reliable storage.

  • The internal drive is typically a Seagate Exos or similar enterprise-class model, compatible with most NAS/RAID systems.

Both options provide a clear path to future NAS/RAID-1 storage for Nextcloud or other self-hosted solutions. Choose based on your comfort with assembling enclosures (Option 1) or convenience and best value per TB (Option 2).

Samsung 990 PRO 2TB: User Experience & Step-by-Step Upgrade (Dec 2025)

My Final Choice: I chose to buy the Samsung 990 PRO 2TB NVMe SSD for my MINISFORUM AI X1-255. This drive offers the best balance of speed, endurance, and reliability for running a full Bitcoin node and Start9 OS in a Proxmox VM.

Why this is best: The Samsung 990 PRO delivers top-tier Gen 4 performance, high endurance (1200 TBW), and proven compatibility with the MINISFORUM AI X1-255. Upgrading to this SSD ensures fast sync, responsive apps, and future-proof storage for blockchain and self-hosted services.

How to migrate Start9 OS and all app data to a new 2TB SSD (cleanest, most future-proof method):

🛑 Important: This method ensures all Start9 OS, apps, and data are on the new 2TB SSD, making future upgrades, backups, and migrations much simpler. The 1TB SSD is now free for Proxmox host use or other VMs.

If you are starting fresh:

  • You can skip the backup and restore steps below. Simply follow the instructions to install the 2TB SSD, prepare it in Proxmox, and perform a clean Start9 OS installation on the new disk.

If you need to migrate existing data:

  • In the Start9 web interface, go to System → Backup and create a full backup of your node, apps, and settings. Download the backup file to your computer or external drive.
  • After installing Start9 OS on the new disk, restore your backup from the web interface to recover your node, apps, and data.

Step-by-step for a clean install or migration:

  1. Shut down all running services and systems:

    • In the Start9 web interface, stop all running apps (including Bitcoin Core).
    • Go to System → Power → Shutdown to power off Start9 OS.
    • In Proxmox VE, select your Start9 VM and click the Stop button to fully power off the VM.
    • Once all VMs are off, shut down the Proxmox VE host if you plan to install the SSD physically.

  2. Physically install the new 2TB SSD:

    • Power off and unplug your MINISFORUM device.
    • Open the case, install the 2TB NVMe SSD in the M.2 slot, and reassemble the device.

  3. Prepare the 2TB SSD for Proxmox VM storage:

    • Power on the device and boot into Proxmox VE.
    • In the Proxmox web UI, go to Datacenter → Node → Disks and confirm the new SSD is listed.
    • Select the 2TB SSD, click Wipe Disk (if needed), then Initialize Disk with GPT.
    • Go to Datacenter → Node → LVM and click Create: Volume Group. Select the 2TB SSD and create a new volume group (e.g., vg-2tb).
    • (Optional) Go to Datacenter → Node → LVM-Thin and click Create: Thinpool. Select the new volume group and allocate ~1900–2000GB for the thin pool (e.g., data-2tb).
      • Only create an LVM-Thin pool if you want to use thin provisioning, fast snapshots, or plan to run multiple VMs/disks on this storage.
      • If you are dedicating the entire disk to a single VM disk, standard LVM is sufficient and simpler.

⚠️ Thin Provisioning Risk: LVM-Thin allows you to over-allocate space (thin provisioning), which is flexible but risky: if the physical disk fills up because thin-provisioned volumes use more space than is physically available, all VMs/disks on that pool can become read-only or fail. Monitor disk usage carefully if using LVM-Thin.

  1. Remove the old 1TB LVM-Thin pool and free up the 1TB SSD for Proxmox host use:

    • In the Proxmox web UI, go to Datacenter → Node → LVM-Thin and identify the existing 1TB LVM-Thin pool (e.g., local-lvm).
    • Make sure no VMs or disks are using this pool (detach and remove any VM disks if needed).
    • Select the LVM-Thin pool, click More → Destroy to delete it.
    • Go to Datacenter → Node → LVM, select the associated volume group from the 1TB SSD, and click More → Destroy to remove it (required if you want to recreate a new LVM or LVM-Thin pool on the 1TB SSD).
    • The 1TB SSD is now ready for new Proxmox VE storage, ISOs, or other VMs as needed.

💡 Tip: Always double-check that all important data is backed up before deleting or removing any disks or storage pools. Handle SSDs and device internals with care to avoid static damage.

  1. Create a new LVM or LVM-Thin storage pool on the 1TB SSD for Proxmox VE:

    • In the Proxmox web UI, go to Datacenter → Node → Disks and select the 1TB SSD.
    • Wipe the 1TB SSD or re-initialize it with GPT if you want to repurpose it for new Proxmox storage or VMs. This will remove all existing partitions (BIOS boot, EFI, LVM, etc.) and allow you to create new storage pools as needed.
    • After destroying the old LVM-Thin pool and LVM volume group, simply create a new volume group and LVM-Thin pool using the existing LVM partition.
    • Go to Datacenter → Node → LVM and click Create: Volume Group. Select the existing LVM partition on the 1TB SSD and create a new volume group (e.g., vg-1tb).
    • (Optional) Go to Datacenter → Node → LVM-Thin and click Create: Thinpool. Select the new volume group and allocate the desired size for the thin pool (e.g., data-1tb).

🗑️ Destructive Operation Warning: Only wipe or re-initialize the disk if you are certain you no longer need any data or partitions on it. This action is destructive and cannot be undone. Double-check that all important data is backed up before proceeding.

  1. Create a new Start9 VM disk on the 2TB SSD:

    • In the Proxmox web UI, select your Start9 VM (or create a new one if desired).
    • Go to the Hardware tab, click Add → Hard Disk.
    • Select the new 2TB LVM pool (not LVM-Thin) as the storage location, set the disk size (e.g., 1900GB or slightly less than the available space), and choose the disk type (scsi or virtio recommended).
    • Remove any old VM disks from the 1TB SSD (detach and delete in the Hardware tab).

🛑 Troubleshooting: Insufficient Free Space When Creating VM Disk

If you see an error like TASK ERROR: lvcreate 'start9/vm-100-disk-0' error: Volume group "start9" has insufficient free space (476932 extents): 486400 required., this means your LVM volume group does not have enough free space for the requested disk size. To resolve:

  1. Double-check the size of your LVM pool in Proxmox (Datacenter → Node → LVM). If it’s smaller than your SSD, you may need to:
    • Delete and recreate the volume group with a larger size.
    • Remove any unused logical volumes or snapshots.
  2. Make sure the volume group (VG) uses the full SSD capacity.
  3. When creating the VM disk, set the size to slightly less than the available space (e.g., 1800GB for a 2TB SSD, not 1900GB).
  4. If needed, destroy and recreate the volume group to use the full disk.

For more details, see Proxmox LVM documentation.

  1. Mount the Start9 OS ISO and reinstall:

    • In the Hardware tab, add a CD/DVD Drive if not already present, and select the Start9 OS ISO image.
    • Go to the Options tab, set the Boot Order to prioritize the CD/DVD drive.
    • Start the VM and boot from the ISO to begin the Start9 OS installation.

  2. Restore your backup and verify:

    • After installing Start9 OS, log in to the web interface and restore your backup if you created one.
    • Go to System → Monitor → Disk to confirm the full 2TB is available for OS, apps, and data.
    • Reinstall or restore Bitcoin Core and other apps as needed.

👉 Next: Installing Mempool Explorer

Installing Mempool Explorer

Mempool Explorer lets you visualize Bitcoin transactions, blocks, and mempool activity in real time. It is the perfect companion to your Bitcoin Core node on Start9 OS.

In this guide, you will:

  • Access the Start9 Marketplace
  • Install Mempool Explorer
  • Configure backend connection
  • Access the Mempool web interface

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Bitcoin Core installed and fully synchronized
  • Start9 OS running and accessible via web interface
  • Master password set during initial setup

Hardware

  • Start9 OS device (native or Proxmox VM)
  • MacBook or desktop for access

Software

  • Web browser (Safari, Chrome, Firefox, etc.)

References

  • Mempool Documentation

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Access Start9 Marketplace

Mempool Explorer is available in the Start9 Marketplace. To begin:

  1. Open your web browser
  2. Navigate to your Start9 web interface:
    • Local access: https://xxx-yyy.local (replace with your device name)
    • IP access: https://192.168.1.101 (use your Start9 IP address)
  3. Log in with your Start9 master password

🌐 Marketplace Help: If you don’t see Mempool Explorer, ensure you’re connected to the internet and refresh the marketplace.

Step 2: Install Mempool Explorer

  1. Click on Marketplace in the left sidebar
  2. Search for “Mempool” in the search bar
  3. Locate Mempool Explorer in the results
  4. Click on Mempool Explorer to open the service details
  5. Review the service description and requirements
  6. Click Install button
  7. Wait for the download and installation to complete (~2-5 minutes)

Step 3: Configure Mempool Explorer

After installation completes:

  1. Go to Services in the sidebar
  2. Click on Mempool Explorer
  3. Click the Configure button (or Menu → Config) to configure the service (see below).
  1. Ensure your Bitcoin Core node is selected as the backend in the dependencies section.

🔗 Backend Connection:

  • Mempool Explorer requires a running Bitcoin Core node. If Bitcoin Core is not detected, make sure it is installed, fully synced, and running.
  • If you have multiple Bitcoin nodes, select the correct one in the backend dropdown.
  1. Review the highlighted Bitcoin Core configuration requirements for Mempool Explorer:
    • Pruning: Pruning must be fully disabled (not “Automatic”). Transaction Index (txindex) cannot be enabled unless pruning is off.
    • Transaction Index (txindex): Must be enabled (On) so Mempool Explorer can access the full transaction database. This option will only be available if pruning is disabled.
    • ZeroMQ: Must be enabled (On) to allow real-time updates from Bitcoin Core to Mempool Explorer.

🗄️ Pruning & Transaction Index:

  • You cannot enable Transaction Index (txindex) unless pruning is fully disabled. If your Start9 interface only shows “Automatic” for pruning, you will not be able to use Mempool Explorer until you can set pruning to “Disabled” (no pruning).
  • Start9 automatically checks whether you have enough storage for the full Bitcoin blockchain; if space is insufficient, the “Disabled” option will not appear. Free up space or add storage, then reopen the settings to see the disabled/no-pruning option.
  • If you cannot disable pruning, Mempool Explorer will not function correctly.

Step 4: Access Mempool Web Interface

  1. In the Services list, click Mempool Explorer
  2. If the service shows Stopped, click Start and wait for it to reach Running
  3. Click the provided link to open the Mempool web interface
  4. Explore mempool transactions, blocks, and fee estimates

Troubleshooting

Mempool web UI does not open after clicking Launch UI — URL is https://xxx-yyy.local:

Start9 serves service web UIs at a .local mDNS hostname (e.g., https://abc123-xyz456.local), not at the IP address you use to reach the admin panel. The browser has to resolve that hostname separately via mDNS (Bonjour) and trust the Start9 Root CA certificate for it. Two things can go wrong:

Step 1 — Check whether your MacBook can resolve the .local hostname:

ping -c 3 abc123-xyz456.local
  • Ping succeeds → mDNS is working; the problem is the certificate (go to Step 2)
  • Ping fails → mDNS is not working (Start9 likely does not run avahi-daemon); use the static hosts entry fix below

Fix for ping failure — add a static hosts entry on your MacBook:

echo "192.168.1.101 abc123-xyz456.local" | sudo tee -a /etc/hosts

Replace abc123-xyz456.local with your actual Start9 hostname (visible in the URL when Launch UI opens a tab). Then verify:

grep abc123 /etc/hosts
ping -c 3 abc123-xyz456.local

Ping should now resolve to 192.168.1.101. macOS checks /etc/hosts before mDNS for .local names, so this bypasses mDNS entirely. This is the recommended long-term fix — it is static, survives reboots, and does not depend on Start9’s mDNS configuration.

If you access Start9 from another device (a second Mac, etc.), add the same hosts entry on that device too.

Step 2 — Install the Start9 Root CA on your MacBook:

The Start9 Root CA is the certificate authority that signs all .local HTTPS certificates for your node. Without it, the browser will refuse to load any https://xxx-yyy.local address (including service UIs).

Follow the Root CA installation steps in Securing Start9 OS — Step 3, Option 1. After installing and trusting it in Keychain, restart your browser — the Mempool UI at https://abc123-xyz456.local will load without warnings.

Why accessing via IP works but .local does not: The certificate issued for abc123-xyz456.local does not cover 192.168.1.101. The browser treats them as completely different identities. Installing the Root CA resolves this for all .local addresses in one step — you will not need to accept certificate warnings again for any Start9 service.

Mempool shows “loading” or stale data:

  • Confirm Bitcoin Core is fully synced (100%) in the Start9 Services list
  • Confirm Bitcoin Core has txindex enabled and pruning disabled (required — see Step 3)
  • Confirm Bitcoin Core has ZeroMQ enabled (required for real-time updates)
  • Restart Mempool Explorer: Services → Mempool Explorer → Stop → Start

Mempool service shows “Needs Config” or dependency error:

  • Go to Services → Mempool Explorer → Configure and confirm Bitcoin Core is selected as the backend
  • If Bitcoin Core’s txindex or ZeroMQ settings changed, re-save its configuration and restart Bitcoin Core first, then restart Mempool

Mempool service stays in “Starting” state:

  • Bitcoin Core must be fully synced before Mempool can initialize. Wait for Bitcoin Core to reach 100% sync.

Service log shows “Could not connect to https://api.bitfinex.com/v1/pubticker/BTCJPY”:

  • This is non-critical log noise. Mempool fetches live Bitcoin price data from several external APIs for currency conversion display. Bitfinex’s v1 API (/v1/pubticker/) is deprecated and unreliable — it is frequently rate-limited, geo-blocked, or simply unavailable.
  • The core Mempool functionality — block explorer, transaction lookup, mempool fee estimates, and real-time visualization — is completely unaffected. Only the BTC/JPY price display may be missing or stale.
  • No action is required. The outbound firewall Rule 11 (TCP 443) already permits this connection — the failure originates from Bitfinex’s servers, not your firewall.

👈 Prev: Installing Bitcoin Core

👉 Next: Installing Electrum Server

Installing Electrum Server

Electrum Server (Electrs) enables you to connect Bitcoin wallets like Electrum and Sparrow directly to your own Bitcoin Core node. This provides privacy, speed, and complete control over your wallet infrastructure without relying on third-party servers.

In this guide, you will:

  • Access the Start9 Marketplace
  • Install Electrs service
  • Configure Electrs to connect to Bitcoin Core
  • Start Electrs and monitor indexing progress
  • Connect your Bitcoin wallets to your Electrum server

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Bitcoin Core installed and fully synchronized
  • Start9 OS running and accessible via web interface
  • Master password set during initial setup
  • Additional 80-100GB storage for Electrum index
  • 4-8 hours available for initial indexing

Hardware

  • Start9 OS device (native or Proxmox VM)
  • MacBook or desktop for access
  • Sufficient disk space (~580GB total for Bitcoin + Electrs)

Software

  • Web browser (Safari, Chrome, Firefox, etc.)
  • Electrum or Sparrow wallet (for connecting after installation)

References

  • Electrs Documentation
  • Electrum Wallet
  • Sparrow Wallet

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Access Start9 Marketplace

Electrs is available in the Start9 Marketplace. To begin:

  1. Open your web browser
  2. Navigate to your Start9 web interface:
    • Local access: https://xxx-yyy.local (replace with your device name)
    • IP access: https://192.168.1.101 (use your Start9 IP address)
  3. Log in with your Start9 master password

🌐 Marketplace Help: If you don’t see Electrs, ensure you’re connected to the internet and refresh the marketplace.

Step 2: Install Electrs

  1. Click on Marketplace in the left sidebar
  2. Search for “Electrs” or “Electrum Server” in the search bar
  3. Locate Electrs in the results
  4. Click on Electrs to open the service details
  5. Review the service description and requirements
  6. Click Install button
  7. Wait for the download and installation to complete (~2-5 minutes)

Step 3: Configure Electrs

After installation completes:

  1. Go to Services in the sidebar
  2. Click on Electrs
  3. Click the Configure button (or Menu → Config) to configure the service:
    • Log Filters: info (default) - Recommended to keep at default for normal operation. Set to debug only if troubleshooting indexing or connection issues.
    • Index Batch Size: 10 (default) - Recommended to keep at default unless experiencing memory constraints. Increase if you have plenty of RAM available.
    • Index Lookup Limit: Leave blank or set to 200 (recommended) - No default value. Setting a limit prevents large queries from consuming excessive resources; increase only if you have wallets with very large transaction histories (10,000+ transactions).
  1. Ensure your Bitcoin Core node is selected as the backend in the dependencies section

🔗 Backend Connection:

  • Electrs requires a running Bitcoin Core node. If Bitcoin Core is not detected, make sure it is installed, fully synced, and running.
  • If you have multiple Bitcoin nodes, select the correct one in the backend dropdown.
  1. Review the highlighted Bitcoin Core configuration requirements for Electrs:
    • Pruning: Pruning must be fully disabled (not “Automatic”). Electrs requires access to the full blockchain to build its index.
    • Transaction Index (txindex): Must be enabled (On) so Electrs can efficiently query transaction data.
    • ZeroMQ: Should be enabled (On) for real-time block notifications.

🗄️ Pruning & Transaction Index:

  • You cannot enable Transaction Index (txindex) unless pruning is fully disabled. If your Start9 interface only shows “Automatic” for pruning, you will not be able to use Electrs until you can set pruning to “Disabled” (no pruning).
  • Start9 automatically checks whether you have enough storage for the full Bitcoin blockchain; if space is insufficient, the “Disabled” option will not appear. Free up space or add storage, then reopen the settings to see the disabled/no-pruning option.
  • If you cannot disable pruning, Electrs will not function correctly.
  1. Click Save to apply the configuration

Step 4: Start Electrs and Monitor Indexing

  1. In the Services list, click Electrs
  2. If the service shows Stopped, click Start
  3. Electrs will begin building its index from the Bitcoin blockchain

⏱️ Indexing Time:

  • Initial indexing typically takes 4-8 hours depending on your hardware (CPU, disk I/O).
  • The service will show Starting or Syncing during this process.
  • You can monitor progress in the service logs or status page.
  • Do not interrupt the indexing process.
  1. Once indexing completes, the service status will change to Running

Step 5: Connect Your Bitcoin Wallets

After Electrs is running, you can connect Bitcoin wallets to your server:

Electrum Wallet

⚡ Electrum setup guide: See Installing Electrum Wallet section below for detailed installation steps.

  1. Open Electrum wallet on your computer
  2. Go to Tools → Network → Server
  3. From the server selection dropdown, choose Connect only to a single server (instead of the default “Auto-connect”)
  4. In Network → Server, set Server to the Quick Connect URL from Start9 (Electrs service → Menu → Properties → Quick Connect). This is a single URI that already combines host and port (no need to enter them separately). Onion format example: <your-onion>.onion:50001:t (e.g., abcdef.onion:50001:t).
  5. (macOS Tor users) Start the Tor service on your Mac, then go to Proxy tab and set SOCKS5 to 127.0.0.1 and Port 9050 to route Electrum over Tor
  1. Click Close to confirm
  2. Verify connection by the network icon in the bottom-right corner (blue = connected but still syncing or no history yet on a new wallet; green = fully connected and synchronized)

Sparrow Wallet

🐦 Sparrow setup guide: See Installing Sparrow Wallet section below for detailed installation steps.

  1. Open Sparrow wallet on your computer
  2. Go to Settings (macOS: Sparrow → Settings)
  3. Click on Server in the left sidebar
  4. Select Private Electrum from the Server Type dropdown
  5. In URL, enter the host and port separately: set Host to your onion address from Start9 (Electrs service → Menu → Properties → Hostname, e.g., abcdef.onion) and Port to the Electrs port from Start9 (Electrs service → Menu → Properties → Port, e.g., 50001). Leave Use SSL toggled off and Certificate blank.
  6. Toggle Use Proxy on, then set Proxy URL to Host 127.0.0.1 and Port 9050.
7. Click **Test Connection** button 8. If successful, click **Close** to save

Step 6: Verify Wallet Connection

  1. In your wallet (Electrum or Sparrow), check the network status icon
  2. Verify the wallet is syncing from your own Electrs server
  3. Check transaction history loads correctly
  4. Send a test transaction to confirm full functionality

✅ Success Indicators:

  • Wallet shows connected status (green icon = fully synced; blue icon = connected but still syncing)
  • Transaction history appears quickly
  • New transactions broadcast successfully
  • No third-party servers in connection log

Installing Electrum Wallet

Electrum is a lightweight Bitcoin wallet that connects to your Electrs server for fast, private transaction management.

Electrum System Requirements

  • macOS: 10.13 or later
  • Windows: 7 or later
  • Linux: Ubuntu 18.04 or later
  • Storage: ~100 MB available disk space

Electrum Installation

Step 1: Download Electrum Wallet

  1. Open your web browser on your desktop/laptop computer
  2. Visit the official Electrum website
  3. Click on the Download button
  4. Select the appropriate version for your operating system:
    • macOS: Download the .dmg file
    • Windows: Download the .exe installer
    • Linux: Download the .AppImage or use your package manager
  5. Wait for the download to complete

Step 2: Verify the Electrum Download (Optional but Recommended)

🔒 Signature Verification:

  • Signature verification is optional for most users but recommended for security-conscious Bitcoin holders
  • If verification fails, delete the downloaded file and try again from the official website

Verifying the signature ensures the file hasn’t been tampered with:

  1. On the Electrum website, locate the GPG Signatures section

  2. Download the signature file (.asc) corresponding to your version (e.g., electrum-4.6.2.dmg.asc)

  3. You’ll need Thomas Voegtlin’s official signing key fingerprint to verify the download:

    • Key fingerprint: 6694 D8DE 7BE8 EE56 31BE D950 2BD5 824B 7F94 70E6
    • This is the official key published on the Electrum website and GitHub repository
    • Verify it matches the published GitHub key:
    curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/spesmilo/electrum/master/pubkeys/ThomasV.asc -o ThomasV.asc
gpg --show-keys --fingerprint ThomasV.asc

    Ensure the fingerprint shown matches 6694 D8DE 7BE8 EE56 31BE D950 2BD5 824B 7F94 70E6.

  4. In your terminal, navigate to your Downloads folder:

    cd ~/Downloads

  5. Import the Electrum signing key from a keyserver using the fingerprint:

    gpg --keyserver keys.openpgp.org --recv-keys 6694D8DE7BE8EE5631BED9502BD5824B7F9470E6
gpg --import ThomasV.asc

  6. Verify the signature:

    gpg --verify electrum-4.6.2.dmg.asc electrum-4.6.2.dmg

    Replace 4.6.2 with your downloaded version number, or use wildcards:

    gpg --verify electrum-*.dmg.asc electrum-*.dmg

  7. Look for “Good signature from Thomas Voegtlin” in the output

ℹ️ About GPG Trust Warnings:

If you encounter gpg: Can't check signature: No public key for an additional fingerprint (e.g., AA0BC6824B397BBA99776E157ED8D82B37192688), import that specific key only if you can confirm it belongs to an official Electrum signer:

gpg --keyserver keys.openpgp.org --recv-keys AA0BC6824B397BBA99776E157ED8D82B37192688

Or use an alternative keyserver:

gpg --keyserver hkps://keyserver.ubuntu.com --recv-keys AA0BC6824B397BBA99776E157ED8D82B37192688

It’s sufficient to have a valid signature from a known Electrum signer (Thomas Voegtlin, SomberNight/ghost43, or Emzy) for the macOS .dmg. Unknown additional signatures can be ignored if at least one trusted signer validates the file.

Step 3: Install Electrum Wallet

macOS:

  1. Double-click the downloaded .dmg file
  2. Drag the Electrum icon to the Applications folder
  3. Open Applications folder
  4. Double-click Electrum to launch
  5. Allow the app to run if prompted by security dialog

💻 Windows/Linux Install: For platform-specific installation steps, follow the official Electrum instructions for your OS. See the Electrum downloads page and your distribution’s package manager/docs for recommended methods.

Step 4: Initial Electrum Setup

When Electrum launches for the first time:

  1. Configure Network Connection - On the initial launch, Electrum will prompt you to configure network settings:
    • Leave both checkboxes unchecked (default):
      • Use proxy: Leave unchecked (unless you’re using Tor)
      • Select electrum server: Leave unchecked for now
    • Electrum will auto-connect to a public server initially
    • You’ll configure your own Electrs server connection after wallet creation
  1. Enter a Wallet Name (e.g., “My Bitcoin Wallet”)
  1. Choose Standard Wallet for single-signature wallets
  1. Select Create a new seed to generate a new recovery seed
  1. Write down your 12-word seed phrase and store it securely offline

⚠️ Seed Phrase Security:

  • Never share your seed phrase with anyone
  • Store the written seed phrase in a secure location (safe, safe deposit box)
  • Without the seed phrase, you cannot recover your wallet if your computer fails
  • Electrum never asks for your seed phrase after initial setup
  1. Confirm your seed by entering the words in the correct order
  1. Set a strong Password to encrypt your wallet file and click Finish
  1. On the version notification prompt, select Yes to receive version update notifications or No to skip

Step 5: Connect Electrum to Your Electrs Server

For detailed instructions on connecting Electrum to your Electrs server, refer to the Electrum Wallet section under Step 5: Connect Your Bitcoin Wallets above.

✅ Electrum is Ready:

  • Your wallet is now connected to your own Electrs server
  • You can receive Bitcoin addresses and send/receive transactions privately
  • Your transaction data remains on your server; no third-party servers are involved

Installing Sparrow Wallet

Sparrow Wallet is a privacy-focused Bitcoin wallet with advanced features for managing multiple wallets and hardware devices. It connects to your Electrs server for full control.

Sparrow System Requirements

  • macOS: 10.14 or later (Intel or Apple Silicon)
  • Windows: 7 or later
  • Linux: Ubuntu 18.04 or later
  • Java: Java Runtime Environment (JRE) 11 or later (usually bundled)
  • Storage: ~200 MB available disk space

Sparrow Installation

Step 1: Download Sparrow Wallet

  1. Open your web browser on your desktop/laptop computer
  2. Visit the official Sparrow Wallet website
  3. Click on Download or scroll to the Downloads section
  4. Select the appropriate version for your operating system:
    • macOS: Download the .dmg file (choose Intel or Apple Silicon as appropriate)
    • Windows: Download the .exe installer
    • Linux: Download the .tar.gz file or use your package manager
  5. Wait for the download to complete (~100-150 MB)

Step 2: Verify the Sparrow Download (Optional but Recommended)

Verifying the signature ensures the file hasn’t been tampered with:

  1. On the Sparrow website, locate the Checksum or PGP Signature section

  2. Download the signature file (.asc) and checksum file

  3. In your terminal, navigate to your Downloads folder:

    cd ~/Downloads

  4. Verify the .dmg checksum against the manifest:

    shasum -a 256 -c sparrow-*.sha256

    If the download provides a manifest text file instead (for example, sparrow-2.3.1-manifest.txt), use that filename:

    shasum -a 256 -c sparrow-*-manifest.txt

    or

    shasum --check sparrow-*-manifest.txt --ignore-missing

    Expect to see an OK line for your .dmg file (for example, Sparrow-2.3.1-aarch64.dmg: OK). This confirms the .dmg file’s integrity matches what’s in the manifest.

  5. Import Craig Raw’s signing key and verify the PGP signature of the manifest file:

    To import directly from Craig Raw’s Keybase page (as linked on the Sparrow site):

    curl https://keybase.io/craigraw/pgp_keys.asc | gpg --import
gpg --fingerprint craigraw

    Verify the manifest signature (note: the .asc signs the manifest .txt file, not the .dmg directly):

    gpg --verify sparrow-*-manifest.txt.asc sparrow-*-manifest.txt

    Example using the current files:

    gpg --verify sparrow-2.3.1-manifest.txt.asc sparrow-2.3.1-manifest.txt

    Look for “Good signature from Craig Raw” in the output.

🚀 Alternate Sparrow in-app verification (Sparrow 1.8.3+):

If you have Sparrow 1.8.3 or later installed, you can verify directly in the app. Download these three files to the same folder (e.g., ~/Downloads):

  • Manifest Signature: sparrow-2.3.1-manifest.txt.asc
  • Manifest: sparrow-2.3.1-manifest.txt
  • Your install file from the list above (e.g., Sparrow-2.3.1-aarch64.dmg)

Then drag and drop any of these files onto Sparrow to open the Verify Download dialog (or open it from Tools → Verify Download). Sparrow will verify the download for you.

  1. Verification complete: The checksum in step 4 verified your .dmg file integrity, and the GPG signature in step 5 confirmed the manifest is authentic. Together, these steps prove your Sparrow download is correct and untampered.

🔒 Signature Verification:

  • Signature verification is optional for most users but recommended for security-conscious Bitcoin holders
  • If verification fails, delete the downloaded file and try again from the official website

Step 3: Install Sparrow

macOS:

  1. Double-click the downloaded .dmg file
  2. Drag the Sparrow icon to the Applications folder
  3. Open Applications folder
  4. Double-click Sparrow to launch (or right-click and select Open)
  5. Allow the app to run if prompted by security dialog

💻 Windows/Linux Install: If installation steps differ on your system, refer to the Sparrow downloads page and your OS/distribution documentation for the most up-to-date guidance.

Step 4: Initial Sparrow Setup

When Sparrow launches for the first time:

  1. Choose whether to configure a server now or select Later / Offline Mode. (You can safely pick later/offline and set your Electrs server after wallet creation.)
  1. Click New Wallet or File → New Wallet
  1. Enter a wallet name (e.g., “My Bitcoin Wallet”) and click Create Wallet

  1. Choose Settings for your new wallet:

    • Choose Policy Type: Single-Signature or Multi-Signature
    • Choose Script Type: for single-sig, use Native Segwit (P2WPKH); for multi-sig, use Native Segwit (P2WSH) or your preferred type
    • Review Script Policy (Descriptor): confirm it matches your selections (e.g., wpkh(Keystore1))
    • Select Keystores: choose one option — connected hardware wallet, airgapped hardware wallet, new or imported software wallet, or xpub/watch-only wallet
    • If using a software wallet, select New or Imported Seed

  1. Choose the seed type:

    • Mnemonic Words (BIP39): Industry standard method; recommended for most users
    • Master Private Key (BIP32): Single master private key (advanced users)
    • Mnemonic Shares (SLIP39): Shamir secret sharing for distributed backup (advanced)
  1. If you selected Mnemonic Words (BIP39), choose word count (12 words recommended for standard security; 24 words for enhanced security)
  2. Click Generate New to create a new seed phrase
  1. Write down your seed phrase and store it securely offline

⚠️ Seed Phrase Security:

  • Never share your seed phrase with anyone
  • Store the written seed phrase in a secure location (safe, safe deposit box)
  • Without the seed phrase, you cannot recover your wallet if your computer fails
  • Sparrow never asks for your seed phrase after initial setup
  • Consider using a hardware wallet (Ledger, Trezor) for large amounts

  1. Confirm your seed by clicking Confirm Backup… and then Re-enter Words

  2. Enter the words in the correct order and click Create Keystore

  3. Review the Keystore Import dialog:

    • Derivation Path (default): m/84'/0'/0' (Native Segwit; this is the default Account 0 — standard for most wallets)
    • Accept the default path or modify if needed for specific use cases (e.g., different accounts)
    • Click Import Keystore to complete

  1. Review the Keystore Settings dialog that appears:

    • Type: Software Wallet
    • Label: BIP39 (or your custom label)
    • Master Fingerprint: Unique identifier for your seed phrase
    • Derivation: m/84'/0'/0' (your chosen derivation path)
    • xpub / zpub: Extended public key for address generation (shown for reference)
    • Click Apply to confirm and save keystore settings

  2. Set a strong Password to encrypt your wallet file

  1. Click Create Wallet

Step 5: Connect Sparrow to Your Electrs Server

For detailed instructions on connecting Sparrow to your Electrs server, refer to the Sparrow Wallet section under Step 5: Connect Your Bitcoin Wallets above.

✅ Sparrow is Ready:

  • Your wallet is now connected to your own Electrs server
  • You can receive Bitcoin addresses and send/receive transactions privately
  • Your transaction data remains on your server; no third-party servers are involved
  • Sparrow offers advanced features like multi-sig wallets and hardware wallet integration
