Installing Nostr RS Relay

This guide provides step-by-step instructions to install and configure a private Nostr RS Relay on your Start9 OS instance. This relay is intended for personal and family event usage, allowing you to keep important data decentralized, private, and backed up across trusted members by leveraging the Nostr network for resilient, censorship-resistant storage.

In this guide, you will:

  • Access the Start9 Marketplace
  • Install the Nostr RS Relay service
  • Configure relay settings for private/family use
  • Connect Nostr clients (Damus, Amethyst, Snort, etc.)
  • Set up authentication and access controls
  • Manage relay data and backups
  • Monitor relay activity

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Start9 OS installed and accessible via web interface
  • Initial Start9 setup wizard completed
  • Master password set during initial setup

Hardware

  • Start9 OS device (native or Proxmox VM)
  • MacBook or desktop for access

Software

  • Start9 OS running
  • Web browser (Safari, Chrome, Firefox, etc.)
  • Nostr client (Damus, Amethyst, Snort, etc.)

References

  • Start9 Guide
  • Nostr Official Website
  • Damus Official Website
  • NostrApps Directory (community resource)

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Access Start9 Marketplace

Nostr RS Relay is available in the Start9 Marketplace. To begin:

  1. Open your web browser
  2. Navigate to your Start9 web interface:
    • Local access: https://xxx-yyy.local (replace with your device name)
    • IP access: https://192.168.1.101 (use your Start9 IP address)
  3. Log in with your Start9 master password

🌐 Marketplace Help: If you don’t see Nostr RS Relay, ensure you’re connected to the internet and refresh the marketplace.

Step 2: Install Nostr RS Relay

  1. Click on Marketplace in the left sidebar
  2. Search for “Nostr RS” in the search bar
  3. Locate Nostr RS Relay in the results
  4. Click on Nostr RS Relay to open the service details
  5. Review the service description and requirements
  6. Click Install button
  7. Wait for the download and installation to complete (~2-5 minutes)

Step 3: Configure Nostr RS Relay for Private/Family Use

After installation completes:

  1. Go to Services in the sidebar
  2. Click on Nostr RS Relay
  3. Click the Configure button (or Menu → Config) to configure the service.

Recommended settings for private/family relay:

  • Relay Type: Set to Private (only allow connections from trusted users; default is Public)
  • Pubkey Whitelist (hex): Add the hex-encoded public keys for each family member who should have access (write/read). Only these pubkeys will be allowed to connect and publish events.

🔍 How to Find Your Pubkey: In your Nostr client (Damus, Amethyst, Snort, etc.), check your profile or account settings for your public key. If it shows as npub (NIP-19 format), use an online tool like damus.io/key or nostrcheck.me to convert npub to hex. Enter the hex-encoded pubkey in the whitelist field.

  1. Click Save to apply configuration

Step 4: Connect Nostr Clients

  1. In the Services list, click Nostr RS Relay
  2. If the service shows Stopped, click Start and wait for it to reach Running
  3. Copy the relay URL (e.g., wss://your-relay.local) from the service page
  4. In your Nostr client (Damus, Amethyst, Snort, etc.), add your relay URL
  5. Authenticate using your pubkey (if required)
  6. Test posting and reading events to ensure connectivity

Damus (iOS App) Setup Example:

  1. Download Damus from the App Store.
  2. Open Damus. On first launch, you can:
  • Create a new Nostr account (generates a new public/private key pair for you), or
  • Sign in with an existing account by importing your public key (npub/hex) for read-only, or your private key (nsec/hex) for full access (posting, DMs, etc.).
  • To import a key, tap “Import Key” and paste your nsec (private key) or npub (public key). For full functionality, use your nsec (private key). Keep your private key secure!
  1. Once signed in, tap the Settings (gear icon, top right).
  2. Tap Relays.
  3. Tap the + button to add a new relay.
  4. Enter your private relay URL (e.g., wss://your-relay.local) and save.
  5. Make sure your relay is enabled in the list (toggle switch on).
  6. You can now post and read events through your private relay.

You can still use other Nostr clients (Amethyst, Snort, etc.) by adding your relay URL in their settings.

📍 Alternative iOS App: You can also use Wherostr as an iOS Nostr client. It supports connecting to custom/private relays and offers a different interface and features for Nostr users. This recommendation is based on my own personal preference and the #siamstr community.

Step 5: Set Up Data Backup and Decentralized Redundancy

Each family member can run their own private Nostr RS Relay, backing up important data/events. This creates a decentralized mesh of trusted relays:

🗄️ Backup Tip: Nostr RS Relay can be included in your Start9 system backups. When configuring backups (see securing_start9_vm.md), be sure to select the Nostr RS Relay app as part of your services to back up. This ensures your relay data is included in your regular Start9 encrypted backups.

  • Enable regular backups in the Start9 Nostr RS Relay settings (download encrypted backups to your computer or external drive)
  • Share backup files with trusted family members for offsite redundancy
  • Optionally, cross-post important events to each family member’s relay for multi-site preservation. To do this, each family member should run their own Nostr RS Relay (e.g., via Start9) and add the other members’ public keys to their relay’s whitelist. This allows everyone to publish and read important events across all trusted relays, increasing redundancy and accessibility.
  • Restore from backup if needed using the Start9 web interface

🛡️ Why Decentralized Backups?

By running multiple private relays and sharing backups, your family ensures that no single device failure or loss can destroy your important data. This provides eternal, private, and resilient data storage.

Step 6: Monitor and Maintain Your Relay

  1. Use the Status section or Logs menu in the Nostr RS Relay service to monitor activity
  2. Review connected clients and recent events
  3. Periodically test backups and restore procedures
  4. Update authentication keys as family members join/leave

Advanced: Custom Policies and Lightning Zaps (Optional)

  • Enable Lightning zaps if you wish to support micro-payments or tips between family members (requires Lightning integration)
  • Set custom event filters, rate limits, or moderation policies as needed

👉 Next: Local AI Agents

Local AI Agents

Running a Bitcoin node establishes financial sovereignty. This section extends that same principle to AI tooling: instead of routing every prompt through a commercial cloud, open-source language models run locally on the same Minisforum hardware — in a dedicated virtual machine isolated from the Bitcoin node by firewall rules, with no path to keys or node processes without an explicit grant.

The stack documented here pairs Ollama (a local model server) with Roo Code in VS Code on a MacBook. Claude Code — already in use as a Pro subscriber — acts as the senior engineering agent that reviews and approves final output. Open-source models handle the bulk of the work (architecture planning, drafting, refactoring, test generation); the high-quality cloud agent does the final pass.

These are the tools used on this journey — not universal recommendations. Every tool here — Minisforum, Proxmox, Start9, Ubuntu, Ollama, Roo Code, Claude Code — reflects the specific hardware and preferences documented in this book. The principles carry over to any equivalent stack. See the alternatives table below for a quick map of popular options at each layer.

In this section:

  • Rationale and architecture for a local, sovereign AI development stack
  • Model and tooling selection for a multi-agent coding workflow
  • Provisioning a dedicated AI VM on Proxmox, isolated from the Bitcoin node VM
  • Installing Ollama and pulling code-focused open-source models
  • Configuring Roo Code in VS Code with distinct model profiles per agent role
  • Wiring up Claude Code as the senior supervising agent

Architecture Overview

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                  Minisforum X1-255 (Proxmox VE Host)                    │
│                                                                         │
│  ┌──────────────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐ │
│  │   Bitcoin Node VM         │   │         AI Agents VM                │ │
│  │   (Start9 OS)             │   │   (Ubuntu 24.04 LTS)                │ │
│  │                           │   │                                     │ │

👉 Next: Choosing the AI Stack

Choosing the AI Stack

Choosing an AI development stack is a statement about values as much as a technical decision. This page documents why I chose open-source model serving (Ollama), a multi-agent VS Code extension (Roo Code), and Claude Code as the senior supervising agent — and why this combination fits a Bitcoiner and Rustacean mindset.

In this guide, you will:

  • Understand why local model serving aligns with self-sovereignty principles
  • Evaluate model serving options and choose Ollama
  • Select code-focused open-source models appropriate for CPU-only inference
  • Compare VS Code AI extensions and understand why Roo Code fits an orchestrator workflow
  • Define Claude Code’s role as the senior agent in the pipeline

Why Local AI Models?

Running AI models locally follows the same reasoning as running your own Bitcoin node: you eliminate dependency on a third party for a resource you need to control.

With a cloud-only AI setup:

  • Every prompt leaves your machine — code context, architecture decisions, internal notes
  • Availability depends on the provider’s uptime and pricing
  • Rate limits and token costs constrain exploratory usage
  • The provider can change terms, raise prices, or deprecate models

With a local model setup on the same hardware that runs your Bitcoin stack:

  • Prompts never leave your LAN for routine tasks
  • No per-token cost for local inference (electricity only)
  • Always available, even offline
  • Models are files you own and can pin to specific versions
  • The cloud (Claude Code) is reserved for high-value final review, keeping cloud costs low

The tradeoff is inference speed. The Minisforum X1-255 has no dedicated GPU, so inference runs on CPU. A 7B-parameter model produces responses in roughly 10–30 seconds — acceptable for background agents handling drafts and refactoring, not suitable for real-time autocomplete. I use local models for agentic tasks (multi-step file editing) and Claude for interactive review.

Comparing Model Serving Options

FeatureOllamaLM Studiollama.cpp (direct)
Target audienceDevelopers, CLI usersGUI-focused usersDevelopers, advanced
InterfaceCLI + REST APIGUI + REST APICLI only
OpenAI-compat APIYes (:11434/v1)YesVia server flag
Model libraryBuilt-in pull (ollama.com)Built-in downloadManual GGUF download
Linux server supportFirst-classGUI required (not headless)First-class
Systemd serviceAutomatic at installNot availableManual
Active communityVery largeLargeLarge
LicenseMITProprietary (free tier)MIT

Chosen: Ollama

Ollama installs as a systemd service on Ubuntu, exposes an OpenAI-compatible REST API, and includes a built-in model registry. It starts automatically on boot, recovers from crashes, and is the easiest path to a headless model server on a Proxmox VM. LM Studio requires a GUI (not viable on a headless Ubuntu VM). Direct llama.cpp is powerful but requires more manual setup with no service management.

Choosing Code-Focused Models

Rust is a harder target

Rust taxes AI models in ways that most languages do not: lifetime annotations, borrow checker enforcement, trait bounds, async runtimes, and unsafe rules all require a level of structured reasoning that smaller models handle inconsistently. No local model currently matches Claude Sonnet/Opus 4.6 on complex Rust, but the gap is narrowing — particularly with 14B+ models on reasoning-heavy tasks. The practical approach is to lean on local models for drafting and boilerplate, and reserve Claude for lifetime errors, trait design, and final review.

Model comparison

For CPU-only inference on 16 GB RAM, the 7B–14B range offers the best quality-to-speed balance. Larger models are slower but produce meaningfully better Rust output.

ModelSize (q4)RAMRust qualitySpeed (CPU)Ollama tag
qwen2.5-coder:7b~4.7 GB~6 GBGood~10–20 tok/sqwen2.5-coder:7b
qwen2.5-coder:14b~8.7 GB~10 GBVery good~5–10 tok/sqwen2.5-coder:14b
qwen2.5-coder:32b~19 GB~21 GBStrongest local option~1–3 tok/sqwen2.5-coder:32b
deepseek-coder-v2:16b~9.1 GB~11 GBVery good~5–8 tok/sdeepseek-coder-v2:16b
deepseek-r1:7b~4.7 GB~6 GBGood (chain-of-thought helps)~10–18 tok/sdeepseek-r1:7b
phi4:14b~8.7 GB~10 GBGood~5–10 tok/sphi4:14b
llama3.3:70b~40 GB~43 GBVery good (needs GPU)impractical on CPUllama3.3:70b
llama3.1:8b~4.7 GB~6 GBDecent for planning~10–20 tok/sllama3.1:8b
codellama:7b~3.8 GB~5 GBFair (superseded)~12–22 tok/scodellama:7b

🖥️ RAM Planning: Ollama loads a model into RAM on first request and keeps it resident until an idle timeout (default 5 minutes). With 16 GB VM RAM and Ubuntu using ~1.5 GB, roughly 14.5 GB is available for models. qwen2.5-coder:7b and qwen2.5-coder:14b both fit comfortably. Running two models simultaneously is possible at 7B; at 14B it gets tight.

⬆️ If you add a GPU later: qwen2.5-coder:32b becomes the best practical local option for Rust. llama3.3:70b is also excellent but requires ~40 GB VRAM or a large unified-memory system.

Recommended starting set:

  • qwen2.5-coder:14b — primary code agent; best CPU-practical quality for Rust (upgrade from 7B if RAM allows)
  • qwen2.5-coder:7b — fallback when speed matters more than depth
  • llama3.1:8b — planning/architecture agent (stronger general reasoning than pure code models)

You can swap models at any time by changing the Ollama tag in Roo Code’s provider settings. The rest of the workflow stays the same.

Comparing VS Code AI Extensions

Several VS Code extensions support multi-model, multi-agent workflows. The distinguishing factor for this setup is whether the extension supports an Orchestrator mode — one agent that breaks a task into steps and delegates each step to a specialized sub-agent, potentially using a different model per sub-agent.

FeatureRoo CodeClineContinue.dev
Orchestrator modeBuilt-inNot built-inNot built-in
Per-mode model selectionYesPer-sessionPer-context (chat vs autocomplete)
Ollama supportYes (OpenAI-compat or native)YesYes
Anthropic supportYesYesYes
Agentic file editingYes (multi-step)Yes (multi-step)Limited
LicenseApache 2.0Apache 2.0Apache 2.0
VS Code marketplaceroo-cline.roo-clinesaoudrizwan.claude-devContinue.continue

Chosen: Roo Code

Roo Code’s built-in Orchestrator mode is a direct match for this workflow. The Orchestrator agent reads a task, breaks it into sub-tasks, and delegates each to a specialized agent — each potentially backed by a different model. Architect mode uses a planning-focused model; Code mode uses a code-focused model; the final review is routed to Claude via the Anthropic provider. This is precisely the pipeline described in the architecture overview.

Continue.dev is excellent for inline autocomplete and quick chat but is not designed for multi-step agentic task delegation. It complements Roo Code for autocomplete (where low latency matters) but is not the right tool for orchestration.

Claude Code’s Role as Senior Agent

I already run Claude Code as a Pro subscriber — which means I have access to Claude (Sonnet/Opus-class models) via the CLI, already integrated into my development workflow. Roo Code supports a Claude Code provider that proxies model requests through the locally installed claude CLI, consuming Pro subscription allowance rather than per-token API credits.

The approach I use:

  • Roo Code routes most tasks to local Ollama models (Code, Debug, all verification modes)
  • Roo Code’s Orchestrator and Architect modes use the Claude Code provider — decomposition and design require senior judgment that 7–8B models handle poorly
  • Roo Code’s Code Reviewer mode uses the Claude Code provider as the final gate — full-context review and commit approval before any code lands

This keeps Claude reserved for the three stages where it genuinely matters, while local models absorb the bulk of the token work at zero cloud cost.

💡 Flexibility: The architecture is intentionally model-agnostic. Any Roo Code agent mode can be pointed at a different Ollama model, an OpenAI-compatible endpoint, or a different cloud provider at any time. The agent roles (Orchestrator, Architect, Code, Reviewer) are defined by their system prompts and tool access, not by the model powering them. Swap models freely as better open-source options become available.

Decision Summary

ComponentChosen ToolReason
Model serverOllamaHeadless Linux service, OpenAI-compat API, large registry
Primary code modelqwen2.5-coder:14bBest CPU-practical Rust quality; falls back to 7b if RAM is tight
Planning modelllama3.1:8bStronger reasoning, pairs well with code-focused model
VS Code extensionRoo CodeBuilt-in Orchestrator mode, per-mode model selection
Senior agentClaude Code (Pro)Already licensed; used for Orchestrator, Architect, Code Reviewer
VM isolationProxmox VM-level firewallPrevents AI VM from accessing Bitcoin node or LAN devices

👈 Prev: Local AI Agents

👉 Next: Provisioning the AI VM on Proxmox

Provisioning the AI VM on Proxmox

The AI agents VM runs on the same Minisforum hardware as the Bitcoin node — but in a separate Proxmox virtual machine with firewall rules that block it from reaching the Bitcoin node VM, the router admin interface, or any other LAN device. The MacBook is the only machine explicitly allowed to connect to it.

This separation enforces the principle that AI agents have no implicit access to my infrastructure. Access must be granted deliberately, one rule at a time.

In this guide, you will:

  • Create a new Ubuntu 24.04 LTS VM in Proxmox with resources sized for Ollama inference
  • Complete Ubuntu installation and configure SSH access from your MacBook
  • Apply Proxmox VM-level firewall rules to isolate the VM from the rest of your LAN
  • Verify the isolation is working before proceeding to install Ollama

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Proxmox VE running and accessible at https://192.168.1.100:8006
  • SSH key already set up on your MacBook (created during Securing Proxmox VE)
  • Ubuntu 24.04 LTS ISO uploaded to Proxmox local storage, or internet access to upload it now
  • Your MacBook’s LAN IP address (check with ipconfig getifaddr en0 in Terminal)

Hardware

  • Minisforum X1-255 running Proxmox VE with at least 16 GB free RAM
  • The Bitcoin node Start9 VM running normally (you will verify isolation against it)

Software

  • Proxmox VE web interface (Safari, Chrome, or Firefox)
  • Ubuntu Server 24.04 LTS ISO (ubuntu.com/download/server)
  • SSH client (Terminal on macOS)

References

  • Ubuntu Server 24.04 LTS Release Notes
  • Proxmox VE Firewall Documentation

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Upload Ubuntu Server ISO to Proxmox

If you already have a Ubuntu Server ISO in Proxmox local storage, skip to Step 2.

  1. Download Ubuntu Server 24.04 LTS ISO on your MacBook:

    • Visit ubuntu.com/download/server
    • Download the latest Ubuntu Server 24.04 LTS ISO
    • File will be saved to ~/Downloads/ubuntu-24.04.x-live-server-amd64.iso

  2. Log in to the Proxmox web interface: https://192.168.1.100:8006

  3. Navigate to Datacenter → pve (your node) → local storage → ISO Images tab

  4. Click Upload, select the Ubuntu ISO, and wait for the upload to complete (~3–5 minutes)

Step 2: Create the AI Agents VM

  1. Click Create VM in the top right of the Proxmox web interface

  2. General tab — set VM identity:

    • VM ID: 101 (or next available; Start9 VM is typically 100)
    • Name: ai-agents
    • Leave Resource Pool and Start after created at defaults for now

  3. OS tab — select the Ubuntu ISO:

    • Use CD/DVD disc image file (iso)
    • Storage: local
    • ISO image: select ubuntu-24.04.x-live-server-amd64.iso
    • Guest OS Type: Linux
    • Version: 6.x - 2.6 Kernel (or latest available)

  4. System tab — leave all defaults:

    • Graphic card, Machine, BIOS, and SCSI Controller at defaults
    • Leave Qemu Agent unchecked (install it later if desired)

  5. CPU tab — allocate CPU cores:

    • Sockets: 1
    • Cores: 8
    • Type: host (enables CPU-specific optimizations, important for llama.cpp SIMD performance)

    🖥️ Core Allocation: The Minisforum X1-255 has 10 CPU cores. The Start9 VM uses 4, and Proxmox host reserves ~1–2. Allocating 8 cores to the AI VM is safe for most workloads; Proxmox overcommits CPU time so actual contention is low when the Bitcoin VM is idle.

  6. Memory tab — allocate RAM:

    • Memory (MiB): 16384 (16 GB)
    • Leave Minimum memory blank (no ballooning needed; Ollama needs stable RAM)

    💾 RAM Allocation: Start9 VM uses 6 GB, Proxmox host uses ~2 GB. With 32 GB total, allocating 16 GB to the AI VM leaves 8 GB of headroom — sufficient buffer for the host and future VMs.

  7. Disks tab — set up storage:

    • Bus/Device: SCSI 0
    • Storage: local-lvm
    • Disk size (GiB): 100
    • Cache: Default
    • SSD emulation: enable (check the box)
    • I/O thread: enable

    💽 Disk Sizing: 100 GB accommodates Ubuntu (~10 GB), several 7B Ollama models (~5–10 GB each), and working space. Each additional model you pull consumes 4–10 GB of disk.

  8. Network tab — connect to the main bridge:

    • Bridge: vmbr0 (same LAN bridge as the Start9 VM)
    • Model: VirtIO
    • Firewall: checked (enables Proxmox VM-level firewall — required for isolation steps below)
    • Leave MAC address as auto-generated

  9. Confirm tab — review, then click Finish

    Do not check “Start after created” yet — complete the ISO boot and install first by starting it manually from the VM view.

Step 3: Install Ubuntu Server

  1. In the Proxmox left sidebar, select your new ai-agents VM → click Console tab

  2. Click Start (▶) to power on the VM

  3. In the Console, you will see the Ubuntu Server installer boot menu:

    • Select Try or Install Ubuntu Server (first option, highlighted by default)
    • Press Enter

  4. Work through the Ubuntu Server installation wizard:

    Language: Select English (or your preference)

    Installer update: Choose Continue without updating (you will update after install)

    Keyboard: Select your layout

    Installation type: Select Ubuntu Server (not minimized)

    Network: DHCP is fine — Ubuntu will get an IP from your router automatically

    Proxy: Leave blank unless you use a proxy

    Ubuntu archive mirror: Leave as default (archive.ubuntu.com)

    Guided storage configuration:

    • Select Use an entire disk
    • Select the 100 GB virtual disk (the only available disk)
    • Leave Set up this disk as an LVM group checked
    • Proceed through the storage summary and confirm

    Profile setup — set your Ubuntu user:

    • Your name: (your name)
    • Server name: ai-agents
    • Username: ai (or your preferred username)
    • Password: strong password (20+ characters; store in your password manager)

    Ubuntu Pro: Skip (select Skip for now)

    SSH setup:

    • Check Install OpenSSH server
    • Leave Import SSH identity blank (you will copy your key manually)

    Featured server snaps: Deselect all (you will install only what you need)

  5. Installation runs for ~5–10 minutes. When complete, select Reboot Now

  6. When prompted, press Enter to eject the virtual CD (Proxmox handles this automatically for VMs)

  7. Ubuntu boots to a login prompt in the Console

Step 4: Copy Your SSH Key to the AI VM

  1. Find the AI VM’s IP address:

    • In Proxmox, select ai-agents → Summary tab → look for the IP under IPs
    • Or check your router’s DHCP leases

  2. From your MacBook Terminal, copy your existing SSH key:

    # Use the same key you set up for Proxmox access
ssh-copy-id -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub ai@192.168.1.XXX
# Replace XXX with the AI VM's IP

    Enter the Ubuntu password you set during installation when prompted.

  3. Test key-based login:

    ssh ai@192.168.1.XXX
# Should log in without a password prompt

  4. Assign a static IP via your router’s DHCP reservation for the AI VM’s MAC address. This prevents the IP from changing on reboot and ensures your VS Code and firewall rules stay valid.

    • Find the MAC address: from the Console or Proxmox ai-agents → Network tab
    • In your router admin interface, add a DHCP reservation for this MAC → assign a fixed IP (e.g., 192.168.1.101)
    • Reboot the AI VM and confirm it gets the reserved IP: ssh ai@192.168.1.101

🌐 Static IP Tip: Your router (AT&T BGW320) supports DHCP reservations under Home Network → IP Allocation. Pinning the AI VM to a fixed LAN IP is important: firewall rules and VS Code provider settings reference this IP by address, so it must not change.

Step 5: Configure VM-Level Firewall in Proxmox

This step applies firewall rules to the ai-agents VM itself — separate from the Datacenter-level firewall that protects Proxmox. These rules control what traffic the AI VM can send and receive.

Goal: The AI VM can be reached from your MacBook (for SSH and Ollama API) and can reach the internet (to pull models). It cannot reach the Bitcoin node VM, the router admin interface, or any other LAN device.

  1. In Proxmox, select ai-agents VM → Firewall tab → Options

  2. Double-click Firewall and set it to Yes to enable the VM-level firewall

  3. Set the default policies:

    • Double-click Input policy → set to DROP
    • Double-click Output policy → set to ACCEPT initially (you will restrict outbound next)

  4. Click Add to create inbound rules. Add these rules in order:

    Rule 1 — SSH from MacBook:
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: in
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Source: 192.168.1.XXX   ← your MacBook's static/reserved LAN IP
- Dest. port: 22
- Comment: SSH from MacBook

    Rule 2 — Ollama API from MacBook:
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: in
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Source: 192.168.1.XXX   ← your MacBook's LAN IP
- Dest. port: 11434
- Comment: Ollama REST API from MacBook

  5. Test that SSH still works from your MacBook after enabling the firewall:

    ssh ai@192.168.1.101
# Must succeed — if not, double-check the Source IP in Rule 1

  6. Now restrict outbound traffic to prevent the AI VM from reaching other LAN devices:

    Change Output policy to DROP, then add these outbound ACCEPT rules:

    Rule 3 — DNS (required for model downloads):
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: out
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: udp
- Dest. port: 53
- Comment: DNS resolution

    Rule 4 — Block HTTP/HTTPS to LAN (isolation):
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: out
- Action: DROP
- Protocol: tcp
- Destination: 192.168.1.0/24
- Dest. port: 80,443
- Comment: Block HTTP/HTTPS to LAN — prevents AI VM reaching Start9 or router admin

    Rule 5 — HTTP/HTTPS (required for Ollama model downloads):
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: out
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: tcp
- Dest. port: 80,443
- Comment: Internet HTTP/HTTPS for model downloads

    Rule 6 — NTP (required for clock sync):
- Enable: ☑
- Direction: out
- Action: ACCEPT
- Protocol: udp
- Dest. port: 123
- Comment: NTP time sync

    🛡️ Outbound Restriction: With Output policy DROP, Rule 4 explicitly drops TCP 80/443 to the entire LAN subnet (192.168.1.0/24) — this blocks the AI VM from reaching the Start9 Bitcoin node VM or the router admin page over HTTP/HTTPS. Rules 5 and 6 then allow those same ports to internet destinations, so Ollama can still pull models. Rule ordering matters: Proxmox evaluates rules top-to-bottom, first match wins, so the LAN DROP must appear before the broad internet ACCEPT.

  7. Verify isolation — from inside the AI VM, these should all fail:

    # Attempt to reach the Bitcoin node VM (Start9)
# Replace 192.168.1.102 with your Start9 VM's actual IP
ping 192.168.1.102
# Expected: no response (packets dropped)

curl http://192.168.1.102:80
# Expected: connection timed out (blocked by Rule 4)

# Verify internet still works (needed for model downloads)
curl -s https://ollama.com | head -5
# Expected: HTML content returned

Troubleshooting

SSH connection refused after enabling firewall:

  • The Input policy is DROP and Rule 1 may not be matching. Check: Enable is ☑, the Source IP exactly matches your MacBook’s LAN IP, and port is 22. Temporarily disable the firewall in Options → Firewall → No, fix the rule, re-enable.

Ollama API unreachable from VS Code:

  • Check Rule 2 is present and enabled. Verify the Source IP matches your MacBook. Confirm Ollama is actually listening on 0.0.0.0:11434 (covered in the next guide).

AI VM cannot pull models after outbound DROP policy:

  • Verify Rules 3 and 4 are present and enabled. Test with curl https://ollama.com from inside the VM. If DNS fails, check Rule 3 (port 53 UDP).

IP address changed after reboot:

  • Set up a DHCP reservation in your router for the AI VM’s MAC address as described in Step 4.

👈 Prev: Choosing the AI Stack

👉 Next: Installing Ollama and Models

Installing Ollama and Models

Ollama turns the AI VM into a local model server: it installs as a systemd service, exposes an OpenAI-compatible REST API on port 11434, and provides a simple CLI to pull and manage models. VS Code on my MacBook connects to this endpoint to run inference.

In this guide, you will:

  • Update Ubuntu and install prerequisites
  • Install Ollama and verify it starts as a systemd service
  • Configure Ollama to accept connections from your MacBook (not just localhost)
  • Pull code-focused open-source models
  • Test the REST API from your MacBook to confirm VS Code will be able to reach it

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • AI VM provisioned and SSH-accessible from your MacBook (ssh ai@192.168.1.101)
  • Proxmox VM firewall rules in place (from Provisioning the AI VM)
  • Outbound HTTP/HTTPS allowed (for downloading Ollama and models)

Hardware

  • AI VM with at least 8 GB free RAM and 50+ GB free disk space
  • MacBook on the same LAN to test the REST API

Software

  • Ubuntu Server 24.04 LTS (installed in the AI VM)
  • curl (pre-installed on Ubuntu)

References

  • Ollama GitHub repository
  • Ollama model library
  • Ollama REST API documentation

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Update Ubuntu

SSH into the AI VM from your MacBook:

ssh ai@192.168.1.101

Update package lists and upgrade installed packages:

sudo apt update && sudo apt upgrade -y

Install useful tools you will need:

sudo apt install -y curl wget htop

Step 2: Install Ollama

Ollama provides an official install script that sets up the binary, systemd service, and the ollama user automatically:

curl -fsSL https://ollama.com/install.sh | sh

The script:

  • Downloads the Ollama binary to /usr/local/bin/ollama
  • Creates a system user ollama that runs the service
  • Installs a systemd service unit at /etc/systemd/system/ollama.service
  • Starts the service and enables it on boot

Verify the service is running:

sudo systemctl status ollama

Expected output includes Active: active (running). If the service is not running:

sudo systemctl start ollama
sudo systemctl enable ollama

Check that Ollama is listening on localhost:

curl http://localhost:11434
# Expected: Ollama is running

Step 3: Configure Ollama to Listen on All Interfaces

By default, Ollama listens only on 127.0.0.1 (localhost). To let VS Code on your MacBook reach it over the LAN, you need to configure it to listen on 0.0.0.0.

  1. Edit the Ollama systemd service override:

    sudo systemctl edit ollama

    This opens a drop-in override file in your editor. Add the following content between the two comment lines:

    [Service]
Environment="OLLAMA_HOST=0.0.0.0:11434"

    Save and exit (Ctrl+O, Enter, Ctrl+X in nano).

  2. Reload systemd and restart Ollama:

    sudo systemctl daemon-reload
sudo systemctl restart ollama

  3. Verify Ollama is now listening on all interfaces:

    sudo ss -tlnp | grep 11434
# Expected: LISTEN  0  ...  0.0.0.0:11434  ...

🔒 Security Note: Ollama has no built-in authentication. It is only safe to expose on 0.0.0.0 because the Proxmox VM firewall (set up in the previous guide) restricts port 11434 to your MacBook’s IP only. Do not expose this port without the firewall rules in place.

Step 4: Pull Code-Focused Models

Pull the primary code model. This downloads the model weights to disk (~4.7 GB):

ollama pull qwen2.5-coder:7b

Download time depends on your internet connection. For a 500 Mbps connection, expect ~5–10 minutes.

Pull the planning/reasoning model (~4.7 GB):

ollama pull llama3.1:8b

List downloaded models to confirm:

ollama list

Expected output:

NAME                    ID              SIZE    MODIFIED
qwen2.5-coder:7b        ...             4.7 GB  just now
llama3.1:8b             ...             4.7 GB  just now

💾 Disk Usage: Each 7B model occupies roughly 4–5 GB of disk. With 100 GB allocated to the VM and Ubuntu using ~10 GB, you have space for 15–20 models. Pull only what you plan to actively use; ollama rm <model> removes a model when you no longer need it.

Step 5: Test Inference Locally

Run a quick test to confirm the model loads and produces output:

ollama run qwen2.5-coder:7b "Write a Rust function that reverses a string in place."

The first run loads the model into RAM (~5–10 seconds). You should receive a Rust code snippet in response. For CPU-only inference, expect the full response in 15–40 seconds for a short prompt.

Exit the interactive session with /bye or Ctrl+D.

Test the llama3.1:8b model:

ollama run llama3.1:8b "What are the key considerations when designing a multi-agent AI system?"

Step 6: Test the REST API from Your MacBook

From your MacBook Terminal (not the VM), verify that VS Code will be able to reach Ollama:

curl http://192.168.1.101:11434
# Expected: Ollama is running

Test a completion via the OpenAI-compatible API endpoint (the format Roo Code uses):

curl http://192.168.1.101:11434/v1/chat/completions \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{
    "model": "qwen2.5-coder:7b",
    "messages": [{"role": "user", "content": "Say hello in Rust"}]
  }'

Expected: a JSON response with a choices array containing a Rust hello-world snippet. If the request times out, check that the Proxmox VM firewall Rule 2 (Ollama API from MacBook, port 11434) is enabled and that the Source IP matches your MacBook.

Step 7: Configure Automatic Model Management (Optional)

By default Ollama keeps a model loaded in RAM for 5 minutes after the last request, then unloads it. You can tune this if you want models to stay resident longer or unload faster:

  1. Add the environment variable to your Ollama service override:

    sudo systemctl edit ollama

  2. Update the override to include both variables:

    [Service]
Environment="OLLAMA_HOST=0.0.0.0:11434"
Environment="OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE=10m"

    OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE=10m keeps models loaded for 10 minutes after the last request — useful when you are actively coding and want fast response on subsequent requests. Set to 0 to unload immediately after each response if you need to conserve RAM.

  3. Reload and restart:

    sudo systemctl daemon-reload
sudo systemctl restart ollama

Troubleshooting

ollama: command not found after install:

  • The install script places the binary at /usr/local/bin/ollama. Confirm: ls -la /usr/local/bin/ollama. If missing, re-run the install script.

Service fails to start (systemctl status ollama shows failed):

  • Check logs: sudo journalctl -u ollama -n 50. A common cause is a port conflict. Verify nothing else is on port 11434: sudo ss -tlnp | grep 11434.

REST API unreachable from MacBook (connection refused or timeout):

  • Verify OLLAMA_HOST=0.0.0.0:11434 is set in the override: sudo systemctl cat ollama | grep OLLAMA_HOST
  • Verify Ollama is listening: sudo ss -tlnp | grep 11434 should show 0.0.0.0:11434
  • Verify the Proxmox firewall Rule 2 is enabled with the correct MacBook IP

Model pull fails mid-download:

  • Run ollama pull <model> again — pulls are resumable

Very slow inference (>60 seconds per response for a 7B model):

  • CPU-only inference at 8 vCPUs should produce ~10–20 tokens/second for a 7B model. Check CPU allocation in Proxmox and ensure the host CPU type is set (enables SIMD optimizations). Also verify no other VM is pegging the CPU: htop on the Proxmox host.

👈 Prev: Provisioning the AI VM on Proxmox

👉 Next: Configuring VS Code Agent Extensions

Configuring VS Code Agent Extensions

With Ollama running on the AI VM and accessible from my MacBook, this page wires it into VS Code through Roo Code — a VS Code extension that supports an Orchestrator mode, per-mode model selection, and agentic file editing. Each mode is configured with a distinct local Ollama model matched to the role’s demands: a reasoning model for planning and design, a code-specialized model for implementation and debugging.

VS Code’s process cannot route directly to LAN IP addresses on macOS in some configurations. The solution is a persistent SSH tunnel: autossh forwards localhost:11434 on the MacBook through SSH to Ollama on the AI VM. Roo Code connects to localhost, which always works. Port 11434 does not need to be exposed on the LAN — only SSH (port 22) is used.

In this guide, you will:

  • Install the Roo Code extension in VS Code
  • Set up a persistent autossh tunnel from the MacBook to the AI VM
  • Pull the Ollama models needed for reasoning and planning roles
  • Add named Ollama API configurations in Roo Code (one per model)
  • Assign a specific model to each built-in agent mode (Orchestrator, Architect, Code, Debug, Ask)
  • Test each mode to verify it generates responses from the expected model

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Ollama running on the AI VM at 192.168.1.101 with SSH access from the MacBook
  • qwen2.5-coder:7b already pulled in Ollama (from Installing Ollama and Models)
  • VS Code installed on your MacBook
  • Homebrew installed on your MacBook

Software

  • VS Code (latest stable release)
  • Roo Code extension (roo-cline.roo-cline in VS Code marketplace)
  • autossh (brew install autossh)

References

  • Roo Code documentation
  • Ollama OpenAI-compatible API docs

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Install Roo Code

  1. Open VS Code on your MacBook

  2. Open the Extensions panel: Cmd+Shift+X

  3. Search for Roo Code

  4. Find the extension by Roo Code (publisher: roo-cline) and click Install

  5. After installation, a Roo Code icon (🦘) appears in the VS Code Activity Bar on the left sidebar

  6. Click the icon to open the Roo Code panel

Step 2: Set up the Autossh Tunnel

autossh runs as a macOS launchd agent, forwarding localhost:11434 on the MacBook to Ollama on the AI VM through SSH. It starts at login and reconnects automatically if the AI VM restarts or the network drops.

  1. Install autossh:

    brew install autossh

  2. Create the launchd agent plist:

    cat > ~/Library/LaunchAgents/com.local.ollama-tunnel.plist << 'EOF'
<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>
<!DOCTYPE plist PUBLIC "-//Apple//DTD PLIST 1.0//EN" "http://www.apple.com/DTDs/PropertyList-1.0.dtd">
<plist version="1.0">
<dict>
    <key>Label</key>
    <string>com.local.ollama-tunnel</string>
    <key>ProgramArguments</key>
    <array>
        <string>/opt/homebrew/bin/autossh</string>
        <string>-M</string>
        <string>0</string>
        <string>-N</string>
        <string>-L</string>
        <string>11434:localhost:11434</string>
        <string>-o</string>
        <string>ServerAliveInterval=60</string>
        <string>-o</string>
        <string>ServerAliveCountMax=3</string>
        <string>-o</string>
        <string>ExitOnForwardFailure=yes</string>
        <string>ai@192.168.1.101</string>
    </array>
    <key>RunAtLoad</key>
    <true/>
    <key>KeepAlive</key>
    <true/>
    <key>StandardErrorPath</key>
    <string>/tmp/ollama-tunnel.log</string>
</dict>
</plist>
EOF

  3. Load the agent:

    launchctl load ~/Library/LaunchAgents/com.local.ollama-tunnel.plist

  4. Verify it is running:

    launchctl list | grep ollama-tunnel
curl http://localhost:11434/v1/models

    The launchctl output should show a PID in the first column (not -). The curl should return a JSON list of Ollama models.

💻 Intel Mac: Replace /opt/homebrew/bin/autossh with /usr/local/bin/autossh in the plist.

🔄 Reloading after plist changes: launchctl unload ~/Library/LaunchAgents/com.local.ollama-tunnel.plist && launchctl load ~/Library/LaunchAgents/com.local.ollama-tunnel.plist

Step 3: Add the Ollama API Configurations

Roo Code stores provider settings as named API Configurations — each is a saved profile with a provider type, base URL, and model. Assigning different models to different modes requires one configuration per model. Create three configurations, all pointing at the same Ollama server:

  1. In the Roo Code panel, click the Settings gear icon (⚙️) in the top-right corner

  2. Go to API Configuration and create the first profile:

    • Configuration name: ollama-coder
    • Provider: OpenAI Compatible
    • Base URL: http://localhost:11434/v1
    • API Key: ollama (any non-empty string; Ollama does not validate keys)
    • Model: qwen2.5-coder:7b
    • Save

  3. Create the second profile:

    • Configuration name: ollama-reasoning
    • Provider: OpenAI Compatible
    • Base URL: http://localhost:11434/v1
    • API Key: ollama
    • Model: qwen3:8b
    • Save

  4. Create the third profile:

    • Configuration name: ollama-architect
    • Provider: OpenAI Compatible
    • Base URL: http://localhost:11434/v1
    • API Key: ollama
    • Model: deepseek-r1:8b
    • Save

🌐 Provider URL: Use http://localhost:11434/v1 — the autossh tunnel (Step 2) forwards this to Ollama on the AI VM. The /v1 suffix is required; Ollama’s OpenAI-compatible endpoint lives at /v1/chat/completions.

📦 Three configs, one server: All three configurations point at the same tunnel endpoint. Three named profiles is the correct pattern — Roo Code uses the configuration name to distinguish which model to invoke when switching modes.

Step 4: Pull the Reasoning Models

The Orchestrator and Architect roles use reasoning-focused models different from the coding-specialized model. Pull them on the AI VM before configuring Roo Code.

  1. SSH into the AI VM:

    ssh ai@192.168.1.101

  2. Pull the two reasoning models:

    ollama pull qwen3:8b
ollama pull deepseek-r1:8b

  3. Verify all three models are available:

    ollama list

    You should see qwen3:8b, deepseek-r1:8b, and qwen2.5-coder:7b in the output.

💡 Model roles: qwen3:8b handles orchestration and general reasoning with a built-in thinking mode. deepseek-r1:8b uses chain-of-thought reasoning before answering, making it well-suited for architectural design decisions. qwen2.5-coder:7b remains the dedicated coding model. llama3.1:8b is no longer needed and can be freed with ollama rm llama3.1:8b.

Step 5: Assign API Configurations to Each Mode

Each mode is assigned one of the named API configurations created in Step 3. The configuration determines which model that mode uses:

ModePurposeAPI ConfigurationModel
OrchestratorBreaking tasks into sub-tasks, delegatingollama-reasoningqwen3:8b
ArchitectSystem design, planning, breaking down tasksollama-architectdeepseek-r1:8b
CodeWriting and editing code filesollama-coderqwen2.5-coder:7b
DebugDiagnosing errors and tracing issuesollama-coderqwen2.5-coder:7b
AskQuestions and explanations, no file editsollama-reasoningqwen3:8b

Custom verification and specialist modes (Quality Guard, Code Reviewer, etc.) are added in the Multi-Agent Workflow with Local AI Agents guide.

To assign a configuration to each mode:

  1. Click the mode selector (dropdown at the top of the Roo Code panel showing the current mode name)

  2. Select a mode (e.g., Architect)

  3. In the mode settings, click the API Configuration dropdown

  4. Select ollama-architect for Architect (or the configuration shown in the table for each mode)

  5. Repeat for all remaining modes following the table above

🔄 Flexibility: To swap a model for a role, edit the API configuration rather than changing each mode individually. All modes sharing that configuration update automatically.

Step 6: Test Each Mode

Test the Code mode (Ollama qwen2.5-coder:7b):

  1. Switch to Code mode in the Roo Code panel

  2. Open a Rust project (or any code file) in VS Code

  3. Send a prompt: Add a unit test for this function

  4. Roo Code should show the model name (qwen2.5-coder:7b) in the response header

  5. You will see the response stream in with a latency of ~5–15 seconds for a short response (CPU inference)

Test the Architect mode (deepseek-r1:8b):

  1. Switch to Architect mode

  2. Send a prompt: I need to build a CLI tool in Rust that monitors Bitcoin mempool fees and sends ntfy notifications. Outline the architecture.

  3. You will see a visible reasoning pass as DeepSeek R1 works through the problem, followed by a structured design document

Test the Orchestrator mode (qwen3:8b):

  1. Switch to Orchestrator mode

  2. Send a prompt: I want to add a fee alert feature to my Bitcoin monitoring tool. Break this down into sub-tasks and identify which agent should handle each.

  3. The Orchestrator decomposes the task and produces a delegation plan with acceptance criteria. Prepend /no_think to the prompt for a faster response without the reasoning pass.

⏱️ Response Times: All models run locally on CPU, producing ~10–25 tokens/second. A 200-token response takes roughly 8–20 seconds. deepseek-r1:8b includes a reasoning pass that adds latency before the visible response begins — this is expected behavior, not an error.

Step 7: Configure VS Code Settings for Roo Code

Open VS Code User Settings (Cmd+,) and add/verify these settings for a smooth Roo Code experience:

{
  "roo-cline.autoApproveReadOnly": true,
  "roo-cline.autoApproveWrite": false,
  "roo-cline.autoApproveExecute": false
}
  • autoApproveReadOnly: true — Roo Code can read files without prompting you each time
  • autoApproveWrite: false — you approve every file write (recommended until you trust the workflow)
  • autoApproveExecute: false — you approve every shell command execution

🛡️ Approval Settings: Starting with write and execute approvals required gives you explicit control over what the AI agents change on your machine. As you grow comfortable with specific task types, you can selectively enable auto-approval for lower-risk actions.

Troubleshooting

Roo Code shows “connection refused”, “fetch failed”, or “connection error” for Ollama:

  • Verify the tunnel is running: launchctl list | grep ollama-tunnel — the first column must show a PID, not -
  • Test the tunnel: curl http://localhost:11434/v1/models — if this fails, the tunnel is down
  • Reload the tunnel: launchctl unload ~/Library/LaunchAgents/com.local.ollama-tunnel.plist && launchctl load ~/Library/LaunchAgents/com.local.ollama-tunnel.plist
  • Check the tunnel error log: cat /tmp/ollama-tunnel.log
  • Verify Ollama is running on the AI VM: ssh ai@192.168.1.101 "ollama list"
  • All three API configurations must use http://localhost:11434/v1 as the Base URL — not the AI VM’s LAN IP

Model name not found error:

  • The model name in Roo Code must exactly match what ollama list shows (e.g., qwen2.5-coder:7b, not qwen2.5-coder)
  • Run ollama list in the AI VM to get exact names

Responses cut off mid-generation:

  • Ollama may have hit a context length or output token limit. Try a shorter prompt or switch to a model with a larger context window.

👈 Prev: Installing Ollama and Models

👉 Next: Multi-Agent Workflow with Local AI Agents

Multi-Agent Workflow with Local AI Agents

With Ollama serving models on the AI VM and Roo Code configured in VS Code, this page builds the full autonomous multi-agent pipeline. A single prompt to Project Planner triggers the complete cycle — design, implementation, quality checks, testing, and final review — without further user input unless a clarification is needed. All agents are custom Roo Code modes backed by local Ollama models.

In this guide, you will:

  • Configure global behavioral rules that apply to every agent
  • Create eleven custom Roo Code modes with all six settings fields filled in
  • Understand how Project Planner delegates automatically to each agent via Roo Code’s new_task tool
  • Walk through a concrete task end-to-end across the full autonomous pipeline

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Ollama running on the AI VM with qwen2.5-coder:7b, qwen3:8b, and deepseek-r1:8b pulled
  • autossh tunnel active (localhost:11434 → AI VM port 11434) — set up in Configuring VS Code Agent Extensions
  • Roo Code installed with the three API configurations (ollama-coder, ollama-reasoning, ollama-architect) created (from Configuring VS Code Agent Extensions)

Software

  • Roo Code VS Code extension (roo-cline.roo-cline)

Pipeline Overview

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Autonomous Task Pipeline                            │
│              (one user prompt → full cycle)                      │
│                                                                   │
│  Phase 1 — Planning                                              │
│                                                                   │
│  PROJECT PLANNER (qwen3:8b)                                       │
│  └── Writes ACs, spawns full pipeline via new_task               │
│                                                                   │
│  Phase 2 — Design & Implementation                               │
│                                                                   │
│  SYSTEMS ARCHITECT (deepseek-r1:8b)                               │
│  └── Designs approach, file list, interfaces, error strategy      │
│                                                                   │
│  SOFTWARE DEVELOPER (qwen2.5-coder:7b)                            │
│  └── Implements code + inline tests                               │
│                                                                   │
│  BUG HUNTER (qwen2.5-coder:7b) — if errors after implementation   │
│  └── Diagnoses root cause, fixes, loops back (max 2 cycles)      │
│                                                                   │
│  + DOCS WRITER (qwen3:8b) — when docs needed                      │
│  + REFACTOR AGENT (qwen2.5-coder:7b) — when refactoring           │
│                                                                   │
│  Phase 3 — Verification                                          │
│                                                                   │
│  QUALITY GUARD (qwen3:8b)                                         │
│  └── fmt/clippy/build/audit + OWASP checklist                    │
│                                                                   │
│  TEST ENGINEER (qwen3:8b)                                         │
│  └── cargo test, flags failures and coverage gaps                 │
│                                                                   │
│  RELEASE REVIEWER (qwen3:8b)                                      │
│  └── Checks docs, CI, Dockerfile match code changes              │
│                                                                   │
│  UI REVIEWER (qwen3:8b) — if frontend changed                     │
│  └── Accessibility checklist: ARIA, WCAG AA contrast, UX         │
│                                                                   │
│  Phase 4 — Final Gate                                            │
│                                                                   │
│  CODE REVIEWER (deepseek-r1:8b)                                   │
│  └── Reviews all ACs, approves or blocks, triggers commit         │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Custom Mode Reference

ModeSlugAPI ConfigurationModel
Project Plannerproject-plannerollama-reasoningqwen3:8b
Systems Architectsystems-architectollama-architectdeepseek-r1:8b
Software Developersoftware-developerollama-coderqwen2.5-coder:7b
Bug Hunterbug-hunterollama-coderqwen2.5-coder:7b
Quality Guardquality-guardollama-reasoningqwen3:8b
Test Engineertest-engineerollama-reasoningqwen3:8b
Release Reviewerrelease-reviewerollama-reasoningqwen3:8b
UI Reviewerui-reviewerollama-reasoningqwen3:8b
Code Reviewercode-reviewerollama-architectdeepseek-r1:8b
Docs Writerdocs-writerollama-reasoningqwen3:8b
Refactor Agentrefactor-agentollama-coderqwen2.5-coder:7b

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Configure Global Behavior

Open Roo Code Settings → Custom Instructions for All Modes and paste the following. These rules apply to every agent in the pipeline:

Confidence rule: if confidence in a decision, approach, or interpretation is
below 95%, stop and ask before proceeding. Never guess on scope, architecture,
or ambiguous acceptance criteria.

Targeted reads: use offset and limit when reading files — never read an entire
file just to gather context. Read only the section relevant to the current task.

Concise output: keep reports to ≤10 bullets per section. No prose summaries.
Skip sections where nothing changed or no findings exist.

No re-reading: do not re-read a file already processed in the same session.

Commit format: single-line message ≤256 characters, imperative mood, no
Co-Authored-By lines. Stage files by name only — never git add -A or git add .

How to Add a Custom Mode

All eleven modes are created the same way:

  1. In the Roo Code panel, click the Settings gear icon (⚙️)

  2. Go to Modes → click Add Mode

  3. Fill in all six fields for each mode as shown in Steps 2–12 below:

    • Name and Slug — from the reference table above
    • Short Description — shown in the mode selector dropdown
    • Role Definition — the agent’s system prompt
    • When to Use — guidance that helps Project Planner pick the right mode
    • Available Tools — enable only what each role needs
    • Custom Instructions — mode-specific behavioral rules layered on top of global rules

  4. Set the API Configuration as shown in the reference table

🔑 Slug accuracy: Project Planner references other modes by slug in its new_task calls. Use the exact slugs from the table — a typo silently breaks automatic delegation to that agent.

Step 2: Project Planner

Name: Project Planner · Slug: project-planner · API Config: ollama-reasoning

Short Description: Orchestrates the full pipeline from a single user prompt

Role Definition:

You are the Project Planner — the single entry point for every coding task.
You receive one prompt from the user and run the full pipeline to completion
without asking for further input unless the task scope is genuinely unclear.

On receiving a task:
1. Write 3–6 acceptance criteria before doing anything else.
2. Run this pipeline in order using new_task, passing full context to each agent:
   - systems-architect: design the approach (skip only for trivial 1-line fixes)
   - software-developer: implement based on the design
   - bug-hunter: only if compiler errors or test failures are reported
   - quality-guard: always
   - test-engineer: always
   - release-reviewer: always (skip only if zero non-code files changed)
   - ui-reviewer: only if frontend files changed
   - code-reviewer: always — final gate before commit

When spawning each sub-task include:
  - The original user task verbatim
  - The acceptance criteria you wrote
  - The full output of every previous agent in the pipeline

Loop rules:
  - If quality-guard or test-engineer reports a Critical finding, re-spawn
    software-developer with the finding as input (max 2 loops before escalating
    directly to code-reviewer with the unresolved issue flagged)
  - If bug-hunter cannot resolve an error in 2 attempts, escalate to
    code-reviewer with the full error history

Ask the user for clarification ONLY when acceptance criteria cannot be written
without information the task did not provide (unknown API, ambiguous data model).
Never ask about implementation choices — delegate those downstream.

When to Use: When starting any new task from scratch; always the first agent in the cycle

Available Tools: ✓ Read Files · ✗ Edit Files · ✗ Run Commands · ✓ Use MCP

Custom Instructions:

Skip a pipeline phase if no files in that phase's scope changed this cycle —
signal the next agent directly rather than reporting a no-op.
Do not spawn ui-reviewer unless frontend files appear in Software Developer's
"Files changed" output.
Do not spawn bug-hunter unless an agent explicitly reports an error.

🔧 Orchestration tools: For automatic delegation, enable the new_task tool in this mode’s Tools settings — the same capability the built-in Orchestrator has. Without it, each agent must be triggered manually.

💭 Thinking mode: qwen3:8b reasons before responding. Prepend /no_think to your prompt for a faster response when task decomposition is straightforward.

Step 3: Systems Architect

Name: Systems Architect · Slug: systems-architect · API Config: ollama-architect

Short Description: Designs technical approach — no code written

Role Definition:

You are the Systems Architect. You receive a task and acceptance criteria and
produce a design document. You do not write production code.

Your output must include:
- Files to create or modify (exact paths relative to repo root)
- New types, structs, traits, or interfaces to define
- Data flow between modules
- Error handling strategy (which error types, where ? propagates to)
- Inline tests the Software Developer must write (describe what to test, not code)
- Any decision marked [RISK] that could affect security or correctness

Rules:
- Read existing source files before designing — do not invent interfaces that
  conflict with the current codebase
- Be specific about Rust types and trait bounds
- End with a one-paragraph summary the Software Developer can use as a brief

Output format: structured Markdown with headings for each section above.

When to Use: Before any implementation; when evaluating two implementation approaches; when documenting existing design before refactoring

Available Tools: ✓ Read Files · ✗ Edit Files · ✗ Run Commands · ✗ Use MCP

Custom Instructions:

Read only the relevant source sections using offset and limit — do not read
entire files. Ground every design decision in what the current code actually
does. Mark any decision that could affect security or cross-module interfaces
with [RISK].

🧠 Reasoning pass: deepseek-r1:8b shows its chain-of-thought before the design document appears — this is expected behavior, not an error.

Step 4: Software Developer

Name: Software Developer · Slug: software-developer · API Config: ollama-coder

Short Description: Implements code and inline tests from Architect’s design

Role Definition:

You are the Software Developer. You receive a design document from the Systems
Architect and implement it exactly.

Rules:
- Implement every file change listed in the design — do not skip items
- Write inline unit tests in #[cfg(test)] modules for all pure functions
- No .unwrap() in non-test code — use ? or match with explicit error handling
- No unsafe blocks without a // SAFETY: comment explaining the invariant
- Follow existing code style exactly — match indentation, naming, module layout
- Do not refactor or add features outside the scope of this task

After all edits, output exactly:
IMPLEMENTATION COMPLETE
Files changed: [comma-separated list]
Errors encountered: [none | description]

When to Use: After Systems Architect produces a design; when implementing a specific function or module

Available Tools: ✓ Read Files · ✓ Edit Files · ✓ Run Commands · ✗ Use MCP

Custom Instructions:

Hard gates before outputting IMPLEMENTATION COMPLETE (stop and report on any
failure — do not proceed to the next gate):
1. Write or update tests for every changed function
2. cargo fmt --all
3. cargo clippy --workspace -- -D warnings
4. cargo clippy --workspace --tests -- -D warnings
5. cargo build --workspace
6. cargo test --workspace

Additional code rules:
- Library crates: errors via #[derive(thiserror::Error)]; never expose anyhow
  in a public API
- Application crates: use anyhow::Result
- No blocking calls inside async fn — use tokio::task::spawn_blocking
- Use LSP (rust-analyzer) for rename and find-all-references operations;
  never use text search for cross-reference operations

✅ Approval prompts: Roo Code prompts you to approve each file write. Keep roo-cline.autoApproveWrite set to false until you trust the workflow for a specific task type.

Step 5: Bug Hunter

Name: Bug Hunter · Slug: bug-hunter · API Config: ollama-coder

Short Description: Diagnoses and fixes errors from exact compiler or test output

Role Definition:

You are the Bug Hunter. You receive exact error output — compiler errors, test
failures, or runtime panics — and produce targeted fixes.

Rules:
- Read the error output character by character — never guess the cause
- Fix root causes, not symptoms — if errors cascade, fix the first one only,
  then re-run to see what remains
- Do not refactor, rename, or add features — only fix the reported errors
- For borrow checker errors: explain the ownership violation before fixing it
- For test failures: reproduce the failure logic mentally before changing code

After fixing, output:
BUG FIX COMPLETE
Root cause: [one sentence]
Files changed: [list]
Remaining errors: [none | list]

When to Use: When compiler errors, test failures, or runtime panics are reported after implementation

Available Tools: ✓ Read Files · ✓ Edit Files · ✓ Run Commands · ✗ Use MCP

Custom Instructions:

Fix one error at a time — fix the first, re-run, then address remaining errors.
Do not change anything outside the direct call chain of the reported error.
Use LSP rename and find-references for any symbol operations — never text search.

Step 6: Quality Guard

Name: Quality Guard · Slug: quality-guard · API Config: ollama-reasoning

Short Description: Runs fmt/clippy/build/audit and OWASP security checklist

Role Definition:

You are the Quality Guard. Run this checklist in order and report all findings.

Commands to run:
1. cargo fmt --all
2. cargo clippy --workspace -- -D warnings
3. cargo build --workspace
4. cargo audit

Security checklist (inspect changed files):
- Any secrets, tokens, or private keys in log:: or tracing:: calls?
- Input validated at all HTTP handlers and CLI entry points?
- Any new unsafe blocks without // SAFETY: comments?
- Any .unwrap() in non-test code?

Output format — one line per check:
[PASS] description
[CRITICAL] description
[MEDIUM] description

Append a final summary line:
VERDICT: PASS
or
VERDICT: CRITICAL — [brief reason]
or
VERDICT: MEDIUM — [brief reason]

When to Use: After every Software Developer or Bug Hunter session; before running Test Engineer

Available Tools: ✓ Read Files · ✓ Edit Files · ✓ Run Commands · ✗ Use MCP

Custom Instructions:

Run commands in exact sequence — stop and report if any step fails; do not
run subsequent steps after a compile or lint failure.
Audit any new unsafe blocks manually — do not rely on clippy for unsafe review.
Critical findings block the pipeline and route back to Software Developer.
Medium findings are non-blocking — list them after VERDICT: PASS.

⚡ Critical vs Medium: Critical = compile failure, CVE with available fix, secret in a log call. Medium = unmaintained dependency, stale lint suppression, missing input validation.

Step 7: Test Engineer

Name: Test Engineer · Slug: test-engineer · API Config: ollama-reasoning

Short Description: Runs tests and reports failures and coverage gaps — does not write tests

Role Definition:

You are the Test Engineer. Run the test suite and report results. You do not
write tests — you report on what the Software Developer produced.

Commands to run:
1. cargo test --workspace 2>&1

Then review which modules changed in this task and check whether tests exist
for the new or modified functions.

Output:
- Test results: [X passed, Y failed, Z ignored]
- Failed tests: [exact test name and failure message for each]
- Coverage gaps: [functions added or changed with no corresponding test]

A test failure is a hard block. Report:
VERDICT: BLOCKED — [failed test names]

No failures:
VERDICT: PASS
Coverage gaps are non-blocking — list them after VERDICT: PASS.

When to Use: After Quality Guard passes; before Release Reviewer

Available Tools: ✓ Read Files · ✗ Edit Files · ✓ Run Commands · ✗ Use MCP

Custom Instructions:

Do not write, edit, or suggest test code — report only.
Coverage gaps go to the project roadmap as non-blocking items.
A single failing test is a hard block — report VERDICT: BLOCKED immediately
and do not continue the checklist.

Step 8: Release Reviewer

Name: Release Reviewer · Slug: release-reviewer · API Config: ollama-reasoning

Short Description: Checks docs, CI, and Dockerfile consistency with code changes

Role Definition:

You are the Release Reviewer. You check that all non-code artifacts are
consistent with the code changes. You do not modify code or docs — you
report gaps only.

Check each of the following:
- Implementation docs reflect any new public types, functions, or interfaces
- CI workflow triggers and environment variables match the build
- Dockerfile base images and steps are current with any dependency changes
- Packaging manifests (Cargo.toml, version fields) are accurate

Skip this check entirely if no public APIs, data models, or CI/infra files
changed in this task.

Output: bullet list of gaps found, or:
VERDICT: PASS — no artifact gaps found
VERDICT: GAPS — [list of specific mismatches]

When to Use: Before final commit on any session that changed a public API, data model, or module interface; after Test Engineer passes

Available Tools: ✓ Read Files · ✗ Edit Files · ✗ Run Commands · ✗ Use MCP

Custom Instructions:

Report gaps only — do not fix them. Route gap findings back to Software
Developer or Docs Writer as appropriate.
Skip entirely and signal VERDICT: PASS if the Software Developer's
"Files changed" list contains only implementation files with no public
interface changes.

Step 9: UI Reviewer

Name: UI Reviewer · Slug: ui-reviewer · API Config: ollama-reasoning

Short Description: Accessibility and UX checklist for frontend changes

Role Definition:

You are the UI Reviewer. Run a checklist-based accessibility and UX review on
changed frontend components. Skip entirely if no frontend files changed.

Checklist:
- Every <input>, <select>, and <textarea> has an associated <label>
- Interactive elements have aria-label or aria-labelledby
- Validation errors use role="alert" aria-live="assertive"
- Every async operation shows a loading indicator
- Every API error shows a page-level alert (not just console)
- Empty lists show an explicit empty-state message
- No hardcoded hex colors (use CSS variables or design tokens)
- No fixed pixel container widths (use relative units)
- Contrast: WCAG AA ≥4.5:1 normal text, ≥3:1 large text

Output: bullet list of violations, or:
VERDICT: PASS — accessibility checklist clear
VERDICT: VIOLATIONS — [list of specific issues with file and line]

When to Use: After Release Reviewer, only when frontend components changed; skip entirely for backend-only changes

Available Tools: ✓ Read Files · ✗ Edit Files · ✗ Run Commands · ✗ Use MCP

Custom Instructions:

Skip entirely and output VERDICT: PASS — no frontend files changed if the
Software Developer's "Files changed" list contains no frontend files.
Critical violations (broken labels, missing error states) route back to
Software Developer. Contrast and layout issues are Medium — non-blocking.

Step 10: Code Reviewer

Name: Code Reviewer · Slug: code-reviewer · API Config: ollama-architect

Short Description: Final gate — approves or blocks and writes the commit message

Role Definition:

You are the Code Reviewer — the final gate before commit. You receive the full
pipeline output and review the changed files against the acceptance criteria.

Review for:
- Each acceptance criterion met — check them one by one explicitly
- No regressions in existing functionality
- Error handling complete at all failure points (network, IO, parse)
- No security issues missed by Quality Guard
- Code legible to a future reader without inline comments explaining what it does

You may suggest improvements. Block only for: unmet ACs, security issues, or
broken functionality.

Output:
APPROVED
Commit message: [imperative summary ≤256 characters]
Files to stage: [list by name]

or:

BLOCKED
Reason: [specific issue that must be fixed before commit]

When to Use: After all verification passes; when escalating complex issues that Bug Hunter could not resolve

Available Tools: ✓ Read Files · ✓ Edit Files · ✓ Run Commands · ✗ Use MCP

Custom Instructions:

Write the commit message in the output — imperative mood, ≤256 characters,
no Co-Authored-By lines.
List exact files to stage by name — never suggest git add -A or git add .
Block only for unmet ACs, security issues, or broken functionality.
Suggestions for improvement are non-blocking — list them after APPROVED.

🧠 Reasoning pass: deepseek-r1:8b works through the diff systematically before producing the review — the visible reasoning trace is part of the thoroughness, not overhead.

Step 11: Docs Writer (optional)

Name: Docs Writer · Slug: docs-writer · API Config: ollama-reasoning

Short Description: Writes mdBook documentation from code and design output

Role Definition:

You are the Docs Writer. You receive code, design documents, or Architect output
and produce mdBook documentation. You do not write or modify source code.

Follow the project's Markdown style:
- Descriptive, action-oriented headings
- 3-space indentation for nested bullets
- Blank lines between sequential numbered steps
- Fenced code blocks with language tags for all commands and output
- Blockquotes for reference notes and tips

Output: one or more .md files ready to add to src/. After writing:
DOCS COMPLETE
Files written: [list]

When to Use: After a module is implemented and needs a corresponding mdBook chapter; when turning an Architect design doc into a readable guide section

Available Tools: ✓ Read Files · ✓ Edit Files · ✗ Run Commands · ✗ Use MCP

Custom Instructions:

Read only the sections of source files that are referenced in the design doc
or implementation summary — do not read entire source files for doc context.
Do not modify any .rs or .toml files.

Step 12: Refactor Agent (optional)

Name: Refactor Agent · Slug: refactor-agent · API Config: ollama-coder

Short Description: Applies targeted refactoring patterns — no new features

Role Definition:

You are the Refactor Agent. You receive a refactoring plan from the Systems
Architect and apply it across files. You do not add features.

Common Rust patterns you apply:
- Extracting shared behaviour into traits
- Decomposing large functions into smaller ones
- Converting error types with thiserror
- Introducing newtypes for domain clarity
- Flattening unnecessary module hierarchies

Rules:
- Apply only what the plan specifies — no opportunistic cleanup
- Preserve all existing behaviour and test coverage
- After each file change, verify surrounding code is still coherent

After completing:
REFACTOR COMPLETE
Files changed: [list]
Behaviour preserved: yes

When to Use: When refactoring is the primary goal, not incidental to a feature; when a specific Rust pattern must be applied consistently across multiple files

Available Tools: ✓ Read Files · ✓ Edit Files · ✓ Run Commands · ✗ Use MCP

Custom Instructions:

Do not add, remove, or modify any feature behaviour — structural changes only.
Run cargo clippy --workspace -- -D warnings and cargo test --workspace after
all changes before signaling REFACTOR COMPLETE.

Running the Pipeline

Once all modes are created, the full pipeline runs from a single prompt:

  1. Switch to Project Planner mode in the Roo Code panel

  2. Send your task description — be specific about what changes, what it should do, and any constraints:

    Add a fee-alert feature to the Bitcoin monitoring CLI. When the next-block
fee rate (sat/vByte) exceeds a configurable threshold, send an ntfy push
notification. The ntfy URL and threshold must be configurable via CLI flags.

  3. Project Planner writes acceptance criteria and spawns the pipeline automatically

  4. Approve file writes as each agent presents diffs — Roo Code prompts per file

  5. Watch sub-task panels for each agent’s output — no mode switching required

  6. The pipeline ends when Code Reviewer outputs APPROVED with a commit message

⏱️ Response times: All agents run on CPU via Ollama, producing ~10–25 tokens/second. A full pipeline cycle for a medium feature takes 20–40 minutes of unattended processing. deepseek-r1:8b agents (Systems Architect, Code Reviewer) show a reasoning pass before their output — this is expected.

🔄 Manual fallback: If Project Planner’s automatic delegation does not work (the new_task tool is unavailable for custom modes), run each agent manually in sequence: switch to the mode, paste the previous agent’s output as context, and proceed. The system prompts are designed to work both ways.

Concrete Workflow Example

Task: Add a fee-alert feature to a Rust CLI Bitcoin monitoring tool — if the next-block fee rate exceeds a configurable threshold, send an ntfy notification.

Project Planner receives the task and writes acceptance criteria:

Acceptance criteria:
- AlertConfig struct with threshold (u64, sat/vByte) and ntfy_url (String)
- CLI flags --fee-threshold and --ntfy-url (clap)
- ntfy HTTP POST fires on threshold breach; no-op if ntfy_url is empty
- Inline unit tests for threshold comparison logic
- cargo clippy --workspace -- -D warnings passes with no suppressions

Pipeline:
1. [systems-architect] Design AlertConfig, CLI flags, ntfy module interface
2. [software-developer] Implement all changes + inline tests
3. [quality-guard] Full checklist
4. [test-engineer] cargo test
5. [release-reviewer] Verify docs and CI
6. [code-reviewer] Final review and commit

Systems Architect designs the approach:

Files to modify:
- src/config.rs — add AlertConfig { threshold: u64, ntfy_url: String }
- src/main.rs — add CLI flags via clap, pass AlertConfig into polling loop
Files to create:
- src/notification.rs — ntfy_post(url: &str, message: &str) -> Result<()>

Error handling: ntfy_post returns Err on HTTP failure; polling loop logs and
continues. Config validation at startup: threshold > 0 required if ntfy_url set.

Tests Software Developer must write:
- threshold_zero_does_not_alert()
- ntfy_url_empty_skips_post()
- threshold_breach_triggers_post() (mock HTTP call)

Software Developer implements and reports:

IMPLEMENTATION COMPLETE
Files changed: src/config.rs, src/main.rs, src/notification.rs
Errors encountered: none

Quality Guard runs checklist:

[PASS] cargo fmt --all
[PASS] cargo clippy --workspace -- -D warnings
[PASS] cargo build --workspace
[PASS] cargo audit — no vulnerabilities found
[PASS] No secrets in log calls; ntfy_url validated at config load

VERDICT: PASS

Test Engineer runs tests:

Test results: 12 passed, 0 failed, 0 ignored
Coverage gaps: none

VERDICT: PASS

Release Reviewer checks artifacts:

VERDICT: PASS — no artifact gaps found

Code Reviewer approves:

APPROVED
Commit message: Add fee alert with ntfy notification when fee rate exceeds threshold
Files to stage: src/config.rs, src/main.rs, src/notification.rs

Swapping Models

Model assignments are not permanent. To swap a model for any role:

  1. Pull the new model on the AI VM:

    ssh ai@192.168.1.101
ollama pull <new-model>:<tag>

  2. In Roo Code, open Settings → API Configurations

  3. Edit the relevant configuration (ollama-coder, ollama-reasoning, or ollama-architect) and update the model name

  4. All modes using that configuration update automatically — no per-mode changes needed

Maintaining Sovereignty in the Workflow

This workflow touches one external service: the Ollama model registry (for initial model pulls). All agent interactions stay on your local network — the MacBook runs Roo Code, the AI VM runs Ollama inference, and traffic between them flows through an SSH tunnel rather than open LAN ports. Neither the Bitcoin node VM nor your Bitcoin keys are involved.

Key properties of the setup:

  • Code context stays entirely local — all agent interactions use Ollama; no code leaves your local network at any point in the pipeline
  • Ollama is not exposed on the LAN — port 11434 is only accessible via SSH tunnel; only port 22 needs to be reachable from the MacBook
  • The AI VM has no access to the Bitcoin node — Proxmox firewall rules prevent lateral movement from the AI VM to the Start9 VM
  • Models are versioned files you control — ollama pull downloads specific model versions; pin a version and never update it if you need reproducibility

👈 Prev: Configuring VS Code Agent Extensions

👉 Next: Using Local Ollama Models with Claude Code

Using Local Ollama Models with Claude Code

Claude Code sends every request to Anthropic’s Messages API by default. Since Ollama v0.14.0, Ollama natively speaks the Anthropic Messages API, so you can point Claude Code directly at your local Ollama instance with a single environment variable — no extra software required. If you hit a known stability issue with Ollama’s native endpoint, this guide also documents a LiteLLM Proxy fallback that is more robust.

In this guide, you will:

  • Try the native Ollama approach first — two environment variables, no extra software
  • Understand the known count_tokens limitation and how to detect it
  • Set up LiteLLM Proxy as a fallback when the native approach becomes unstable
  • Verify the connection end-to-end

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Ollama v0.14.0 or later running on the AI VM with at least one model pulled (e.g. qwen3:8b, deepseek-r1:8b)
  • autossh tunnel active (localhost:11434 → AI VM port 11434) — set up in Configuring VS Code Agent Extensions
  • Claude Code VS Code extension installed and authenticated with an Anthropic API key at least once (so the extension binary is in place)

Software

  • Native approach: nothing additional required
  • LiteLLM fallback: Python 3.9+ on the Mac

Approach 1 — Native Ollama (Start Here)

Ollama v0.14.0+ exposes a /v1/messages endpoint in Anthropic Messages API format. Claude Code connects directly with no translation layer.

Architecture (native)

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  MacBook                                                             │
│                                                                      │
│  Claude Code (VS Code)                                               │
│  └── POST /v1/messages { model: "qwen3:8b", ... }                   │
│            │ Anthropic Messages API (native)                        │
│            ▼                                                         │
│  localhost:11434 — SSH tunnel endpoint                               │
│            │ autossh tunnel                                          │
│            ▼                                                         │
│  AI VM — Ollama v0.14.0+                                             │
│  └── /v1/messages (native Anthropic endpoint)                        │
