└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 1: Pull Models on the AI VM

SSH into the AI VM and pull whichever models you want to use:

ssh ai@192.168.1.101

ollama pull qwen3:8b
ollama pull deepseek-r1:8b
ollama pull qwen2.5-coder:7b
ollama pull gemma3:12b
ollama pull llama3.3:latest

Context window: Ollama defaults to a 2 048-token context window, which is too short for Claude Code’s long prompts. Set OLLAMA_NUM_CTX globally on the AI VM:

# In ~/.zshrc or the systemd unit that starts Ollama
export OLLAMA_NUM_CTX=32768

Restart Ollama after setting it: sudo systemctl restart ollama

Step 2: Set Environment Variables on the Mac

Add both variables to ~/.zshrc:

export ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL=http://localhost:11434
export ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=ollama   # any non-empty string; Ollama does not validate it

Reload the shell:

source ~/.zshrc

Shell restart required: VS Code inherits environment variables from the shell it was launched from. Close VS Code fully and relaunch it from a terminal — not the Dock — so it picks up the new variables:

source ~/.zshrc && code .

Step 3: Select a Model in Claude Code

Run /model inside the Claude Code panel and type the exact Ollama model name (e.g. qwen3:8b). Unlike the LiteLLM approach there is no alias mapping — the name must match the pulled model name exactly as shown by ollama list on the AI VM.

Step 4: Verify the Connection

Send a minimal test request mimicking what Claude Code sends:

curl http://localhost:11434/v1/messages \
  -H "x-api-key: ollama" \
  -H "anthropic-version: 2023-06-01" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{
    "model": "qwen3:8b",
    "max_tokens": 64,
    "messages": [{"role": "user", "content": "Reply with exactly: native working"}]
  }'

A response containing the model’s reply confirms the full chain: Claude Code → SSH tunnel → AI VM Ollama.

Known Limitation: count_tokens Stability Issue

Claude Code periodically calls POST /v1/messages/count_tokens to estimate token usage. Ollama’s native Anthropic endpoint does not support this endpoint, and the resulting 404 can push Ollama into an error state that requires a service restart (Ollama issue #13949, open as of May 2026).

Symptom: Claude Code freezes mid-task, new requests hang indefinitely, and ollama ps on the AI VM shows the model stuck.

Recovery:

# On the AI VM
sudo systemctl restart ollama

If this happens repeatedly during normal Claude Code use, switch to Approach 2 — LiteLLM Proxy.

Approach 2 — LiteLLM Proxy (Fallback)

LiteLLM sits between Claude Code and Ollama, translating Anthropic Messages API requests into Ollama’s OpenAI-compatible format. It handles the count_tokens call gracefully and lets you alias Ollama model names to Claude model names so the /model picker shows familiar names.

Architecture (LiteLLM)

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  MacBook                                                             │
│                                                                      │
│  Claude Code (VS Code)                                               │
│  └── POST /v1/messages { model: "claude-sonnet-4-6", ... }          │
│            │ Anthropic Messages API                                  │
│            ▼                                                         │
│  localhost:4000 — LiteLLM Proxy                                      │
│  └── Maps model name → Ollama model                                  │
│  └── Translates Anthropic format → OpenAI/Ollama format              │
│            │ OpenAI-compatible API                                   │
│            ▼                                                         │
│  localhost:11434 — SSH tunnel endpoint                               │
│            │ autossh tunnel                                          │
│            ▼                                                         │
│  AI VM — Ollama                                                      │
│  └── /v1/chat/completions (OpenAI-compatible)                        │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Step 1: Install LiteLLM Proxy

pip install 'litellm[proxy]'

Verify the install:

litellm --version

Step 2: Create the LiteLLM Config

Create the config directory:

mkdir -p ~/.config/litellm

Write ~/.config/litellm/config.yaml:

model_list:
  # model_name must exactly match what Claude Code sends in the request body
  - model_name: claude-sonnet-4-6
    litellm_params:
      model: ollama_chat/qwen3:8b
      api_base: http://localhost:11434
      num_ctx: 32768

  - model_name: claude-opus-4-7
    litellm_params:
      model: ollama_chat/deepseek-r1:8b
      api_base: http://localhost:11434
      num_ctx: 16384

  - model_name: claude-haiku-4-5-20251001
    litellm_params:
      model: ollama_chat/qwen2.5-coder:7b
      api_base: http://localhost:11434
      num_ctx: 32768

litellm_settings:
  drop_params: true   # silently strips Anthropic-only params Ollama does not support

drop_params: true: Claude Code sends Anthropic-specific fields like cache_control and thinking that Ollama does not understand. This drops them silently rather than returning a 400 error.

Finding the exact model name: If Claude Code returns a model-not-found error, run LiteLLM in the foreground and watch the log — it prints the incoming model field on every request. Copy that exact string into model_name.

Step 3: Start the LiteLLM Proxy

For a foreground test run:

litellm --config ~/.config/litellm/config.yaml --port 4000

For a persistent background process:

nohup litellm --config ~/.config/litellm/config.yaml --port 4000 \
  &> ~/.config/litellm/proxy.log &
echo $! > ~/.config/litellm/proxy.pid

To stop the background process later:

kill $(cat ~/.config/litellm/proxy.pid)

Step 4: Point Claude Code at the Proxy

Update ~/.zshrc:

export ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL=http://localhost:4000
export ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=sk-litellm-local   # any non-empty string; LiteLLM does not validate it

Reload and relaunch VS Code:

source ~/.zshrc && code .

Step 5: Verify the Proxy

Confirm the proxy is reachable:

curl http://localhost:4000/health

Then send a test request:

curl http://localhost:4000/v1/messages \
  -H "x-api-key: sk-litellm-local" \
  -H "anthropic-version: 2023-06-01" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{
    "model": "claude-sonnet-4-6",
    "max_tokens": 64,
    "messages": [{"role": "user", "content": "Reply with exactly: proxy working"}]
  }'

Swapping Models (LiteLLM)

To change which Ollama model backs a Claude Code model name, edit ~/.config/litellm/config.yaml and restart the proxy:

kill $(cat ~/.config/litellm/proxy.pid)
nohup litellm --config ~/.config/litellm/config.yaml --port 4000 \
  &> ~/.config/litellm/proxy.log &
echo $! > ~/.config/litellm/proxy.pid

Approach Comparison

Native OllamaLiteLLM Proxy
Extra softwareNonePython + LiteLLM
Process to manageNoneProxy daemon
Model name in /modelActual name (qwen3:8b)Claude alias (claude-sonnet-4-6)
count_tokens stabilityKnown crash bugHandled gracefully
Anthropic params (thinking, cache_control)Silently ignored by OllamaStripped by drop_params

Known Limitations

Claude Code is designed around Anthropic model capabilities. When running on Ollama:

  • Tool-use quality varies — Claude Code relies heavily on tools (file reads, bash, MCP). Models trained for tool use (qwen2.5-coder, qwen3, llama3.1+) work best; models without explicit tool-use training cannot reliably produce the structured JSON Claude Code expects
  • Extended thinking is silently ignored — the thinking parameter is dropped at the Ollama layer; the model does not chain-of-thought unless it has built-in reasoning (like deepseek-r1)
  • Smaller effective context — even with OLLAMA_NUM_CTX=32768, local models fall far short of Claude’s 200k-token window; very large repos or long conversations may hit the limit mid-task
  • Slower interactive features — local CPU inference produces 10–25 tokens/second; inline completions and quick fixes feel slower than with hosted Claude

Troubleshooting

Claude Code freezes mid-task (native approach)

Ollama is in an error state from the count_tokens call. Restart Ollama on the AI VM, then consider switching to the LiteLLM fallback:

sudo systemctl restart ollama

Connection refused on port 11434 or 4000

The autossh tunnel is down. Re-establish it:

autossh -M 20001 -f -N -L 11434:localhost:11434 ai@192.168.1.101

If using LiteLLM, also check that the proxy is running:

litellm --config ~/.config/litellm/config.yaml --port 4000

Model not found (LiteLLM)

The model name Claude Code sent does not match any model_name in the config. Run LiteLLM in the foreground, trigger a request from Claude Code, and copy the exact model string from the log into a new model_name entry.

Claude Code still calls Anthropic

ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL is not in the environment VS Code was launched from. Relaunch from a terminal that has the variable set:

source ~/.zshrc && code .

Tool-use errors or malformed function_calls JSON

Switch to a model with stronger tool-use support (qwen3:8b, qwen2.5-coder:7b, llama3.1+). Models without explicit tool-use training cannot reliably produce the structured output Claude Code expects.

Reverting to Anthropic Claude

To switch Claude Code back to the official Anthropic API, undo the two environment variable changes and (if running) stop the LiteLLM proxy.

Step 1: Remove the Ollama Environment Variables

Open ~/.zshrc and delete (or comment out) these two lines:

# Remove or comment out both of these:
export ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL=http://localhost:11434   # or :4000 for LiteLLM
export ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=ollama                    # or sk-litellm-local

Then restore your real Anthropic API key:

export ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=sk-ant-...   # your actual Anthropic API key

Reload the shell:

source ~/.zshrc

Step 2: Stop the LiteLLM Proxy (if running)

If you started a background LiteLLM process, stop it:

kill $(cat ~/.config/litellm/proxy.pid)

If there is no PID file, find and kill the process by name:

pkill -f "litellm"

Step 3: Relaunch VS Code

VS Code inherits environment variables from the shell it was launched from. Close VS Code fully and relaunch from a terminal so it picks up the restored variables:

source ~/.zshrc && code .

Step 4: Select an Anthropic Model

Run /model inside the Claude Code panel and select or type an Anthropic model name (e.g. claude-sonnet-4-6, claude-opus-4-7). Claude Code will now route requests to api.anthropic.com using your real API key.

Verify: Send a test message and confirm the model name shown in the Claude Code header matches the Anthropic model you selected, not a local model name.

Maintaining Sovereignty in the Workflow

In both approaches, no code or prompts leave your local network:

  • Claude Code sends requests to localhost:11434 (native) or localhost:4000 (LiteLLM) — both on the Mac
  • Traffic flows through the SSH tunnel to the AI VM on the local LAN
  • Ollama performs inference on the AI VM’s GPU or CPU
  • No external API calls are made; the ANTHROPIC_API_KEY placeholder never reaches Anthropic

The only data that leaves the local network is the initial ollama pull when downloading model weights.

Here I document experiments at the intersection of Bitcoin infrastructure, Rust development, and AI-assisted coding. Every experiment starts with a real problem encountered while building the node, and every solution is tested on the actual hardware described in this book.

The spirit here is: build, break, learn, share.

Remote Claude Code Access from iPhone via Tor

After building a full Bitcoin node stack, the natural next experiment is: can I manage my Claude Code AI agents on the MacBook from my iPhone, from anywhere, without opening any port to the internet or using any cloud service?

The answer is yes — using a hardened Tor hidden service on the MacBook. The MacBook exposes its SSH port as a .onion address through the Tor network, protected by Tor v3 client authorization and SSH public-key-only authentication. The iPhone connects via Orbot (iOS Tor proxy) and Termius (SSH client). No port forwarding, no VPN subscription, no cloud server.

Security model:

  • SSH is restricted to 127.0.0.1 — unreachable from the LAN or internet without Tor
  • Tor v3 client authorization means only your iPhone can reach the hidden service at all
  • Password authentication is completely disabled — only Ed25519 public key accepted
  • The .onion address itself is unknown to anyone who does not hold the client auth private key

In this guide, you will:

  • Install Tor on MacBook via Homebrew
  • Configure a Tor v3 hidden service for SSH
  • Generate a Tor v3 client authorization keypair (only your iPhone can connect to the .onion address)
  • Harden sshd_config to disable password auth and restrict SSH to localhost only
  • Generate an Ed25519 SSH key pair and install the public key
  • Enable SSH (Remote Login) on MacBook after hardening is in place
  • Install tmux on MacBook for persistent Claude Code sessions
  • Install VS Code and the Claude Code extension on MacBook
  • Install Orbot on iPhone and import the Tor client auth key
  • Install Termius on iPhone and connect using the Ed25519 key
  • Install ntfy on iPhone for push notifications from Claude Code
  • Configure Claude Code hooks to send real-time notifications to iPhone
  • Test the complete end-to-end workflow

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • MacBook with macOS and Homebrew installed
  • iPhone with App Store access
  • SSH (Remote Login) will be enabled after hardening — do not enable it before Step 8
  • MacBook must be awake and at home — it is the Tor hidden service endpoint

Hardware

  • MacBook (running VS Code and Claude Code agents)
  • iPhone (your remote control device)

Software

  • Homebrew on MacBook — install if missing:

    /bin/bash -c "$(curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Homebrew/install/HEAD/install.sh)"

References

  • Tor Project — Onion Services
  • Tor Project — Client Authorization
  • Orbot iOS App Store
  • Claude Code Documentation
  • ntfy Documentation
  • tmux GitHub

Step-by-Step Instructions

Step 1: Install Tor on MacBook

  1. Open Terminal on your MacBook

  2. Install Tor via Homebrew:

    brew install tor

  3. Confirm the Homebrew prefix (differs between Intel and Apple Silicon):

    brew --prefix
    • Apple Silicon (M1/M2/M3/M4): /opt/homebrew
    • Intel Mac: /usr/local

    The steps below use $(brew --prefix) so they work on both architectures automatically.

Step 2: Configure Tor Hidden Service for SSH

  1. Create a permanent directory to store the hidden service keys, hostname, and client authorization:

    mkdir -p ~/.tor/ssh_hidden_service/authorized_clients
chmod 700 ~/.tor/ssh_hidden_service
chmod 700 ~/.tor/ssh_hidden_service/authorized_clients

  2. Confirm your macOS username:

    whoami

  3. Open the Tor configuration file (Homebrew does not create this file by default — nano will open a blank new file, which is expected):

    nano $(brew --prefix)/etc/tor/torrc

  4. The file will be blank. Type these lines (replace yourusername with your macOS username from the previous step):

    HiddenServiceDir /Users/yourusername/.tor/ssh_hidden_service
HiddenServiceVersion 3
HiddenServicePort 22 127.0.0.1:22

  5. Save the file (Ctrl+X, Y, Enter)

📁 What these lines do:

HiddenServiceVersion 3 explicitly selects v3 onion addresses (56-character, Ed25519-based). V3 is the current standard — stronger cryptography and longer addresses than legacy v2.

HiddenServiceDir tells Tor where to store the hidden service keys and the hostname file. Using a permanent path means your .onion address survives reboots.

HiddenServicePort 22 127.0.0.1:22 maps .onion port 22 to the MacBook’s local SSH daemon — only reachable through Tor.

Step 3: Generate Tor v3 Client Authorization Keypair

Tor v3 client authorization means your hidden service rejects all connection attempts from anyone who does not hold the matching private key — including anyone who discovers your .onion address. This is the outermost layer of security.

  1. Install coreutils via Homebrew (provides gbase32 for base32 encoding):

    brew install coreutils

  2. Generate an X25519 keypair using OpenSSL (built into macOS):

    openssl genpkey -algorithm x25519 -out ~/.tor/client_auth.priv.pem
openssl pkey -in ~/.tor/client_auth.priv.pem -pubout -out ~/.tor/client_auth.pub.pem

  3. Extract the base32-encoded private key (you will give this to Orbot on iPhone):

    openssl pkey -in ~/.tor/client_auth.priv.pem -outform DER | tail -c 32 | gbase32 | tr -d '='

    Save this output — it is your client private key (base32). You will use it in Step 11.

  4. Extract the base32-encoded public key (you will install this on MacBook):

    openssl pkey -pubin -in ~/.tor/client_auth.pub.pem -pubout -outform DER | tail -c 32 | gbase32 | tr -d '='

    Save this output — it is your client public key (base32).

🔑 Key security property: The X25519 public key is installed on the MacBook. Tor uses it to encrypt hidden service descriptor data so only the holder of the matching private key can even discover that the service exists. Your .onion address is useless to anyone else.

Step 4: Install Client Authorization on MacBook

  1. Create the server-side authorization file (replace <PUBLIC_KEY_BASE32> with the public key from Step 3):

    echo "descriptor:x25519:<PUBLIC_KEY_BASE32>" > ~/.tor/ssh_hidden_service/authorized_clients/iphone.auth
chmod 600 ~/.tor/ssh_hidden_service/authorized_clients/iphone.auth

    Example (your key will differ):

    descriptor:x25519:AAAAAABBBBBBCCCCCCDDDDDDEEEEEEFFFFFFFFGGGGGGHHHHHHIIIIIIJJJJJJ

  2. Verify the file was created correctly:

    cat ~/.tor/ssh_hidden_service/authorized_clients/iphone.auth

Step 5: Start Tor and Retrieve the .onion Address

  1. Start Tor as a background service (auto-restarts on login):

    brew services start tor

  2. Wait 15–20 seconds for Tor to bootstrap and generate the hidden service keys

  3. Retrieve your MacBook’s .onion address:

    cat ~/.tor/ssh_hidden_service/hostname

    You will see something like:

    abc123xyz456def789ghi012jkl345mno678pqr901stu234vwx567yz89.onion

  4. Copy and save this address — you will enter it into Termius on your iPhone

✅ Verify Tor is running:

brew services list | grep tor

Status should show started. If it shows error, check logs:

cat $(brew --prefix)/var/log/tor.log

Step 6: Harden SSH Configuration (Required)

⚠️ This step is required before enabling SSH. The default macOS SSH configuration accepts password authentication. Enabling Remote Login without hardening first would expose your MacBook to brute-force attacks via the Tor network.

  1. Open the SSH daemon configuration file:

    sudo nano /etc/ssh/sshd_config

  2. Find and set (or add) these directives — comment out any conflicting existing entries with #:

    ListenAddress 127.0.0.1
PermitRootLogin no
PasswordAuthentication no
ChallengeResponseAuthentication no
PubkeyAuthentication yes
AuthenticationMethods publickey
MaxAuthTries 3
LoginGraceTime 20
X11Forwarding no
AllowTcpForwarding no

  3. Save the file (Ctrl+X, Y, Enter)

🔒 What each directive does:

ListenAddress 127.0.0.1 — SSH only binds to localhost. The port is invisible on the LAN and internet. Only Tor (which connects internally via 127.0.0.1:22) can reach it.

PasswordAuthentication no and ChallengeResponseAuthentication no — completely disables password login. Only public key authentication is accepted.

AuthenticationMethods publickey — redundant confirmation; only public key accepted.

MaxAuthTries 3 — disconnects after 3 failed attempts.

LoginGraceTime 20 — closes unauthenticated connections after 20 seconds.

X11Forwarding no and AllowTcpForwarding no — minimal attack surface; no tunnelling capabilities.

Step 7: Generate Ed25519 SSH Key Pair

Ed25519 is the recommended SSH key type — faster and more resistant to side-channel attacks than RSA.

  1. Generate the key pair (on MacBook — this key pair belongs to your iPhone):

    ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "iphone-termius" -f ~/.ssh/iphone_ed25519

    When prompted for a passphrase, enter a strong passphrase — this protects the private key if your iPhone is lost.

  2. Install the public key in the authorized keys file:

    cat ~/.ssh/iphone_ed25519.pub >> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
chmod 600 ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
chmod 700 ~/.ssh

  3. Display the private key for transfer to iPhone (you will copy this into Termius in Step 12):

    cat ~/.ssh/iphone_ed25519

    The output starts with -----BEGIN OPENSSH PRIVATE KEY-----. Copy the entire block including the header and footer lines.

⚠️ Private key security: Transfer the private key to Termius over a local connection (cable, AirDrop on your local network) whenever possible. Delete ~/.ssh/iphone_ed25519 from MacBook after transferring — the MacBook only needs authorized_keys with the public key, not the private key.

Step 8: Enable SSH on MacBook

SSH is now safe to enable because:

  • sshd_config restricts it to 127.0.0.1 (Tor-only)
  • Password authentication is disabled
  • Only the Ed25519 public key from Step 7 is accepted

  1. Open System Settings → General → Sharing

  2. Toggle Remote Login to On

  3. Confirm your user appears in the allowed users list

  4. Restart the SSH daemon to apply the hardened configuration:

    sudo launchctl stop com.openssh.sshd
sudo launchctl start com.openssh.sshd

  5. Verify SSH is only listening on localhost (not 0.0.0.0):

    sudo lsof -i :22

    You should see sshd bound to 127.0.0.1:ssh only. If you see *:ssh or 0.0.0.0:ssh, the sshd_config change has not taken effect — restart macOS and re-check.

Step 9: Install tmux on MacBook

tmux keeps your Claude Code session alive when you disconnect from SSH. Without it, closing Termius on your iPhone immediately kills the running Claude Code process.

  1. Install tmux:

    brew install tmux

  2. Verify installation:

    tmux -V

Essential tmux commands for this workflow:

  • Start a named session: tmux new -s claude
  • Detach and leave running: Ctrl+B, then D
  • Reattach from SSH: tmux attach -t claude
  • List all sessions: tmux ls

⚠️ Critical habit: Always start Claude Code inside a named tmux session. If Claude Code runs in a plain terminal and the SSH connection drops, the process is killed immediately.

📍 When to use tmux: Do not start a tmux session now. These commands are used later — after you SSH into your MacBook from your iPhone (Step 12). Once inside that SSH session, run tmux new -s claude, then launch Claude Code. You can then detach and close Termius without killing the process. For now, close this terminal and continue to Step 10.

Step 10: Install VS Code, Claude Code Extension, and Claude Code CLI

  1. Download and install Visual Studio Code from code.visualstudio.com

  2. Open VS Code

  3. Press Cmd+Shift+X to open the Extensions panel

  4. Search for Claude Code and install the official Anthropic extension

  5. Follow the setup wizard to authenticate with your Anthropic account

  6. Verify it works: Cmd+Shift+P → type Claude: Start Session

  7. Install the Claude Code CLI (needed to run claude in the terminal from your SSH session):

    curl -fsSL https://claude.ai/install.sh | bash

  8. Verify the CLI is installed:

    claude --version

Why both? The VS Code extension gives you the full GUI experience when working locally. The CLI (claude command) is what you run inside a tmux session over SSH from your iPhone — there is no GUI over a terminal connection.

Step 11: Install Orbot on iPhone and Import Client Auth Key

Orbot is the official Tor proxy app for iOS. In VPN mode it routes all iPhone traffic through Tor — including Termius SSH connections. You must also import the Tor v3 client authorization private key so Orbot can authenticate to your hidden service.

  1. On your iPhone, open the App Store

  2. Search for and install Orbot (free — by Guardian Project / Tor Project)

  3. Open Orbot → tap Start

  4. Enable VPN Mode (toggle in Orbot’s main screen)

  5. Wait for Orbot to show Connected

  6. Import the Tor v3 client authorization key:

    • Tap the gear icon in the top-right corner of Orbot’s main screen
    • In the pop-up menu, tap Auth Cookies
    • Tap + to add a new entry
    • Enter your MacBook .onion address from Step 5
    • Enter the base32 private key you saved in Step 3
    • Tap Save

    Note: Older guides show Settings → Onion Services → Client Authorization — that path no longer exists. Auth Cookies is now in the gear icon dropdown on the main screen.

📡 VPN Mode is required: Without VPN mode, iOS apps cannot resolve .onion domains. With VPN mode active, Orbot intercepts all DNS lookups and routes .onion connections through Tor automatically.

🔑 Client auth is required: Without the imported private key, Tor returns an empty descriptor for your .onion address — Termius will time out as if the service does not exist. This is the intended behavior.

Step 12: Install Termius on iPhone and Connect with Ed25519 Key

  1. On your iPhone, open the App Store

  2. Search for and install Termius (free — basic SSH included in the free tier)

  3. Confirm Orbot is running in VPN mode before attempting to connect

  4. Import your Ed25519 private key into Termius:

    • Open Termius → Settings → Keychain → + → New Key
    • Paste the full private key content from Step 7 (the -----BEGIN OPENSSH PRIVATE KEY----- block)
    • Enter the key passphrase you set in Step 7
    • Save as iphone-termius

  5. Create a new host in Termius → tap New Host

  6. Fill in:

    • Hostname: your MacBook .onion address from Step 5
    • Port: 22
    • Username: your macOS username
    • SSH Key: select iphone-termius (the key imported above)
    • Password: leave blank — password auth is disabled on the server

  7. Tap Save → tap the host to connect

⏱️ Expected connection time: 10–20 seconds. Tor v3 circuit setup with client authorization takes longer than a plain connection — Tor is negotiating three relay hops and verifying your client authorization before the SSH handshake begins. Once connected, interactive SSH is responsive enough for approving Claude Code actions.

Step 13: Install ntfy on iPhone

ntfy sends push notifications from MacBook to iPhone whenever Claude Code is about to take an action or finishes a session.

  1. On your iPhone, open the App Store

  2. Search for and install ntfy (free — by Binwiederhier)

  3. Open ntfy → tap + → Subscribe to topic

  4. Enter a long, random topic name (e.g., claudecode-j7k2x9m4p3) — this acts as a private channel without needing an account

  5. Tap Subscribe

🔒 Topic privacy: Anyone who knows your topic name can subscribe. Use a long random string (16+ characters) that cannot be guessed. Never reuse a topic name across devices or share it publicly.

Step 14: Configure Claude Code Hooks for Notifications

  1. On your MacBook, open Terminal and edit the Claude Code settings file:

    mkdir -p ~/.claude
nano ~/.claude/settings.json

  2. Add the following (replace your-topic with your ntfy topic name from Step 13):

    {
  "hooks": {
    "PreToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Bash|Write|Edit",
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "command",
            "command": "curl -s -d \"⚙️ About to run: $CLAUDE_TOOL_NAME\" ntfy.sh/your-topic"
          }
        ]
      }
    ],
    "Stop": [
      {
        "matcher": "",
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "command",
            "command": "curl -s -d \"✅ Claude Code session finished\" ntfy.sh/your-topic"
          }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}

  3. Save (Ctrl+X, Y, Enter) and restart Claude Code for hooks to take effect

📱 What you will see on iPhone: Every time Claude Code is about to run a Bash command, write a file, or edit code, your iPhone buzzes with a notification. When the session finishes, you get a completion ping — your cue to open Termius and give an approval or new instruction.

Step 15: End-to-End Test

  1. On MacBook — navigate to your project folder, then start Claude Code inside a tmux session:

    cd ~/path/to/your-project
tmux new -s claude
claude

    💡 Tip: Always cd into your project directory before starting tmux. Claude Code uses the working directory as context — launching from the right folder means it immediately sees your codebase without needing to navigate later.

  2. Detach — press Ctrl+B, then D. Claude Code keeps running in the background.

  3. On iPhone — confirm Orbot is connected (VPN badge visible in status bar) and the client auth key is imported (Step 11)

  4. Open Termius → tap your .onion host → wait 10–20 seconds for the Tor connection to establish

  5. Reattach to the session:

    tmux attach -t claude

  6. You are now live in the same Claude Code session from your iPhone

  7. Test notifications — trigger any tool use in Claude Code and verify ntfy fires on your iPhone within seconds

Step 16: End a Session and Stop All Services

When you are done working, stop all services that expose attack surface. Running Tor and SSH continuously when not in use is unnecessary risk.

On iPhone:

  1. Close the Termius SSH session (Termius needs no special shutdown — closing the connection is enough)
  2. ntfy needs no action — it is a passive notification receiver and can stay open
  3. Open Orbot → tap Stop to disconnect from Tor
  4. Disable VPN mode

On MacBook — stop Tor and SSH:

# Detach from tmux first if still attached (Ctrl+B, D), then:

# Stop Tor hidden service
brew services stop tor

# Stop SSH listener
sudo launchctl stop localhost.ssh
sudo launchctl unload /Library/LaunchDaemons/localhost-ssh.plist

Verify nothing is listening on port 22:

sudo lsof -i :22

Expected: no output.

To start a new session later:

Follow these steps in order:

  1. On MacBook — start SSH and Tor:

    sudo launchctl load /Library/LaunchDaemons/localhost-ssh.plist
brew services start tor

  2. On iPhone — open Orbot → tap Start → enable VPN mode. Wait until Orbot shows a green connected state.

  3. On iPhone — open ntfy → confirm your topic channel is still subscribed (no action needed beyond verifying it is open).

  4. On iPhone — open Termius → tap your saved host → connect. You should land at the MacBook shell prompt.

  5. On iPhone (in Termius) — reattach to your existing tmux session:

    tmux attach -t claude

    Or start a new one if the previous session was closed:

    cd ~/path/to/your-project
tmux new -s claude
claude

⚠️ Reboot behaviour: After a reboot, neither SSH nor Tor start automatically — you must run the start commands above before connecting. Verify with sudo lsof -i :22 and brew services list | grep tor before attempting to connect from iPhone.

Verify who is connected before ending a session:

# Show all active SSH sessions — who is logged in and from where
who

# Show open SSH connections with source address (will show 127.0.0.1 for Tor connections)
sudo lsof -i :22 -n -P | grep ESTABLISHED

# Full SSH session detail: user, PID, login time, source
last | head -10

Expected output for a legitimate Tor-only session:

  • who shows your own username with a pts/ or ttys terminal
  • lsof shows 127.0.0.1:22 → 127.0.0.1:<ephemeral-port> (ESTABLISHED) only while an SSH session is actively open — no output means no active remote connection, which is normal
  • last shows two kinds of entries: logins from 127.0.0.1 (SSH from iPhone via Tor) and logins with no IP address (local Terminal or VS Code sessions on the MacBook itself) — both are expected and legitimate

🔍 What to look for: Since SSH is bound to 127.0.0.1 and only reachable via the Tor hidden service, any ESTABLISHED connection on port 22 originated through Tor. A connection from any address other than 127.0.0.1 would indicate the SSH listener is unexpectedly exposed — stop sshd immediately and re-check your launchd plist.

🔑 Why malicious connections are structurally prevented: An attacker would need (1) your .onion address, (2) the X25519 client auth private key, and (3) the Ed25519 SSH private key with its passphrase — all three independently. The Tor client auth layer means the hidden service is invisible without the key, so port scanning or address brute-forcing cannot reach the SSH layer at all.

Troubleshooting

Tor hidden service hostname file not found

  • Confirm the HiddenServiceDir path in torrc exactly matches the directory you created
  • Confirm permissions: chmod 700 ~/.tor/ssh_hidden_service
  • Check Tor logs: cat $(brew --prefix)/var/log/tor.log
  • Restart Tor: brew services restart tor

Termius times out connecting to .onion address

  • Confirm Orbot is running in VPN mode — the VPN badge must be visible in iPhone status bar
  • Confirm the Tor v3 client auth private key is imported in Orbot (Step 11) — without it, the hidden service is invisible
  • Allow 10–20 seconds — Tor v3 with client auth has a longer circuit setup time
  • Verify Tor is running on MacBook: brew services list | grep tor

Termius reports “connection reset by peer”

This error means Tor reached port 22 on the MacBook successfully but the SSH daemon immediately dropped the connection. It is distinct from a timeout (Tor/auth issue) and from “connection refused” (sshd not running). Work through these checks in order:

  1. Validate sshd_config for syntax errors:

    sudo sshd -t

    Any error printed here will cause sshd to reset every incoming connection. Fix the reported line, then restart sshd (see Step 8 or reload the custom plist).

  2. Confirm SSH host keys exist:

    ls /etc/ssh/ssh_host_*

    Expected: files like ssh_host_ed25519_key, ssh_host_rsa_key, etc. If the directory is empty, regenerate them:

    sudo ssh-keygen -A

  3. Check live sshd logs for the reset reason:

    sudo log show --predicate 'process == "sshd"' --last 5m --style compact

    Look for lines like error: Bind to port 22, error: Could not load host key, or authentication-related messages.

  4. Test SSH locally from the MacBook itself:

    ssh -v -i ~/.ssh/iphone_ed25519 localhost

    If this succeeds, the problem is on the Tor/iPhone side. If it fails with the same “reset” message, the issue is in the local sshd configuration.

  5. Verify the custom launchd plist is loaded (if you went through the ListenAddress fix):

    sudo launchctl list | grep localhost.ssh

    Expected outputs:

    12345    0    localhost.ssh   ← running normally (PID, exit code 0)
-        0    localhost.ssh   ← loaded but idle, this is fine
-      255    localhost.ssh   ← loaded but sshd crashed on last start

    No output at all means the plist is not loaded — reload it:

    sudo launchctl load /Library/LaunchDaemons/localhost-ssh.plist

    If you see exit code 255, sshd launched but crashed immediately. Diagnose in order:

    # 1. Check sshd_config for syntax errors
sudo sshd -t

# 2. Check if port 22 is already claimed by another process
sudo lsof -i :22

# 3. Check the crash reason in logs
sudo log show --predicate 'process == "sshd"' --last 5m --style compact

    Fix whatever the log reports (bad config line, port conflict, missing host key), then unload and reload the plist:

    sudo launchctl unload /Library/LaunchDaemons/localhost-ssh.plist
sudo launchctl load /Library/LaunchDaemons/localhost-ssh.plist

  6. Confirm Remote Login is still enabled in System Settings → General → Sharing. macOS may re-disable it after a system update or preference reset, which stops sshd from being managed by launchd at all.

SSH connection refused

  • Confirm Remote Login is enabled: System Settings → General → Sharing → Remote Login
  • Check sshd is running: sudo launchctl list | grep ssh
  • Verify SSH is listening on 127.0.0.1 (not 0.0.0.0): sudo lsof -i :22

SSH still listens on *:ssh after adding ListenAddress 127.0.0.1

On macOS, launchd binds the SSH socket before passing it to sshd, so ListenAddress in sshd_config is ignored — launchd wins. The fix is a custom launchd plist that binds to 127.0.0.1 and replaces the system-managed socket.

  1. Create /Library/LaunchDaemons/localhost-ssh.plist:

    sudo tee /Library/LaunchDaemons/localhost-ssh.plist > /dev/null << 'EOF'
<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>
<!DOCTYPE plist PUBLIC "-//Apple//DTD PLIST 1.0//EN"
  "http://www.apple.com/DTDs/PropertyList-1.0.dtd">
<plist version="1.0">
<dict>
  <key>Label</key>
  <string>localhost.ssh</string>
  <key>ProgramArguments</key>
  <array>
    <string>/usr/sbin/sshd</string>
    <string>-i</string>
  </array>
  <key>Sockets</key>
  <dict>
    <key>Listeners</key>
    <dict>
      <key>SockFamily</key>
      <string>IPv4</string>
      <key>SockNodeName</key>
      <string>127.0.0.1</string>
      <key>SockServiceName</key>
      <string>ssh</string>
      <key>SockType</key>
      <string>stream</string>
    </dict>
  </dict>
  <key>inetdCompatibility</key>
  <dict>
    <key>Wait</key>
    <false/>
  </dict>
  <key>StandardErrorPath</key>
  <string>/dev/null</string>
</dict>
</plist>
EOF

  2. Unload the system plist and load the custom one:

    sudo launchctl stop com.openssh.sshd
sudo launchctl unload /System/Library/LaunchDaemons/ssh.plist 2>/dev/null || true
sudo launchctl load /Library/LaunchDaemons/localhost-ssh.plist

  3. Verify:

    sudo lsof -i :22

    Expected output: sshd ... TCP localhost:ssh (LISTEN) — not *:ssh.

After a reboot, confirm with sudo lsof -i :22 that the binding held. If the system plist reloads, ensure Remote Login remains disabled in System Settings → General → Sharing so macOS does not re-activate com.openssh.sshd.

SSH public key authentication fails

  • Verify ~/.ssh/authorized_keys contains the correct public key: cat ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
  • Check permissions: chmod 700 ~/.ssh && chmod 600 ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
  • In Termius, confirm the correct key (iphone-termius) is selected for the host, not password auth
  • Confirm the key passphrase entered in Termius matches the passphrase set during key generation

tmux session not found after SSH

  • Run tmux ls — if empty, start fresh with tmux new -s claude
  • Always start Claude Code inside a named tmux session

Tor not running after MacBook reboot

  • Re-run: brew services start tor
  • Confirm it is in the services list: brew services list | grep tor

ntfy notifications not arriving on iPhone

  • Test manually from MacBook: curl -d "test" ntfy.sh/your-topic — should appear on iPhone immediately
  • Verify topic name in settings.json exactly matches the subscribed topic in the ntfy app
  • Restart Claude Code after editing settings.json

👉 Next: Claude Desktop: AI Travel Planning Partner

Claude Desktop: AI Travel Planning Partner

Planning a multi-leg trip is research-heavy work — comparing cruise schedules, checking hotel pricing across seasons, reading weather forecasts by port, and tailoring everything to whoever is actually traveling. Most people do this manually: switching between browser tabs, copy-pasting prices into a spreadsheet, and starting over when the traveler changes. Claude Desktop collapses all of that into a single conversation.

This guide walks through one concrete end-to-end workflow: reading an existing trip captured in a Google Sheet, adapting it to a new traveler with a different profile, and generating a ready-to-use itinerary in a new Google Sheet. The baseline trip is a 14-day Vancouver-to-Alaska journey including a 7-day Discovery Princess cruise. The goal is to use everything that previous planner already figured out — logistics, costs, what booked out early, what was worth the money — and build a new plan that fits someone different.

In this guide, you will:

  • Install Claude Desktop and configure a Travel Planning Project
  • Import a past trip’s Google Sheet data and run a structured analysis
  • Profile a new traveler with different constraints, budget, and preferences
  • Ask Claude to research current cruise schedules, pricing, weather, and availability
  • Generate a customized 14-day itinerary and export it to a new Google Sheet

Setup

Step 1: Install Claude Desktop

Download the macOS app from claude.ai/download and sign in with your Anthropic account. The browser version at claude.ai works identically — use either.

Claude Pro ($20/month) is required for this workflow. It adds file upload support, longer context (needed to paste full itinerary data), and web search access for real-time pricing research.

Step 2: Create a Travel Planning Project

Claude Projects persist a custom instruction set across all conversations inside the project. Set one up so Claude knows your role and output preferences without re-explaining them every session.

  1. Open Claude Desktop

  2. Click New Project in the left sidebar

  3. Name it Travel Planning — Vancouver/Alaska Baseline

  4. In the Project Instructions field, describe your context:

    About me: [Your role — "planning a trip for my parents", "travel agent
comparing itinerary options", "solo traveler adapting a past cruise trip"]

Planning preferences:
- Budget style: [budget / mid-range / luxury]
- Pace preference: [slow and relaxed / active and packed / mix]
- Priorities: [wildlife / history / food / adventure / comfort]

Output format:
- Prefer tables for day-by-day itineraries
- Include cost estimates in CAD and USD where relevant
- Flag any recommendations where information may be outdated

  5. Save the Project — every travel planning conversation you start inside it inherits this context

💡 One Project per context: If you are planning trips for multiple people with different profiles, create a separate Project for each. “Planning for my mother (68, limited mobility, comfort-focused)” gives Claude more useful defaults than “I plan trips for various people.”

Step 3: The Core Technique — Context-Goal-Format

Every effective Claude prompt has three parts:

Context:  What you have (past trip data, traveler profile, constraints)
Goal:     What you want Claude to produce (analysis, itinerary, research summary)
Format:   How to structure the output (table, bullet list, CSV for Google Sheets)

Prompts that skip Context produce generic outputs. Prompts that skip Format produce walls of text. The prompts throughout this guide embed all three — notice the pattern as you adapt them to your situation.

The Travel Planning Workflow

This guide is split into three parts. Complete them in order — each part builds on the output of the previous one.

Part 1: Reading the Past Travel Plan

→ Part 1: Reading the Past Travel Plan

You export the existing Google Sheet, paste it into Claude, and ask Claude to extract the key logistics, cost structure, booking lessons, and what worked. This becomes the research foundation — Claude now “knows” the past trip in detail and can reference it throughout the planning process.

Part 2: Profiling the New Traveler & Researching Current Conditions

→ Part 2: Profiling the New Traveler & Researching Current Conditions

You describe the new traveler’s profile — age, mobility, budget, interests, travel dates — and ask Claude to research how current conditions differ from the baseline trip. Claude identifies what Princess Cruises itineraries are available in the new travel window, how August pricing compares to June, what weather changes by port, and which excursions need different handling for this traveler’s physical profile.

Part 3: Building the New Trip Plan & Creating the Google Sheet

→ Part 3: Building the New Trip Plan & Creating the Google Sheet

You ask Claude to synthesize the baseline analysis, traveler profile, and research into a complete customized itinerary. Claude generates four tabs of output in CSV format — daily itinerary, cruise excursions, accommodation, and budget estimate — formatted for direct paste into a new Google Sheet. You copy, paste, and have a complete travel document in minutes.

👈 Prev: Remote Claude Code Access from iPhone via Tor

👉 Next: Part 1: Reading the Past Travel Plan

Part 1: Reading the Past Travel Plan

Before Claude can plan a new trip, it needs to understand the existing one. A past trip captured in a Google Sheet is a dense source of real-world data: actual costs versus estimates, timing that worked, logistics that failed, and the sequence of decisions that shaped the experience. This part covers how to prepare that data, bring it into Claude, and extract a research foundation Claude will carry through the rest of the planning process.

In this guide, you will:

  • Structure your existing Google Sheet so Claude can parse it accurately
  • Paste the past trip data into Claude and confirm it was read correctly
  • Run a structured analysis to extract logistics, cost lessons, and booking timing
  • Ask Claude to flag how the itinerary would change for a different traveler profile

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • Claude Pro subscription (required for long-context pasting)
  • A Google Sheet documenting a past trip — the examples here use a 14-day Vancouver-to-Alaska trip with a 7-day Discovery Princess cruise
  • A Travel Planning Project configured in Claude Desktop (see Claude Desktop: AI Travel Planning Partner)

Recommended Google Sheet Structure

If your existing sheet is unstructured, reorganize it before importing. Claude reads tabular data most accurately when columns are consistent and clearly labeled. The four-tab structure below is the reference format used throughout this guide.

Tab 1 — Trip Overview

FieldValue
Trip nameVancouver to Alaska — 14 Days
Travel datesJun 2–15, 2023
Travelers2 adults (couple, early 40s)
Total actual costCAD $12,400
Cruise linePrincess Cruises
Cruise shipDiscovery Princess
Cruise duration7 days
Embarkation / disembarkationVancouver Canada Place
Cruise routeInside Passage — Ketchikan, Juneau, Skagway, Tracy Arm Fjord
Overall satisfaction9/10

Tab 2 — Daily Itinerary

1Jun 2VancouverArrive YVR, taxi to Fairmont Pacific Rim$540Early check-in requested, worth it
2Jun 3VancouverStanley Park bike loop, Granville Island$90Bike rental best way to see the park
3Jun 4VancouverCapilano Suspension Bridge, dinner Gastown$160Book Capilano online to skip queue
4Jun 5VancouverFree morning, board Discovery Princess 1pm$45Check-in opens 11am; luggage to cabin by 9pm
5Jun 6Sea dayAt sea — Tracy Arm Fjord scenic cruisingIncludedBest views at dawn from the bow
6Jun 7KetchikanCreek Street walking tour, Saxman totem poles$20Self-guided, no advance booking needed
7Jun 8JuneauMendenhall Glacier + whale watching combo$230Booked 8 weeks ahead — sold out if later
8Jun 9SkagwayWhite Pass & Yukon Railroad$200Most popular excursion; book 10+ weeks ahead
9Jun 10Sea dayAt sea — onboard activities$0Cooking class worth the $35 upcharge
10Jun 11Ketchikan (return)Ship-guided kayaking$110Easier than expected, beginner-friendly
11Jun 12Disembark VancouverCanada Place to hotel, afternoon city walk$190Late flight; stored luggage at hotel
12–14Jun 13–15ReturnTransit / fly YVR → home$920—

Tab 3 — Cruise Shore Excursions

KetchikanCreek Street self-guidedSelf$07/10Good but short
KetchikanSaxman Village totem tourPrincess$658/10Informative guide
JuneauMendenhall Glacier hikeLocal operator$12010/10Book direct, cheaper than ship
JuneauWhale watching add-onLocal operator$11010/10Combined with glacier, same operator
SkagwayWhite Pass & Yukon RailroadPrincess$2009/10Book early, fills fast
SkagwayKlondike gold panningSelf$257/10Fun, low effort
Sea dayShip cooking classShip$359/10Optional upcharge, highly recommended

References

  • Claude.ai
  • Google Sheets — Export as CSV

Step 1: Export the Past Trip Data

  1. Open your Google Sheet with the past trip data

  2. For each tab, copy the cells directly (do not export as file — paste works better):

    • Select all rows including the header row
    • Copy with Cmd+C
    • You will paste each tab as a labeled block in Step 2

  3. If you prefer file export, use File → Download → Comma-separated values (.csv) for each tab and name each file clearly

💡 Paste beats upload for spreadsheet data: Pasting copied cells directly into Claude gives it a cleaner signal than uploading a CSV file. Spreadsheet exports often include invisible characters that confuse column alignment. Copy from the sheet and paste inside the chat.

Step 2: Load the Data into Claude

  1. Open Claude Desktop and start a new conversation inside your Travel Planning Project

  2. Send one message with all four tabs pasted in, labeled by section:

    I am planning a new trip and want to use an existing past trip as a
baseline for research and comparison. Here is the complete data from
that trip, exported from Google Sheets.

Please read all four sections carefully before I ask you any questions.
Confirm once you have processed all four tabs.

--- TAB: TRIP OVERVIEW ---
[paste Tab 1 content here]

--- TAB: DAILY ITINERARY ---
[paste Tab 2 content here]

--- TAB: CRUISE SHORE EXCURSIONS ---
[paste Tab 3 content here]

--- TAB: BUDGET SUMMARY ---
[paste Tab 4 content here]

  3. Wait for Claude to confirm it has read all four sections before continuing

Step 3: Run the Trip Analysis

Once Claude confirms it has the data, ask for a structured analysis:

  1. Send this prompt:

    Based on the past trip data, produce a Trip Analysis covering:

1. Logistics summary
   - Total duration and rough arc of the trip (pre-cruise, cruise, post-cruise)
   - Cruise ship, route, embarkation port, duration
   - Number of hotel nights and city (pre and post)

2. Cost breakdown
   - Total actual cost and cost per person per day
   - Categories that ran over budget and by how much
   - Top 3 largest line items
   - Hidden or underestimated costs (flag these explicitly)

3. What worked well
   - Activities rated 9/10 or above with a one-sentence reason
   - Booking decisions that saved time or money
   - Logistics that made the trip smoother

4. What to do differently
   - Anything rated below 8/10 with a note on why
   - Budget categories significantly underestimated
   - Timing or booking mistakes worth avoiding

5. Must-book-early items
   - Excursions or activities that required advance booking
   - Anything weather-dependent with a backup recommendation
   - Anything that was close to selling out or required early action

  2. Save Claude’s output — you will reference the Trip Analysis in Part 2 when profiling the new traveler

Step 4: Ask About Adaptability

Before closing this conversation, probe how the itinerary would change for a different traveler:

  1. Ask about booking timing:

    Based on the past trip notes, build a booking timeline.
List each trip component (cruise, flights, hotels, excursions) with:
- How far in advance to book
- Why (sold out quickly, price increases closer to date, etc.)
- Any component that was booked too late or cut it close

  2. Ask about seasonal flexibility:

    The past trip ran in June. What aspects of this itinerary are
season-specific — where a different month would significantly change
the experience, cost, or availability? Include:
- Wildlife viewing (whales, bears) by month
- Weather risk at each Alaskan port
- Cruise pricing patterns (peak vs. shoulder season)
- Glacier visibility at Tracy Arm Fjord

  3. Ask about traveler profile flexibility:

    How adaptable is this itinerary for travelers with different needs?
Flag any activities or logistics that would change significantly for:
- A traveler with limited mobility (knee replacement, prefers paved surfaces)
- A solo traveler on a tighter budget
- A family with children under 12
- A traveler who has never been on a cruise before

For each profile, name the 2–3 items that need the most adjustment.

💡 Keep this conversation open: Do not close it before Part 2. Claude retains the full trip data and analysis within the same session. If you need to continue later, start a new conversation with: “I analyzed a 14-day Vancouver–Alaska Princess cruise trip in a previous session. Here is the Trip Analysis we produced: [paste the analysis output].”

Troubleshooting

Claude says the pasted data is too long

  • Split the paste into two messages: Trip Overview and Daily Itinerary first, then Shore Excursions and Budget Summary second
  • Tell Claude: “I will send the data in two parts. Confirm when you are ready for Part 2.”

Claude misreads the column structure or confuses values

  • Check that your paste includes the header row — Claude infers column meaning from headers
  • If the table structure broke during paste, re-copy from Google Sheets using Edit → Copy (not Export) and paste into a plain text editor first to verify it looks correct

The trip notes are sparse and Claude cannot extract much

  • Add context manually before the analysis prompt: “The notes column is minimal. Here is additional context for specific days: [add your recollections about what worked or did not]”
  • Claude works well with informal memory notes alongside structured data

👈 Prev: Claude Desktop: AI Travel Planning Partner

👉 Next: Part 2: Profiling the New Traveler & Researching Current Conditions

Part 2: Profiling the New Traveler & Researching Current Conditions

A trip plan built for one traveler rarely fits another. The past trip baseline captures what the original travelers wanted — their pace, budget, physical capacity, and priorities. The new traveler is different. This part covers how to build a detailed traveler profile, how to ask Claude to research what has changed since the baseline trip, and how to identify the adaptations required before building the new itinerary.

In this guide, you will:

  • Build a structured traveler profile covering every dimension Claude needs to adapt the itinerary
  • Ask Claude to flag compatibility gaps between the new traveler and the baseline trip
  • Research current Princess Cruises Alaska schedules and pricing for the new travel window
  • Compare June (baseline) vs. August (new trip) conditions port by port
  • Research accommodation and flight options for the new dates
  • Produce a Past vs. New Trip Conditions Summary table ready for Part 3

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • The Trip Analysis from Part 1 (either still open in the same conversation, or saved to paste back in)
  • Claude Pro subscription with web search enabled for real-time pricing research
  • The new traveler’s known details: travel dates, group composition, budget range, physical considerations, and priorities

References

  • Claude.ai
  • Princess Cruises Alaska itineraries
  • Travel Alaska — trip planning

Step 1: Build the Traveler Profile

  1. Continue in the same conversation from Part 1, or start a new conversation in your Travel Planning Project and paste the Trip Analysis at the top

  2. Introduce the new traveler with a full structured profile. The example below uses a realistic scenario — a 68-year-old solo traveler flying from Toronto — to show the level of detail Claude needs:

    I want to plan a new Alaska cruise trip for a different traveler.
Here is their profile:

TRAVELER:
- Identifier: Margaret (my mother)
- Age: 68, traveling solo
- Home city / departure airport: Toronto, Canada (YYZ)
- Target travel dates: August 14–28, 2025 (flexible ±2 weeks)
- Budget: CAD $9,000–$11,000 all-in (solo, single-occupancy)

PHYSICAL CONSIDERATIONS:
- Mobility: Limited — knee replacement two years ago; comfortable up to 2km
  on paved or well-maintained surfaces; avoids stairs and steep terrain
- Fitness: Moderate — prefers low-exertion options; can handle light walking
  tours but not hikes with elevation gain
- Seasickness: Mild concern; first time on a large ocean cruise

TRAVEL PREFERENCES:
- Style: Comfort-first, relaxed pace, prefers small groups over large tours
- Priorities: Wildlife viewing, scenic photography, cultural history
- Dislikes: Crowded buffets, early-morning starts before 8am, overnight
  bus or long coach transfers
- Cruise experience: Never cruised before

LOGISTICS:
- Prefers non-stop or single-connection flights
- Needs accessibility-friendly accommodation (elevator access, no stairs)
- Travel insurance: Needs to purchase — has pre-existing knee condition
- Must return to Toronto by August 29

  3. After sending the profile, ask for a compatibility check against the baseline trip:

    Based on the past trip analysis and Margaret's traveler profile, what are
the main compatibility gaps? For each gap, tell me:
- What the issue is
- Which part of the baseline itinerary is affected
- What the adaptation would be

Focus on the most significant adjustments — mobility, solo pricing,
first-time cruiser considerations, and the August vs. June timing difference.

Step 2: Research Current Cruise Options

The baseline trip used Discovery Princess departing Vancouver in June 2023. Margaret wants August 2025. Cruise schedules, ships, and pricing change year to year.

  1. Ask Claude to search for current Alaska cruise options:

    Search for Princess Cruises Alaska itineraries for August 2025,
departing from Vancouver or Seattle.

I need:
1. Which Princess ships are operating the Inside Passage route in August 2025
2. Available 7-day sailing dates in the August 14–28 window
3. Price range for a balcony cabin (solo, single-occupancy) in CAD or USD
4. Whether the same ports are included: Ketchikan, Juneau, Skagway,
   Tracy Arm Fjord
5. Any itinerary changes, new ships, or discontinued routes compared
   to the 2023 Discovery Princess sailing

If web search is unavailable, tell me what search terms to use and I
will paste the results back.

    ⚠️ Verify pricing directly: Cruise prices change daily and solo supplements vary. Claude’s research gives you a baseline range — confirm with Princess Cruises or a travel agent before booking. Use Claude’s output to know what to look for and what questions to ask, not as a purchase quote.

  2. If the balcony cost for a solo traveler is significantly over budget, ask for alternatives:

    Solo single-occupancy balcony pricing often includes a 100% solo supplement.
What are Margaret's options for reducing this cost? Consider:
- Interior cabin (explain the accessibility trade-off given her mobility)
- Solo-friendly cabin categories Princess offers
- Sailing dates where solo supplements are reduced or waived
- Whether a 10-day or 14-day cruise offers better per-day value

For each option, estimate the cost impact and rate the experience trade-off
as low, medium, or high.

  3. Ask for a comparison table between the baseline and the best current option:

    Compare the 2023 Discovery Princess baseline with the best current
Princess option for Margaret. Format as a table:

| Dimension | Past Trip (Jun 2023) | Margaret's Trip (Aug 2025) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Ship | | |
| Departure port and date | | |
| Ports of call | | |
| Duration | | |
| Cabin type | | |
| Estimated price per person (CAD) | | |
| Key differences | | |

Step 3: Research Weather and Seasonal Conditions

Alaska in August differs meaningfully from Alaska in June — the month of the original trip.

  1. Ask Claude to compare seasonal conditions port by port:

    Compare June vs. August conditions in the Alaska Inside Passage ports:
Ketchikan, Juneau, Skagway, and Tracy Arm Fjord.

For each port and month, provide:
- Average temperature range (°C and °F)
- Typical rain days per month
- Daylight hours (approximate)
- Wildlife viewing highlights: humpback whales, bears, eagles
- Glacier visibility at Tracy Arm Fjord
- Crowd levels relative to June

Then flag August-specific considerations for a first-time Alaska cruiser,
particularly anything that would change excursion selection.

  2. Ask about weather risk for the specific excursions from the baseline trip:

    The baseline trip included three top-rated excursions:
- Mendenhall Glacier + whale watching combo (Juneau)
- White Pass & Yukon Railroad (Skagway)
- Tracy Arm Fjord glacier cruising (sea day, ship-guided)

For each excursion in August:
- What is the weather-related cancellation risk?
- Is an August booking harder to secure than June? Why?
- Is the excursion suitable for Margaret's mobility profile
  (limited walking, no steep terrain)?
- If not suitable or unavailable, what is the best accessible alternative?

Step 4: Research Accommodation and Flights

  1. Ask Claude about pre-cruise accommodation near the Vancouver cruise terminal:

    The baseline trip used the Fairmont Pacific Rim in Vancouver for 3 nights
at roughly CAD $520/night in June 2023.

For Margaret arriving August 13, 2025 (one night pre-cruise):
1. Approximate price range for accessible hotels within walking distance
   of Canada Place in August
2. Two or three accessible options — elevator access, no stairs to lobby,
   close to transit — that are better value than the Fairmont
3. Is one pre-cruise night enough for a solo traveler flying in from Toronto,
   or is two nights recommended given the flight risk of a delayed departure?

  2. Ask about flights and arrival timing:

    Margaret is flying from Toronto (YYZ) to Vancouver (YVR) for a cruise
departing in August 2025.

Please advise:
1. Typical YYZ–YVR non-stop flight duration and main airlines
2. How far in advance to book for August travel to get reasonable pricing
3. Recommended arrival day relative to embarkation — day before or two days
   before — and the reasoning for each given flight delay risk
4. Return flight logistics: typical Princess disembarkation time at Canada
   Place, and whether a same-day afternoon flight home to Toronto is realistic
5. Travel insurance considerations for a traveler with a pre-existing
   knee condition

Step 5: Build the Conditions Comparison Summary

Before moving to Part 3, ask Claude to synthesize all research into a single comparison table:

  1. Send this synthesis prompt:

    Based on everything we have researched, produce a Past vs. New Trip
Conditions Summary. Format as a table with one row per dimension:

| Dimension | Past Trip (Jun 2023) | Margaret Aug 2025 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Travelers | | |
| Cruise ship | | |
| Departure port and date | | |
| Cruise cabin type | | |
| Cruise price per person (CAD) | | |
| Pre-cruise nights and hotel | | |
| Hotel cost per night (CAD) | | |
| Flights route and est. cost | | |
| Top excursions | | |
| Weather and season | | |
| Total estimated budget (CAD) | | |
| Key differences from baseline | | |

  2. Review the table and correct any cell where Claude’s research conflicts with information you have verified directly

  3. Ask Claude to flag the top planning risks:

    Based on this comparison, what are the three biggest planning risks for
Margaret's trip that were not present in the baseline trip?
For each risk: describe it, rate it low / medium / high, and give the
specific mitigation action.

💡 Save the Conditions Summary: Copy this table into a notes document or a new Google Sheet tab. In Part 3, you will reference it to generate the full new itinerary and budget. Having it in text form makes it easy to paste back into Claude if you start a fresh conversation.

Troubleshooting

Claude says its knowledge may be outdated and cannot confirm current prices

  • This is expected — Claude’s training has a knowledge cutoff. Ask: “What exact search terms or pages on the Princess Cruises website should I use to find this? I will paste the results.”
  • Paste the relevant data from Princess Cruises, Google Flights, or hotel sites directly into the chat
  • Claude will incorporate your pasted research into the analysis even without web access

The traveler profile reveals conflicting preferences

  • Treat conflicts as a planning question, not an error: “Margaret wants to do whale watching, the White Pass train, and Mendenhall Glacier but also wants a relaxed pace. Is fitting all three into three port days realistic for a solo traveler with limited mobility?”
  • Claude will surface when a combination is logistically tight and suggest which to prioritize

Seasonal research reveals the preferred month is a poor fit

  • Ask Claude to show the trade-off explicitly: “August is Margaret’s preferred month but the research shows heavier crowds and higher prices. What would the same trip look like in early September? What changes and what is lost?”
  • Let the comparison inform the traveler’s decision before committing to dates

👈 Prev: Part 1: Reading the Past Travel Plan

👉 Next: Part 3: Building the New Trip Plan & Creating the Google Sheet

Part 3: Building the New Trip Plan & Creating the Google Sheet

With a baseline trip analyzed, a detailed traveler profile built, and current conditions researched, Claude now has everything it needs to generate a complete customized itinerary. This part covers how to ask Claude to synthesize all of that into a tailored trip plan, how to format it for direct paste into Google Sheets, and how to iterate until the plan fits the traveler precisely.

In this guide, you will:

  • Ask Claude to synthesize the baseline analysis, traveler profile, and research into a full itinerary
  • Iterate on specific sections — excursions, budget, pace — without rebuilding from scratch
  • Generate four Google Sheet tabs in CSV format ready for copy-paste
  • Populate a new Google Sheet and run a final review with Claude

Pre-requisites

Preparation

  • The Trip Analysis from Part 1 (in the active conversation or saved to paste back in)
  • The Conditions Comparison Summary from Part 2
  • The traveler profile from Part 2
  • A blank Google Sheet ready to receive the output — name it something like Alaska 2025 — Margaret

References

  • Claude.ai
  • Google Sheets

Step 1: Generate the New Itinerary

  1. Continue in the same conversation from Parts 1 and 2, or open a new conversation in your Travel Planning Project and paste the Trip Analysis and Conditions Comparison Summary at the top

  2. Send the synthesis prompt:

    Using everything we have established — the past trip analysis, Margaret's
traveler profile, and the research on current conditions — please build a
complete 14-day itinerary for her new trip.

REQUIREMENTS:
- Start and end in Vancouver (Canada Place)
- Include a 7-day Princess Cruise on the Inside Passage route
- Adapt all excursions for Margaret's mobility (max 2km paved walking,
  no steep terrain, no stairs-heavy activities)
- Stay within CAD $9,000–$11,000 total (solo, single-occupancy)
- Travel window: August 14–28, 2025

FORMAT — produce four tables for Google Sheets tabs:

TABLE 1: Daily Itinerary
Day | Date | Location | Activity / Plan | Estimated Cost CAD | Notes

TABLE 2: Cruise Shore Excursions
Port | Excursion | Accessibility | Book Through | Est. Cost CAD | Book By | Notes

TABLE 3: Accommodation
Dates | Property | Location | Nightly Rate CAD | Total CAD | Notes

TABLE 4: Budget Estimate
Category | Estimated CAD | Notes

After the tables, add a Key Adaptations section listing the 5 most
significant changes made relative to the 2023 baseline trip, and
the reason each was made for Margaret specifically.

  3. Review each table before iterating — flag anything that conflicts with what you know about the traveler’s constraints or the researched prices

Step 2: Iterate on Specific Sections

Rarely does the first plan need no changes. Adjust by section rather than regenerating the entire itinerary:

  1. Adjust an excursion that does not fit the mobility profile:

    The Mendenhall Glacier hike involves 1.5km of uneven glacier moraine
terrain — not suitable for Margaret's knee. She still wants a Juneau
glacier experience.

Suggest two accessible Juneau alternatives that:
- Offer comparable glacier or wildlife viewing
- Require less than 1km of walking on paved or stable surfaces
- Are bookable through Princess or a local operator
- Fall within a similar cost range (CAD $100–$200)

For each: name, brief description, why it fits Margaret, and how
to book it.

  2. Bring the budget into range if the estimate is over target:

    The current estimate puts the trip at CAD $12,100 — above Margaret's
$11,000 ceiling. Find CAD $1,100 in savings without significantly
reducing the experience.

For each saving option: the amount saved, the experience trade-off,
and your recommendation on whether it is worth it for this traveler.
Consider:
- Interior vs. balcony cabin (weigh the accessibility argument for balcony)
- One fewer pre-cruise night
- Excursion substitutions
- Meal or onboard spending adjustments

  3. Adjust the pace for a first-time cruiser:

    Margaret has never cruised before and may find a fully scheduled
port day tiring. Identify the one port day where I should leave the
afternoon unscheduled — no bookings, no transfers — so she can
rest or explore independently at her own pace.

Recommend which port and explain why that day is the best to keep open.

  4. Add weather contingency plans:

    What are the most weather-dependent activities in this itinerary?
For each one, provide:
- The weather risk in August at that port
- A backup activity that is accessible for Margaret and does not
  require advance booking
- Whether the primary excursion operator offers a rain-day refund
  or reschedule

Step 3: Generate the Final Google Sheets Output

Once the itinerary is finalized through iteration, ask Claude to produce a clean, paste-ready output:

  1. Request the full CSV output:

    Produce the final itinerary as four separate CSV blocks, one per
Google Sheet tab. Use commas as delimiters. Wrap any field that
contains a comma in double quotes.

Label each block clearly before the CSV rows:

=== TAB: Daily Itinerary ===
[CSV rows]

=== TAB: Cruise Shore Excursions ===
[CSV rows]

=== TAB: Accommodation ===
[CSV rows]

=== TAB: Budget Estimate ===
[CSV rows]

  2. Add a fifth tab summarizing the trip for the traveler:

    Add a fifth block for a Trip Summary tab. This tab is for Margaret to
read directly — written from her perspective, not the planner's.

Include:
- The 5 things she most needs to know before departure
- A day-by-day summary in 2–4 words per day (e.g., "Arrive Vancouver",
  "Stanley Park morning", "Board Discovery Princess")
- The 3 excursions that must be confirmed as booked before she leaves home
- What to pack that is specific to this itinerary (not a generic list)
- Emergency contacts and booking reference summary (leave placeholders
  for actual confirmation numbers)

=== TAB: Trip Summary ===
[CSV rows]

Step 4: Populate the Google Sheet

  1. Open Google Sheets at sheets.google.com and create a new blank spreadsheet

  2. Name it Alaska 2025 — Margaret (or your traveler’s name)

  3. For each CSV block from Claude:

    1. Click the + tab button at the bottom to add a new sheet tab

    2. Name the tab to match the block label — Daily Itinerary, Cruise Shore Excursions, Accommodation, Budget Estimate, Trip Summary

    3. Click cell A1 in the new tab

    4. Copy the CSV rows from Claude (include the header row)

    5. Paste using Edit → Paste special → Paste as values only (or Cmd+Shift+V on Mac)

    6. Select all populated columns and choose Format → Column → Fit to data to auto-resize

  4. On the Budget Estimate tab, add a totals row:

    • Click the first empty cell below the last budget category in column B
    • Enter =SUM(B2:B[last row number]) to get a live running total
    • As you adjust any cost estimate, this cell updates automatically

  5. On the Daily Itinerary tab, highlight high-cost days:

    • Select the Estimated Cost CAD column
    • Go to Format → Conditional formatting
    • Add a rule: cell value greater than 300 → fill color yellow
    • This flags days worth reviewing if the budget needs trimming

💡 Google Sheets import as an alternative: For long outputs with special characters, save Claude’s CSV as a .csv file using TextEdit or Notepad, then use File → Import in Google Sheets. This handles edge cases more reliably than paste.

Step 5: Final Review with Claude

Before closing the planning session, run one last check:

  1. Ask Claude to review the completed plan for gaps:

    Here is the final trip summary for Margaret:
[paste the Trip Summary tab content]

Please review and identify:
1. Any logistical gaps — transitions that are too tight, bookings that
   depend on each other without a buffer
2. Anything inconsistent with Margaret's profile: mobility, solo travel,
   first-time cruiser, or budget
3. Booking checklist items that must happen immediately vs. closer to
   departure
4. One thing we have probably not thought of for a solo senior traveler
   on a first Alaska cruise

  2. Ask for a one-page printable trip brief for the traveler:

    Generate a one-page trip brief Margaret can print and carry.
Write it for her to read directly — not a planning document.

Include:
- Trip at a glance: dates, ship name, departure port
- Day-by-day summary (2–4 words per day)
- The 3 most important bookings to have confirmed before departure
- 5 things specific to Alaska in August she should know before she goes
- What to pack that most guides miss for this itinerary
- One sentence for each port: what is worth seeing if nothing else

Format for print: clear headers, no tables, readable at 12pt.

Troubleshooting

CSV output has formatting errors when pasted into Google Sheets

  • Use Paste special → Values only (Cmd+Shift+V) to prevent Google Sheets from interpreting the content as HTML
  • If a cell bleeds into the next column, the source field has an unquoted comma — ask Claude: “Field [X] has a formatting issue in the CSV. Re-generate that tab with all comma-containing fields wrapped in double quotes.”

The budget estimate is significantly higher or lower than expected

  • Ask Claude to justify each line: “Walk me through the Accommodation estimate — which property, how many nights, and what rate per night are you assuming?”
  • Paste in actual rates you have verified and ask: “Update the Budget Estimate tab using these confirmed prices: [list]. Recalculate the total.”

The new itinerary looks too similar to the baseline

  • Ask for explicit differentiation: “This plan is quite similar to the 2023 baseline. Propose 5 meaningful changes that make this feel like a genuinely different trip for Margaret — not just swaps for mobility, but things that would make it memorable for a first-time solo cruiser.”

Claude cannot find the specific sailing dates or pricing

  • This is likely a knowledge cutoff issue. Ask: “Give me the exact URL and search path on Princess Cruises’ website to find Alaska August 2025 sailings and solo pricing. I will look it up and paste the results.”
  • Once you paste the current data, Claude can incorporate it immediately into the plan
